### [1] [Quantum Thermodynamics of Electron Transport along Chains of Redox Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09728)
*Fabrizio Cleri,Ralf Blossey,Stefano Giordano*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends the Lindblad equation quantum model to electron transport in biological redox chains (iron-sulfur clusters/heme groups), analyzing heat/entropy transfer, currents, transfer times, and efficiency across a range of physical parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether non-trivial quantum mechanical effects play a functional role in electron transport within biological redox chains, moving beyond the classical semi-classical hopping description.

Method: Extension of the Lindblad equation (open quantum system dynamics) to model long chains of redox centers (iron-sulfur clusters or heme groups) in multi-domain proteins, exploring a wide range of physical parameters.

Result: Key features of electron transport in these biological systems are characterized, including electron currents, average transfer times, transport efficiency, and heat/entropy transfer between electrons and the protein bath.

Conclusion: The Lindblad quantum model provides a physically realistic framework for electron transport in biological redox chains, with explicit characterization of thermodynamic quantities and transport metrics.

Abstract: Intramolecular electron transport in biological systems is typically described as a diffusive hopping process, according to the semi-classical rate theories of Marcus and Hopfield combined with classical Pauli-type master equations. However, the possibility that non-trivial quantum mechanical effects could play a functional role in the transport dynamics in certain biomolecular processes has attracted increasing attention. Here, we extend the quantum mechanical model of open system dynamics by the Lindblad equation to a key biological component, the long chains of redox centers based on iron-sulfur clusters or heme groups that are widespread in many biological organisms, where they realize the cellular respiration. This approach allows to explore a wide range of physical parameters, showing key features of electron transport in these multi-domain protein structures. We pay particular attention to heat and entropy transfer between the electrons and the protein bath, which constitutes a benchmark of physical realism for the models. Electron currents, average transfer times and relative efficiency of the transport process are also explicitly characterized.

</details>


### [2] [Q-Score: A Quantum-Native Scoring Function for Molecular Docking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09737)
*Kangyu Zheng,Yidong Zhou,Ruihao Li,Zixin Ding,Zhiding Liang,Shaohua Li*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Q-Score introduces a quantum-classical hybrid method (DC-QAOA) that encodes GNN-predicted orbital donor-acceptor interactions into a weighted graph and solves a maximum-weight clique problem to score protein-ligand binding, achieving strong performance on NISQ hardware up to 6 qubits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical scoring functions rely on empirical pairwise contacts and ignore quantum-mechanical effects like orbital charge transfer that are critical for binding specificity, creating a bottleneck in drug discovery.

Method: Q-Score encodes GNN-predicted orbital donor-acceptor energies into a weighted graph, where each interaction anchor maps to a qubit and compatibility constraints become edges. Binding scoring is formulated as a maximum-weight vertex clique problem solved via Digitized-Counterdiabatic QAOA (DC-QAOA).

Result: Across 11 protein targets, DC-QAOA recovers exact optimum on 8 at 10 qubits. On 1000 AI-generated molecules, Q-Score is orthogonal to classical scoring (Spearman ρ=0.05), driven by orbital quality (ρ=0.90), and free of molecular-weight bias, enriching strong orbital interactions at 2x random rate. DC-QAOA achieves mean approximation ratio 0.94 with 52% exact solutions. IBM Eagle execution confirms 6-qubit solvability on NISQ hardware.

Conclusion: Q-Score demonstrates that quantum-computed orbital interaction scoring provides orthogonal information to classical scoring, captures quantum mechanical binding effects, and is feasible on current NISQ hardware, offering a new paradigm for drug discovery scoring functions.

Abstract: Molecular docking predicts how a small molecule binds to a protein and is a key bottleneck in drug discovery. Classical scoring functions sum empirical pairwise contacts, blind to quantum-mechanical effects like orbital charge transfer that govern binding specificity. We introduce Q-Score, encoding GNN-predicted orbital donor-acceptor energies into a weighted graph and scoring binding by solving a maximum-weight vertex clique problem via Digitized-Counterdiabatic QAOA. Each interaction anchor maps to one qubit and compatibility constraints become edges. Across 11 protein targets, DC-QAOA recovers the exact optimum on 8 at 10 qubits. On 1000 AI-generated molecules, Q-Score is orthogonal to classical scoring with Spearman rho of 0.05, driven by orbital quality with rho of 0.90, and free of molecular-weight bias, enriching for strong orbital interactions at twice the random rate. DC-QAOA achieves a mean approximation ratio of 0.94 with 52 percent exact. Execution of 1000 circuits on IBM Eagle confirms 6-qubit solvability on NISQ hardware.

</details>


### [3] [The application of theory of probability to the modelling of chemical kinetics systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09758)
*Maxim Nazarov*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A chemical kinetics model based on joint and geometric probability (not mass action) is generalized for reaction-diffusion in heterogeneous media with convective/diffusive heat transfer, using an ODE-based methodology (similar to finite volume but with statistical/geometric probability) to simplify numerical implementation and parallel efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To derive chemical kinetics equations without relying on the law of mass action, instead using joint probability and geometric probability principles, and to construct a generalization for reaction-diffusion systems in heterogeneous media with convective and diffusive heat transfer.

Method: An alternative methodology based fully on systems of ordinary differential equations (no partial derivatives), using statistical simplifying positions and geometric probability to describe diffusion processes — akin to the finite volume method but with probabilistic foundations.

Result: Simplified numerical implementation, simplified quantitative analysis via dynamical systems theory, and increased efficiency of parallel implementation.

Conclusion: The model is applicable to example reactions with quasi-periodic regimes, and an algorithm for transitioning from standard models with dimensional kinetic constants to the proposed formalism is provided.

Abstract: We consider a model of chemical kinetics for which the derivation of equations does not rely on the law of mass action, but is rather based on such principles as the joint probability and the geometric probability. For this model a generalization is constructed for the case of reaction-diffusion systems in heterogeneous medium with respect to the convective and diffusive transfer of heat. The construction of this generalization is carried out by an alternative methodology which is based fully on a systems of ordinary differential equations, without a transition to the partial derivatives. The description of this new method is a bit similar to the finite volume method, except that it uses statistical simplifying positions and geometric probability to describe the diffusion processes. Such approach allows us to greatly simplify the numerical implementation of the resulting model, as well as to simplify the quantitative analysis of it with dynamical systems theory. Moreover, the efficiency of the parallel implementation of the numerical method is increased for the resulting model. In addition, we will consider an application of this model for the description of some example reaction with quasi-periodic regime, as well as consider an algorithm for the transition from standard models with dimensional kinetic constants to its formalism.

</details>


### [4] [Challenges in the simulation of enzymatic transition states with emerging multireference character](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09769)
*Valentin Kasper,Sanjoy Ray,Matthias Kaiser*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A proprietary PexMachina solver accurately reproduces exact ground-state energy across transition state scans where single-reference quantum chemistry methods fail to achieve chemical accuracy due to multireference character breakdown.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transition-state inhibitor design requires sub-Å quantum chemistry accuracy, but classical single-reference methods deviate substantially from exact energy at strongly correlated transition states due to multireference character.

Method: Study multireference breakdown using a model system with a multireference transition state; apply a proprietary PexMachina solver and compare against single-reference methods across the transition state scan.

Result: PexMachina solver reproduces exact ground-state energy throughout the transition state scan, while single-reference methods fail to achieve chemical accuracy for certain geometries.

Conclusion: The PexMachina solver overcomes multireference challenges in transition state simulations, enabling accurate quantum chemistry for transition-state inhibitor design.

Abstract: Highly successful drugs, such as transition-state inhibitors, were designed by mimicking the precise geometry of enzymatic transition states, a strategy that requires quantum chemistry accurate enough to specify molecular structure at sub-Å resolution. A central challenge during such a quantum chemistry simulation is that classical single-reference method can deviate substantially from the exact energy at strongly correlated transition states. We study the breakdown due to multireference character by studying a model system with a multireference transition state. In this work we present a proprietary PexMachina solver which reproduces the exact ground-state energy throughout the transition state scan, while single-reference methods struggle for certain geometries to achieve chemical accuracy.

</details>


### [5] [Inverse-IMPRESSION: A Graph-based Platform for Molecular Structure Elucidation from Experimental NMR Spectroscopic Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09978)
*Zheqi Jin,Grace Armitage,Richard Cox,Ben Honoré,Mohammad Golbabaee,Craig Butts*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: IMPRESSION-G2 is a platform built on an inverted Graph Transformer Network that reconstructs molecular bonding directly from NMR data, achieving 77.8% accuracy on simulated data (up to 30 heavy atoms) and 53% on experimental data (up to 480 Da).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an automated, machine learning-based approach for molecular structure elucidation from experimental NMR data that can handle complex synthetic and natural products, which currently challenge chemists.

Method: Three-stage platform: (1) one-shot model predicting bond connectivity; (2) structure-correction stage removing uncertain bonds with iterative reassignment; (3) noise-augmented multi-shot prediction generating candidate structures ranked for best fit. Uses inverted Graph Transformer Network (IMPRESSION-G2) with ¹H/¹³C NMR data including 2D experiments (COSY, HSQC, HMBC).

Result: 77.8% correct identification for molecules with ≤30 heavy atoms using simulated NMR data; 10 out of 19 (53%) using experimental NMR data; experimental structures up to 480 Da.

Conclusion: Inverse-IMPRESSION provides the first effective automated molecular structure elucidation approach using graph-based machine learning on experimental NMR data.

Abstract: Here, we present a platform built on our inverted Graph Transformer Network, IMPRESSION-G2, which can accurately and rapidly reconstruct molecular bonding directly from experimental nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) spectroscopic information. It comprises three interconnected stages: a one-shot model that predicts bond connectivity between atoms; a structure-correction stage that corrects the predicted structures by removing uncertain bonds and iteratively reassigning them; noise-augmented multi-shot prediction, generating an ensemble of candidate structures, which are ranked to identify the best-fit structure. By integrating a range of $^{1}$H and $^{13}$C NMR data, including two-dimensional (2D) experiments such as COSY, HSQC, and HMBC, the inverse-IMPRESSION platform correctly identifies the structures of 77.8% of molecules with up to 30 heavy atoms (H, C, N, O and F) using simulated NMR data, and 10 of 19 (53%) molecules using experimental NMR data. The experimental structures solved have molecular weights of up to 480 Da and are representative of the complex structures in synthetic and natural products that routinely challenge chemists. The inverse-IMPRESSION framework thus provides the first effective approach for automated molecular structure elucidation using graph-based machine learning on experimental data.

</details>


### [6] [Symmetry-adapted generalised normal-ordered coupled-cluster theory for excited states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10007)
*Nicholas Lee,David P. Tew,Bang C. Huynh*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a new theoretical framework combining QSym$^2$ symmetry treatment with GNOCC multi-reference coupled cluster theory to accurately describe degenerate ground and excited states in highly symmetric systems, demonstrating improved performance on (H₆)²⁺, H₆, and Li₄ model systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current quantum-chemical methods struggle to accurately describe strong correlation effects in highly symmetric systems with degenerate electronic states, preventing reliable computation of important chemical and spectroscopic properties.

Method: The framework unifies QSym$^2$ (non-Abelian symmetry symbolic treatment via symmetry orbits) with GNOCC (Generalised Normal Ordered Coupled Cluster, a state-specific multi-reference method) to dynamically correlate arbitrary spin eigenfunctions in a size-extensive and spin-free manner while restoring broken spatial symmetries.

Result: Applied to three model systems: octahedral (H₆)²⁺, octahedral H₆, and tetrahedral Li₄. The method targets both degenerate and non-degenerate states and delivers improved numerical performance relative to conventional single-reference coupled-cluster approaches.

Conclusion: The QSym$^2$+GNOCC framework enables balanced and targeted description of difficult ground and excited states in highly symmetric systems, overcoming limitations of single-reference methods for strongly correlated, degenerate states.

Abstract: Ground and excited electronic states in highly symmetric systems typically possess high degrees of spatial degeneracy as a consequence of point-group symmetry. However, many current quantum-chemical methods struggle to accurately describe the strong correlation effects inherently present in these states, thereby precluding the ability to obtain meaningful insights into the electronic structure of the underlying systems. Consequently, many of their important chemical and spectroscopic properties cannot be reliably computed and predicted. In this article, a new theoretical framework is described that unifies the symbolic treatment of non-Abelian symmetry in QSym$^2$ and the recently developed state-specific multi-reference coupled cluster theory termed Generalised Normal Ordered Coupled Cluster (GNOCC) to describe such difficult ground and excited states in a balanced and targeted manner. This is ensured by the ability of QSym$^2$ to exploit symmetry orbits to restore any broken spatial symmetries and generate symmetry-adapted multi-determinantal wavefunctions, as well as the ability of GNOCC to dynamically correlate arbitrary spin eigenfunctions in a size-extensive and spin-free manner. To illustrate the capabilities of this framework, several ground and excited states in three model systems are examined in detail: (i) octahedral $(\textrm{H}_6)^{2+}$, (ii) octahedral $\textrm{H}_6$, and (iii) tetrahedral $\textrm{Li}_4$. The results demonstrate that the proposed method can target both degenerate and non-degenerate states, while delivering improved numerical performance relative to conventional single-reference coupled-cluster approaches.

</details>


### [7] [Quantitative Analysis of Exciton Composition and Dynamics in Y6 Films for Single-Component Solar Cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10009)
*Saba Mahmoodpour,Jiyeon Oh,Yanlin Liu,Zijian Gan,Wei You,Andrew M. Moran*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops an analytical model based on hybrid Frenkel-charge-transfer states to analyze ultrafast transient absorption spectroscopy data in Y6 films, revealing 20-40% charge-transfer character in excitons and delocalization-mediated transport with ~1 ps quenching dynamics within ~4 nm of interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The microscopic mechanisms governing exciton transport and charge dissociation in non-fullerene acceptors like Y6 remain under active investigation, particularly the interplay between Frenkel-charge-transfer excitations and their coupling to environmental fluctuations.

Method: Ultrafast transient absorption spectroscopy is used to probe exciton quenching dynamics in Y6 films interfaced with hole-transport layers, interpreted via an analytical model based on hybrid Frenkel-charge-transfer states that extracts intermolecular electronic couplings, charge-transfer character, and system-bath interaction strengths.

Result: Substantial charge-transfer admixture of 20-40% in exciton states; transport regime characterized by delocalization-mediated exciton motion; quenching dynamics on ~1 ps timescale within ~4 nm of interface; short-range injection mechanism facilitated by exciton delocalization.

Conclusion: The study establishes a quantitative framework connecting spectroscopic observables to microscopic transport mechanisms for Y6, which can be generalized to other non-fullerene acceptors.

Abstract: Non-fullerene acceptors such as Y6 have enabled high-efficiency organic photovoltaic devices and motivated the development of single-component architectures; however, the microscopic mechanisms governing exciton transport and charge dissociation remain under active investigation. In particular, the interplay between Frenkel-charge-transfer excitations and their coupling to environmental fluctuations complicates the description of light absorption and subsequent exciton dynamics. Here, ultrafast transient absorption spectroscopy is used to probe exciton quenching dynamics in Y6 films interfaced with hole-transport layers. To interpret these measurements, we develop an analytical model based on hybrid Frenkel-charge-transfer states that enables direct extraction of intermolecular electronic couplings, charge-transfer character, and system-bath interaction strengths from experimental data. The analysis reveals a substantial charge-transfer admixture of 20-40% in the exciton states and identifies a transport regime characterized by delocalization-mediated exciton motion rather than purely diffusive hopping. Consistent with this interpretation, the corresponding quenching dynamics occur on a ~1 ps timescale within ~4 nm of the interface, suggesting a short-range injection mechanism facilitated by exciton delocalization. In addition to providing physical parameters for Y6, these results establish a quantitative framework that connects spectroscopic observables to microscopic transport mechanisms and can be generalized to other non-fullerene acceptors.

</details>


### [8] [Theoretical study of electronic structure and spectroscopic properties of the TlO molecule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10514)
*Alexander V. Oleynichenko,Yuriy A. Demidov*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents the first comprehensive study of the electronic structure and properties of TlO, TlO⁺, and TlO⁻ using relativistic Fock-space coupled cluster and DFT methods, revealing the TlO⁺ ground state is unbound, with key properties calculated for interpreting experimental data on thallium and nihonium compounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide systematic electronic structure data for TlO, its cation, and anion, which is highly relevant for interpreting experimental thermochromatography data on compounds of thallium and its superheavy homologue nihonium (element 113).

Method: Relativistic Fock-space coupled cluster method with full inclusion of connected triple excitations, combined with density functional theory (DFT).

Result: Detailed data on low-lying electronic states of TlO, TlO⁺, and TlO⁻ obtained for the first time; dissociation energies, adiabatic electron affinity, vertical ionization potential, dipole moment, and static polarizability tensor components calculated; ground electronic state of TlO⁺ found to be unbound.

Conclusion: The calculated characteristics of TlO are highly relevant for interpreting experimental thermochromatography data on compounds of thallium and its superheavy homologue nihonium (element 113).

Abstract: The electronic structure and properties of the thallium monoxide (TlO) molecule, as well as its cation and anion, have been systematically studied using both the relativistic Fock-space coupled cluster method with full inclusion of connected triple excitations and the density functional theory. For the first time, detailed data on the low-lying electronic states of TlO, its cation, and anion have been obtained. The dissociation energies of these systems, the adiabatic electron affinity and vertical ionization potential of TlO, as well as its dipole moment and components of the static polarizability tensor have been calculated. It is shown that the ground electronic state of TlO$^+$ cation is unbound. The obtained characteristics of TlO are highly relevant for interpreting experimental thermochromatography data on compounds of thallium and its superheavy homologue nihonium (element 113).

</details>


### [9] [A Three-Degree-of-Freedom Chesnavich Model for Roaming: Derivation, Phase-Space Geometry, NHIM-Anchored Dividing Surfaces, and Roaming Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10761)
*Stephen Wiggins*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A 3-DoF extension of the Chesnavich CH₄⁺ roaming model is constructed by breaking cylindrical symmetry with a C₃-symmetric azimuthal coupling. This activates out-of-plane dynamics, making the roaming periodic orbit transversely unstable and enabling 3D escape. Three NHIMs are constructed and shown to persist for small coupling. The coupling increases roaming fractions by ~0.040 and decreases the direct non-reactive fraction by ~0.032 at low energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Roaming reactions violate conventional transition state theory assumptions, and prior phase-space theory for roaming was limited to 2-DoF symmetric models. A 3-DoF extension is needed to capture the full out-of-plane dynamics and understand how symmetry breaking affects roaming mechanisms, periodic orbit stability, and reaction rates.

Method: Starting from the rigid-body formulation of Ezra and Wiggins, the cylindrical symmetry of the 2-DoF Chesnavich model is broken by adding an azimuthal coupling term with C₃ symmetry (matching the methyl fragment symmetry). This yields a Hamiltonian family H_b that reduces to the 2-DoF model at b=0 and is genuinely 3-DoF for b>0. Linear stability analysis of the roaming periodic orbit is performed, and normally hyperbolic invariant manifolds (NHIMs) are constructed at b=0 and their persistence proven for small b>0. Microcanonical trajectory simulations quantify roaming fractions.

Result: With physical inertia ratio I_z=2I_x, arbitrarily weak azimuthal coupling makes the roaming periodic orbit transversely unstable (except for a narrow elliptic window 0.58≲b≲0.63). A period-doubling bifurcation occurs at b_c≈0.63. Three 3D NHIMs (one per transition state) are constructed at b=0, with rigorous persistence proof for small b>0. At E=0.5 kcal·mol⁻¹, coupling lowers the direct non-reactive fraction by 0.032 and raises roaming fractions by 0.040; the effect diminishes with increasing energy.

Conclusion: The 3-DoF roaming model reveals that out-of-plane dynamics are activated immediately upon breaking cylindrical symmetry, destabilizing the roaming orbit and providing a mechanism for 3D escape. The NHIM framework successfully extends to 3-DoF roaming systems. The coupling enhances roaming probability at low energy, showing that symmetry breaking can qualitatively change roaming reaction dynamics.

Abstract: Roaming reactions, in which a dissociating fragment moves through a flat region of the potential surface rather than down the minimum-energy path, lie outside the assumptions of conventional transition state theory. The phase-space theory of roaming -- unstable periodic orbits and their invariant manifolds organizing transport -- has been developed for the Chesnavich model of $\mathrm{CH_4^+}\to\mathrm{CH_3^+}+\mathrm{H}$, which is cylindrically symmetric and reduces to two degrees of freedom (2-DoF). We construct and analyze a three-degree-of-freedom (3-DoF) extension. From the rigid-body formulation of Ezra and Wiggins, we break the symmetry with an azimuthal coupling respecting the three-fold ($C_3$) symmetry of the methyl fragment, obtaining a family $H_b$ whose planar reduction at $b=0$ is the 2-DoF model exactly and which is genuinely 3-DoF for $b>0$. This activates the out-of-plane degree of freedom at once: with the physical planar-top inertia ratio $I_z=2I_x$, arbitrarily weak coupling makes the periodic orbit on the roaming shelf transversely unstable, opening an escape route out of the reaction plane. Apart from a narrow elliptic window $0.58\lesssim b\lesssim0.63$, the instability persists across the range studied, changing type through a period-doubling at $b_c\approx0.63$. Because a periodic orbit cannot anchor a dividing surface in three degrees of freedom, we construct the objects that do -- three three-dimensional normally hyperbolic invariant manifolds, one per transition state -- at $b=0$, and prove that every compact interior piece of each persists for sufficiently small $b>0$. At $E=0.5\ \mathrm{kcal\,mol^{-1}}$ the coupling lowers the direct non-reactive fraction of a microcanonical ensemble of incoming trajectories by $0.032$ and raises the two roaming fractions by $0.040$; the effect decreases as the energy increases.

</details>


### [10] [Thawed Gaussian Ehrenfest dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10847)
*Jiří J. L. Vaníček*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces thawed Gaussian Ehrenfest dynamics (TGED), a unified variational framework that generalizes both Ehrenfest dynamics and thawed Gaussian wavepacket dynamics to capture electronic nonadiabaticity and nuclear quantum effects simultaneously, with explicit geometric integrators and analysis of limiting cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a single theoretical framework that can simultaneously capture both electronic nonadiabatic effects and nuclear quantum effects, which are typically treated separately by existing mixed quantum-classical (Ehrenfest) and semiclassical (thawed Gaussian) methods.

Method: The authors apply the time-dependent variational principle to a Hartree product of electronic and Gaussian nuclear wavepackets, deriving a fully variational formulation. By replacing the effective locally quadratic molecular potential with alternative forms, they obtain an infinite family of TGED methods. They also present explicit geometric integrators for the entire family.

Result: TGED reduces to conventional Ehrenfest dynamics in the classical nuclear limit and to thawed Gaussian wavepacket dynamics in the absence of electronic coupling. Conditions under which different approximations become exact are identified.

Conclusion: TGED unifies and generalizes Ehrenfest dynamics and thawed Gaussian wavepacket dynamics, providing a variational framework that bridges mixed quantum-classical and semiclassical approaches for nonadiabatic molecular dynamics with nuclear quantum effects.

Abstract: Ehrenfest dynamics is a widely used mixed quantum--classical approach for nonadiabatic molecular dynamics, whereas thawed Gaussian wavepacket dynamics provides an efficient semiclassical description of adiabatic nuclear quantum dynamics. Here we describe thawed Gaussian Ehrenfest dynamics (TGED), which unifies and generalizes these two methods to capture both electronic nonadiabaticity and nuclear quantum effects within a single framework. The fully variational formulation of TGED is derived by applying the time-dependent variational principle to a Hartree product of electronic and Gaussian nuclear wavepackets. Replacing the effective locally quadratic molecular potential obtained from this variational treatment by alternative effective locally quadratic potentials yields an infinite family of TGED methods, of which we present several members. We analyze the limiting cases of the general formalism and show, in particular, that it reduces to conventional Ehrenfest dynamics in the classical limit for the nuclei and to thawed Gaussian wavepacket dynamics in the absence of electronic coupling. Finally, we present explicit geometric integrators for the entire family of methods and identify the conditions under which the different approximations become exact.

</details>


### [11] [Autonomous Transition State Search with Soft Actor-Critic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10854)
*Utham Suresh,Konstantinos D. Vogiatzis*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a reinforcement learning-based transition state search model (soft actor-critic) that navigates potential energy surfaces using local energetic and curvature information, enabling automated saddle point identification without labeled data or prescribed pathways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional TS search algorithms are computationally intensive and depend heavily on initial guesses, user expertise, and chemical intuition, while existing ML approaches require large datasets or geometric interpolation, limiting generality.

Method: Formulates TS search as a sequential decision-making process in internal coordinates using the soft actor-critic reinforcement learning algorithm. The agent learns from local energetic and curvature information and is guided by a reward function that promotes movement toward saddle point regions, without requiring labeled trajectories or prescribed reaction pathways.

Result: The method successfully identifies TS geometries for standard benchmark reactions by operating directly on realistic molecular potential energy surfaces.

Conclusion: Reinforcement learning offers a general strategy for reducing dependence on initial guesses and enabling scalable, automated reaction discovery across diverse chemical systems.

Abstract: Transition state (TS) search is a crucial step in understanding chemical reactivity and mechanisms, yet conventional algorithms remain computationally intensive and heavily reliant on initial guesses, user s expertise, and chemical intuition. While recent machine learning approaches have shown promise, they demand either large training datasets or geometric interpolation between known endpoints, limiting their generality. In this work, we introduce a TS search model based on the soft actor-critic model, an advanced reinforcement learning algorithm in which an agent learns to navigate potential energy surfaces directly from local energetic and curvature information starting from a given reactant and its corresponding product. By formulating the search as a sequential decision-making process in internal coordinates, the agent adaptively proposes chemically meaningful structural updates through a reward function designed to promote movement towards saddle point regions. Without labelled trajectories or prescribed reaction pathways, the method successfully identifies TS geometries for standard benchmark reactions, operating directly on realistic molecular potential energy surfaces. These results highlight the potential of RL as a general strategy for reducing dependence on initial guesses and enabling scalable, automated reaction discovery across diverse chemical systems.

</details>


### [12] [Transferable Implicit Solvent Machine Learning Potential for Drugs and Proteins Approaching Ab Initio Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10887)
*Jan Eckwert,Julija Zavadlav*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: TWIN is an implicit solvent MLP trained solely on ab initio and experimental data that achieves DFT-level accuracy for biomolecular systems while being two orders of magnitude faster than explicit solvent MLPs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLPs are orders of magnitude slower than classical force fields, limiting their application to biomolecular systems requiring microsecond+ timescales. Implicit solvent MLPs could address this but previous approaches relied on empirical force field data, limiting accuracy.

Method: TWIN (Transferable Water Implicit Network): an implicit water MLP parametrized entirely by an Equivariant Graph Neural Network, trained solely on ab initio and experimental labels (no empirical force field data).

Result: TWIN achieves excellent results on ab initio and experimental crystallographic and NMR benchmarks, consistently outperforming previous ML-based implicit solvent or coarse-grained models. It closely matches DFT-based explicit solvent MLPs while providing two-order-of-magnitude faster timestep evaluation.

Conclusion: TWIN paves the way for efficient ab initio-level modeling of biomolecular systems in aqueous environments by combining DFT-level accuracy with significantly improved computational efficiency.

Abstract: Machine learning interatomic potentials (MLPs) have revolutionized atomistic modeling, offering the potential to replace traditional methods like Density Functional Theory (DFT). However, inference time of MLPs is orders of magnitude slower than that of classical force fields, hindering real-world applications for biomolecular systems that require timescales of microseconds and beyond. Implicit solvent MLPs can address this issue, but are faced with data challenges associated with coarse-grained modeling. Consequently, previous approaches relied on empirical force field data, thereby inherently limiting the MLP's accuracy. Here, we introduce the Transferable Water Implicit Network (TWIN), an implicit water MLP parametrized entirely by an Equivariant Graph Neural Network and trained solely on ab initio and experimental labels. We demonstrate TWIN's transferability across drug-like molecules, peptides, and proteins, achieving excellent results on ab initio and experimental crystallographic and NMR benchmarks, consistently outperforming previous machine-learning-based implicit solvent or coarse-grained models. Furthermore, TWIN closely matches DFT-based explicit solvent MLPs while providing a two-order-of-magnitude faster timestep evaluation, paving the way for efficient ab initio-level modeling of biomolecular systems in aqueous environments.

</details>


### [13] [Efficient, direct calculation of reaction rate coefficients based on a partially rearranged rovibrational Hamiltonian. A full-dimensional case study of the H$_2$ + D $\rightarrow$ HD + H reaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11418)
*Gábor A. Ecseri,Attila G. Császár,Csaba Fábri*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a fully quantum mechanical calculation of thermal reaction rate coefficients for the H₂ + D → HD + H reaction using a rearranged rovibrational Hamiltonian and an accurate potential energy surface, with emphasis on exact treatment of molecular rotation and nuclear spin symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that efficient, direct, fully quantum mechanical calculation of thermal reaction rate coefficients requires a partially rearranged form of the exact kinetic energy part of the rovibrational Hamiltonian expressed in internal coordinates.

Method: A new partially rearranged form of the numerically-constructed exact kinetic energy part of the rovibrational Hamiltonian in internal coordinates was developed. This was combined with an accurate full-dimensional potential energy surface by Mielke, Garrett, and Peterson to compute rate coefficients.

Result: Reaction rate coefficients were computed for the H₂ + D → HD + H reaction in the temperature range of 75–800 K.

Conclusion: Exact treatment of overall molecular rotation and nuclear spin symmetry is crucial for accurate fully quantum mechanical thermal rate coefficient calculations.

Abstract: It is shown that an efficient, direct, and fully quantum mechanical calculation of thermal reaction rate coefficients requires a new, partially rearranged form of the numerically-constructed exact kinetic energy part of the rovibrational Hamiltonian expressed in internal coordinates. Using this Hamiltonian and an accurate, full-dimensional potential energy surface characterizing the H$_2$ + H exchange reaction, developed by Mielke, Garrett, and Peterson (J. Chem. Phys. 2002, 116, 4142), reaction rate coefficients in the temperature range of $75-800$ K have been computed for the H$_2$ + D $\rightarrow$ HD + H reaction. The paper puts particular emphasis on the exact treatment of overall molecular rotation and on nuclear spin symmetry.

</details>


### [14] [Correlation-consistent Gaussian basis sets for copper solids from material-constrained atomic optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11711)
*Jincheng Yu,Xiaoyu Zhang,Min-Ye Zhang,Yu Cao,Qiming Sun,Hong-Zhou Ye*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Material-Constrained Atomic Optimization (MCAO) to adapt correlation-consistent Gaussian basis sets for periodic solids while preserving their atomic character. It generates MCAO-cc-pVXZ basis sets for Cu, which remain numerically stable for solids and surfaces, reproduce molecular and bulk reference properties, and enable CBS-extrapolated benchmarks for bulk Cu and CO adsorption on Cu(111).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Correlation-consistent Gaussian basis sets suffer from severe linear dependence in periodic solids due to diffuse primitives, especially for metallic systems. Reliable complete-basis-set (CBS) extrapolation is needed for correlated-wavefunction benchmarks in solids, but existing basis sets are not directly applicable.

Method: Material-Constrained Atomic Optimization (MCAO): a basis-set optimization framework that penalizes large overlap-matrix condition numbers in representative solids while preserving atomic and correlation-consistent character. Applied to generate Dunning-style MCAO-cc-pVZ basis sets for Cu across all-electron, scalar-relativistic all-electron, ECP, and pseudopotential treatments.

Result: MCAO basis sets remain numerically stable for Cu solids and surfaces, reproduce molecular Cu dimer energetics and plane-wave reference bulk Cu properties. CBS-extrapolated RPA calculations enable controlled assessment of pseudopotential, relativistic, and basis-set errors in bulk Cu and CO adsorption on Cu(111).

Conclusion: MCAO provides a general framework enabling correlation-consistent Gaussian basis sets for periodic solids, yielding scalar-relativistic all-electron Gaussian-basis benchmarks for the CO adsorption puzzle.

Abstract: Correlation-consistent Gaussian basis sets are central to systematic molecular quantum chemistry, but their direct use in periodic solids is often limited by severe linear dependence from diffuse atomically optimized primitives. This problem is particularly acute for metallic and metal-containing systems, where reliable complete-basis-set (CBS) extrapolation is needed for correlated-wavefunction benchmarks. We introduce material-constrained atomic optimization (MCAO), a basis-set optimization framework that preserves the atomic and correlation-consistent character of Gaussian basis sets while penalizing large overlap-matrix condition numbers in representative solids. As a proof of concept, we generate Dunning-style MCAO-cc-pVXZ basis sets (X = D, T, Q) for Cu with all-electron, scalar-relativistic all-electron, effective core potential (ECP), and pseudopotential treatments. The resulting basis sets remain numerically stable for Cu solids and surfaces while reproducing molecular Cu dimer energetics and plane-wave reference properties of bulk Cu. CBS-extrapolated random-phase approximation calculations further enable a controlled assessment of pseudopotential, relativistic, and basis-set errors in bulk Cu and CO adsorption on Cu(111), providing scalar-relativistic all-electron Gaussian-basis benchmarks for the CO adsorption puzzle.

</details>


### [15] [Robust nuclear hyperpolarization of small molecules through intermolecular transfer of parahydrogen-derived polarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11723)
*Bogdan A. Rodin,Anna Parker,Laurynas Dagys,Vitaly Kozinenko,Martin Korzeczek,Martin B. Plenio,Salvatore Mamone,Rokas Šakalys,Federico De Biasi,Ran Wei,Pinelopi Moutzouri,Lyndon Emsley,Stephan Knecht,James Eills,Ilai Schwartz*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: PHIPNOE is a hyperpolarization platform that combines parahydrogen-induced polarization (PHIP) with spin polarization-induced nuclear Overhauser effect (SPINOE) to create a scalable, instrumentally simple method for enhancing NMR signals across a broad range of target molecules, demonstrated with 300-fold amplification and applications in low-field NMR and PFAS detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperpolarization techniques significantly enhance NMR signals but suffer from either complex instrumentation or limited molecular applicability; there is a need for a platform that is both simple to implement and broadly applicable across diverse molecular classes.

Method: PHIP is used to hyperpolarize a concentrated "source molecule," which then transfers polarization to target molecules in solution via the spin polarization-induced nuclear Overhauser effect (SPINOE). A predictive model based on molecular mass and T1 relaxation times was developed to estimate enhancement.

Result: 300-fold signal amplification demonstrated on a benchtop spectrometer; complete polarization transfer and detection in under one minute; no spectrometer modifications required; repeatable enhancements across molecular classes; successful application to single-shot multidimensional NMR, real-time monitoring, and enhanced PFAS detection.

Conclusion: PHIPNOE lowers the barrier to hyperpolarized NMR by combining the simplicity of PHIP with the broad applicability of SPINOE, enabling practical low-field NMR and detection of environmentally relevant substances like PFAS.

Abstract: The recent advent of hyperpolarization techniques, which can enhance NMR signals by several orders of magnitude relative to thermally polarized samples, has enabled applications traditionally out of reach due to the inherently low sensitivity of NMR techniques. However, a high barrier to entry remains, as most hyperpolarization approaches either require complex instrumentation or are applicable only to a relatively small set of molecules. Here we introduce PHIPNOE, a platform that directly addresses both limitations. PHIPNOE is based on parahydrogen-induced polarization (PHIP), which is well-established as a scalable route to hyperpolarization requiring minimal instrumentation, but has been mostly restricted to molecules that undergo specific chemical reactions. We overcome this barrier by tailoring PHIP to create highly polarized, highly concentrated solutions of one specific molecule, which acts as an intermediate source of polarization. This 'source molecule' then distributes polarization to a broad range of target molecules mixed into the solution, via the spin polarization-induced nuclear Overhauser effect (SPINOE). We investigate chemical influences on PHIPNOE, and develop a predictive model to estimate enhancement based on molecular mass and T1 relaxation times. A complete run from PHIP hyperpolarization to PHIPNOE polarization transfer and signal detection takes less than one minute, the approach does not require any modifications to the NMR spectrometer, and enhancements are repeatable across molecular classes. PHIPNOE thus enables applications including single-shot multidimensional NMR, real-time monitoring of dynamic processes, and, with 300-fold signal amplification demonstrated on a benchtop spectrometer, practical low-field NMR, where we show enhanced sensitivity in detecting per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS).

</details>


### [16] [Unconventional Spin Valve Based on Normal Metal/Chiral Molecule/Altermagnet Junctions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11788)
*Tian-Yi Zhang,Peng-Yi Liu,Yu-Fei Sun,Ai-Min Guo,Qing-Feng Sun*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes combining chiral molecules with altermagnets to create highly efficient and tunable spin valves. Using theoretical methods (nonequilibrium Green's function and Landauer-Büttiker formula), the authors find that conductance can be tuned by reorienting the altermagnet's Néel vector, and magnetoresistance increases with molecular length and altermagnetic anisotropy, while vanishing for achiral molecules or without molecular spin-orbit coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To combine chiral molecules (which exhibit the chiral-induced spin selectivity effect) with altermagnets to construct highly efficient and tunable spin valves for spintronics applications.

Method: The authors use the nonequilibrium Green's function method and the Landauer-Büttiker formula to theoretically calculate the conductance and magnetoresistance of a normal metal/chiral molecule/altermagnet spin valve.

Result: Conductance can be effectively tuned by reorienting the altermagnet's Néel vector; magnetoresistance increases with molecular length and altermagnetic anisotropy; magnetoresistance vanishes for achiral molecules or in the absence of molecular spin-orbit coupling.

Conclusion: This work paves the way for developing efficient, controllable, and stray-field-free spintronic devices by integrating chiral molecules with altermagnets.

Abstract: Chiral molecules have attracted broad interdisciplinary interest for their ability to produce highly spin-polarized current. This phenomenon, known as the chiral-induced spin selectivity effect, holds great potential in the field of spintronics. Here, we propose to combine chiral molecules with altermagnets to construct highly efficient and tunable spin valves. Using the nonequilibrium Green's function method and the Landauer-Büttiker formula, we obtain the conductance and the magnetoresistance of a normal metal/chiral molecule/altermagnet spin valve. Our theoretical results reveal that the conductance of the spin valve can be effectively tuned by reorienting the Néel vector of the altermagnet, and the magnetoresistance of the spin valve increases with molecular length and altermagnetic anisotropy. Moreover, the magnetoresistance vanishes for achiral molecules or in the absence of molecular spin-orbit coupling. Our work paves the way for developing efficient, controllable, and stray-field-free spintronic devices.

</details>
