### [17] [SlaKoNet-VQD: A universal Slater-Koster tight-binding Hamiltonian for variational quantum band-structure calculations on near-term hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09761)
*Akshaya Ajith,Jaehyung Lee,Charles Rhys Campbell,Kamal Choudhary*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: SlaKoNet combines deep learning with the Slater-Koster tight-binding formalism to enable deterministic, structure-agnostic Hamiltonian construction for periodic solids. Coupled with VQD and executed on quantum simulators/hardware, it achieves meV-level accuracy for band structures and extends to correlated materials via DMFT, enabling high-throughput VQA benchmarking and materials discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Variational quantum algorithms (VQE/VQD) are promising for electronic structure but limited in periodic solids by the high computational cost of constructing faithful second-quantized Hamiltonians, which typically require DFT plus Wannierization or hand-fit tight-binding parameters.

Method: SlaKoNet — a deep-learning model trained on JARVIS-TBmBJ data using the Slater-Koster formalism — is coupled with a Qiskit-based VQD algorithm, replacing costly Hamiltonian construction with a universal neural Hamiltonian generator. The workflow is structure-agnostic, differentiable, and extended to Hubbard models solved via DMFT.

Result: Recovers full eight-band silicon structure along the standard k-path with mean absolute deviation of 1.78 meV from exact diagonalization on a 3-qubit simulator. Similar accuracy on five superconductors (Al, Ta, Nb, V, ZrN). Demonstrated on IBM Quantum hardware for Al k-point ground-state calculation (MAE ~0.37 eV). DMFT extension recovers weakening correlations across group-5 metals and strong quasiparticle renormalization in La2CuO4.

Conclusion: SlaKoNet-VQD enables high-throughput VQA benchmarking across the periodic table and supports gradient-based ansatz-Hamiltonian co-optimization for materials discovery, with the impurity problem identified as a natural quantum solver target.

Abstract: Variational quantum algorithms such as VQE and VQD are promising for near-term electronic structure calculations, but for periodic solids their reach is limited by the cost of building a faithful second-quantized Hamiltonian, typically via DFT plus Wannierization or hand-fit tight-binding parameters. SlaKoNet addresses this by combining deep learning with the Slater-Koster tight-binding formalism to fit hopping and overlap parameters across 65 elements, enabling deterministic Hamiltonian construction for any crystal built from these elements. Here we couple a SlaKoNet model trained on JARVIS-TBmBJ with a Qiskit-based VQD algorithm, replacing costly Hamiltonian construction with a universal neural Hamiltonian generator. The resulting SlaKoNet-VQD workflow is structure-agnostic, differentiable, and suited to high-throughput bandstructure screening. We benchmark on silicon, recovering the full eight-band structure along the standard k-path with mean absolute deviation of 1.78 meV from exact diagonalization on a 3-qubit simulator, and extend to five conventional superconductors (Al, Ta, Nb, V, ZrN) with similar accuracy. We demonstrate execution on IBM Quantum hardware for a k-point ground-state calculation on aluminum (MAE ~0.37 eV). We further promote the Hamiltonian to a correlated Hubbard model solved via dynamical mean-field theory, recovering weakening correlations across group-5 metals and strong quasiparticle renormalization in La2CuO4, identifying the impurity problem as a natural quantum solver target. This pipeline enables high-throughput VQA benchmarking across the periodic table and gradient-based ansatz-Hamiltonian co-optimization for materials discovery. Web app: https://atomgpt.org/quantum.

</details>


### [18] [Approximate explicit formulas for Stoner-Wohlfarth hysteresis loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09881)
*Savin Vladimir P.,Koksharov Yury A*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper derives approximate explicit formulas for hysteresis loops in the Stoner-Wohlfarth model, both for single particles with fixed easy-axis orientation and ensembles with random anisotropy axes. The formulas are based on the assumption that the magnetic moment lies near the easy axis at low fields and near the external field direction at high fields, achieving good accuracy even near the Stoner-Wohlfarth astroid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To derive simple, explicit approximate formulas for hysteresis loops in the Stoner-Wohlfarth model that are accurate across different field regimes, avoiding the need for full numerical calculations.

Method: The authors use physical assumptions that the magnetic moment stays near the easy axis at low fields and near the external field at high fields. They derive low-field and high-field approximations, then match these functions piecewise across different field intervals, choosing intervals that maximize formula accuracy.

Result: The low-field formula remains approximately valid even near the Stoner-Wohlfarth astroid where h0 is not very small. For the averaged hysteresis loop, the maximum deviation from numerical results occurs at h0 = 0.5, corresponding to the sharp change in magnetization slope.

Conclusion: The derived piecewise explicit formulas provide a reasonably accurate approximation of hysteresis loops in the Stoner-Wohlfarth model, with controlled and small deviations from exact numerical calculations.

Abstract: Approximate explicit formulas for the hysteresis loops in the Stoner-Wohlfarth model are derived. We consider the hysteresis loops both for a single particle with a fixed easy-axis direction and for an ensemble of particles with randomly oriented anisotropy axes. The physical assumption used to derive the formulas is that the particle magnetic moment lies in the vicinity of the easy axis or the external field direction, at low and high fields, respectively. Surprisingly, the low-field formula is approximately valid even near the Stoner-Wohlfarth astroid, where the reduced magnetic field h0 is not very small. The general piecewise formula is obtained by an appropriate matching of the functions defined on different intervals of the magnetic field, which are chosen to maximize the formula accuracy. For the averaged hysteresis loop, the maximal, but reasonably small, deviation of our formula from numerically calculated magnetization occurs at h0 = 0.5, which corresponds to the sharp change in magnetization slope.

</details>


### [19] [Bragg Interferometry of Moiré Superlattices: From Geometric Phase Principles to Atomic Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09901)
*Isaac M. Craig,D. Kwabena Bediako*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review surveys characterization techniques for mapping atomic reconstruction in moiré superlattices, with emphasis on Bragg interferometry—a dark-field method using overlapping Bragg reflections—for measuring interlayer displacement fields and strain in twisted bilayer/trilayer graphene and TMD moiré systems, discussing its advantages for buried interfaces and challenges from dynamical scattering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The emergence of moiré superlattices from twisted/stacked 2D materials has created a need for characterization techniques capable of mapping sub-angstrom atomic reconstruction across micron-scale fields of view.

Method: Comparative analysis of geometric phase analysis, converged beam electron diffraction holography, ptychographic paradigms, and Bragg interferometry (dark-field interferometric method using interference of overlapping Bragg reflections).

Result: Demonstrated utility of Bragg interferometry through case studies of twisted bilayer/trilayer graphene and TMD moiré systems; identified unique advantages for probing buried interfaces and encapsulated heterostructures, along with challenges from incomplete phase information under dynamical scattering.

Conclusion: The review highlights conceptual similarities and practical trade-offs among high-resolution structural mapping approaches, positioning Bragg interferometry as a key technique for studying the interplay between structural relaxation and electronic behavior in moiré materials.

Abstract: The emergence of moiré superlattices formed by twisting and stacking two-dimensional materials has created a need for characterization techniques capable of mapping sub-angstrom atomic reconstruction across micron-scale fields of view. This review surveys a suite of methodologies developed to extract geometric phases in electron microscopy and X-ray spectroscopy, with a primary focus on the interference of overlapping Bragg reflections in the dark field. We provide a comparative analysis of some established techniques, including geometric phase analysis, converged beam electron diffraction holography, and various ptychographic paradigms, culminating in a discussion of Bragg interferometry for measuring interlayer displacement fields and strain in moiré materials. We demonstrate the utility of Bragg interferometry through case studies of twisted bilayer and trilayer graphene as well as transition metal dichalcogenide moiré systems. Special attention is given to the unique advantages of this dark-field interferometric method for probing buried interfaces and encapsulated heterostructures, as well as the inherent challenges of interpreting incomplete phase information in the presence of dynamical scattering. By examining the physical principles underlying these approaches, this review highlights the conceptual similarities and practical trade-offs involved in high-resolution structural mapping of materials in which the interplay between structural relaxation and electronic behavior defines a scientific frontier at the nexus of modern condensed matter physics, nanomaterials engineering, and interfacial chemistry.

</details>


### [20] [High-Quality Ge-Doped (010) $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ Homoepitaxial Films Grown by Low-pressure CVD: Structural, Electrical, and Schottky Diode Characteristics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10907)
*Ahmed Ibreljic,Saleh Ahmed Khan,Sourav Sarker,Stephen Margiotta,Stephen Lam,Anhar Bhuiyan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Ge-doped β-Ga₂O₃ homoepitaxial films were grown by LPCVD, demonstrating controlled n-type doping (carrier concentration ~10¹⁷–10¹⁸ cm⁻³, mobility up to 105 cm²/V·s), high crystalline quality, and effective Ge donor activation (14 meV). Vertical Schottky barrier diodes fabricated from the films showed good rectification (turn-on 0.74 V, ideality 1.32, barrier height ~1.02 eV, R_on,sp 2.49 mΩ·cm²), positioning LPCVD Ge-doped β-Ga₂O₃ as a strong candidate for high-voltage power electronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve controllable n-type doping in β-Ga₂O₃ homoepitaxial films via Ge doping using LPCVD, enabling high-quality drift layers for next-generation high-voltage power electronic devices.

Method: Ge-doped β-Ga₂O₃ films were grown on (010) substrates by LPCVD. Characterization included Hall measurements (temperature-dependent), X-ray diffraction, Raman spectroscopy, XPS, and AFM. Vertical Ni/β-Ga₂O₃ Schottky barrier diodes were fabricated and evaluated via I-V and C-V measurements.

Result: Carrier concentration: 7.4×10¹⁷–2.57×10¹⁸ cm⁻³; mobility: 105–62 cm²/V·s; RMS roughness: 2.94–3.97 nm; peak mobility 234 cm²/V·s at 116 K; shallow donor activation energy 14 meV; Schottky diode: turn-on 0.74 V, ideality factor 1.32, barrier height ~1.02–1.13 eV, specific on-resistance 2.49 mΩ·cm².

Conclusion: LPCVD enables controllable Ge doping with high structural and electronic quality, establishing LPCVD-grown Ge-doped β-Ga₂O₃ as a promising platform for high-voltage power electronics.

Abstract: In this work, Ge-doped $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ homoepitaxial films were grown on native (010) $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ substrates using low-pressure chemical vapor deposition (LPCVD). Controlled $n$-type doping was achieved with room-temperature carrier concentrations ranging from $7.4\times10^{17}$ to $2.57\times10^{18}\ \mathrm{cm}^{-3}$ and corresponding electron mobilities of 105-62 cm$^2$/V$\cdot$s. The films exhibited smooth surface morphology with RMS roughness values of 2.94-3.97 nm, while X-ray diffraction, Raman spectroscopy, and X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy confirmed phase-pure $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ with excellent crystalline quality and near-stoichiometric composition. Temperature-dependent Hall measurements on the film with a room-temperature carrier concentration of $7.4\times10^{17}\ \mathrm{cm}^{-3}$ and mobility of 105 cm$^2$/V$\cdot$s yielded a peak electron mobility of 234 cm$^2$/V$\cdot$s at 116 K, while charge-neutrality and transport modeling revealed a dominant shallow donor level with an activation energy of 14 meV, confirming efficient electrical activation of Ge donors. Vertical Ni/$β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ Schottky barrier diodes fabricated using the Ge-doped drift layer exhibited good rectifying behavior with a turn-on voltage of 0.74 V, an ideality factor of 1.32, a Schottky barrier height of 1.02 eV, and a specific on-resistance of 2.49 m$Ω\cdot$cm$^2$. Capacitance-voltage measurements yielded a net donor concentration of $7.7\times10^{17}\ \mathrm{cm}^{-3}$ and a Schottky barrier height of 1.13 eV, in good agreement with Hall and current-voltage measurements. These results demonstrate that LPCVD enables controllable Ge doping while maintaining high structural and electronic quality, establishing LPCVD-grown Ge-doped $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ as a promising platform for future high-voltage power electronic devices.

</details>


### [21] [The Precursor Genome: A Pairwise Reaction Dataset for Solid-State Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09903)
*Lauren N. Walters,Matthew J. McDermott,Bernardus Rendy,Yuxing Fei,Kristin A. Persson,Gerbrand Ceder*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The Precursor Genome is a dataset of 1,035 pairwise solid-state reactions autonomously generated by the A-Lab self-driving laboratory, with full experimental metadata and expert-validated XRD-based phase assignments, providing a FAIR benchmark for machine learning in synthesis science.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No large, machine-readable dataset exists that reports solid-state reaction experimental protocols and outcomes with consistent provenance, hindering data-driven and machine-learning approaches to synthesis science.

Method: 1,035 pairwise solid-state reactions were autonomously generated by the A-Lab self-driving laboratory; product mixtures were identified from 1,351 raw X-ray diffraction scans via automated Rietveld refinement using the Dara framework, resulting in 1,950 refinement cases independently validated by human experts on a three-tier quality scale; full traceability is maintained through a Pydantic-validated JSON ledger.

Result: The dataset spans 46 precursors and 39 elements, includes measured thermal profiles, precursor and recovered masses, instrument configuration, and expert-validated phase assignments with three-tier quality scoring.

Conclusion: The Precursor Genome establishes a FAIR, reusable benchmark for training and evaluating predictive models of solid-state reactivity, enabling first-principles, data-driven, and machine-learning approaches to synthesis science.

Abstract: Solid-state reactions remain the dominant route to inorganic materials, yet no large, machine-readable dataset reports their experimental protocols and outcomes with consistent provenance; this gap obstructs first-principles, data-driven, and machine-learning approaches to synthesis science. Here, we present the Precursor Genome, a dataset of 1,035 pairwise solid-state reactions generated autonomously by the A-Lab self-driving laboratory, spanning 46 precursors and 39 elements. Every reaction is reported together with its full experimental metadata, including measured thermal profiles, precursor and recovered masses, and instrument configuration. Every product mixture is identified from raw X-ray diffraction (1,351 scans) through automated Rietveld refinement with the Dara framework, yielding 1,950 refinement cases that are independently validated by human experts on a three-tier quality scale. Raw pattern files, serialized refinement objects, and reviewer annotations are distributed through a Pydantic-validated JSON ledger, preserving full traceability from each precursor pair to its final phase assignment. The Precursor Genome establishes a FAIR, reusable benchmark for training and evaluating predictive models of solid-state reactivity.

</details>


### [22] [High-Mobility Ge-Doped $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ Growth on Sapphire by Low-Pressure Chemical Vapor Deposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10908)
*Ahmed Ibreljic,Saleh Ahmed Khan,Sourav Sarker,Ibrahim Isah,Stephen Margiotta,Michael Davenport,Stephen Lam,Anhar Bhuiyan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Ge-doped β-Ga₂O₃ thin films grown on offcut sapphire substrates via LPCVD achieved record-high electron mobilities (117 cm²/V s at RT, 337 cm²/V s at 128 K) due to improved step-flow growth, with transport modeling revealing shallow donors, low compensation, and dislocation scattering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the impact of sapphire substrate offcut angle on the structural, compositional, and electrical properties of heteroepitaxial Ge-doped β-Ga₂O₃ thin films, aiming to improve crystalline quality and achieve higher carrier mobilities for potential power electronic applications.

Method: Ge-doped (-201) β-Ga₂O₃ films were grown on c-plane sapphire with 0°, 2°, 6°, and 8° offcut angles using LPCVD. Characterization included XRD, Raman, XPS, Hall effect measurements, and transport modeling using charge-neutrality and Boltzmann transport models incorporating multiple scattering mechanisms.

Result: 6° offcut sample exhibited the best performance: RT mobility of 117 cm²/V s (n=1.43×10¹⁷ cm⁻³) and peak low-T mobility of 337 cm²/V s at 128 K (n=8.96×10¹⁶ cm⁻³), the highest reported for Ge-doped β-Ga₂O₃ on sapphire. Fitting revealed shallow donor energies of 12.5-19 meV, a deeper 80 meV level, low acceptor compensation (<5×10¹⁵ cm⁻³), and dislocation density ~10⁹ cm⁻².

Conclusion: Increasing sapphire offcut angle promotes step-flow growth, reducing surface roughness and improving crystallinity. The 6° offcut yields optimal electrical properties, demonstrating that substrate offcut engineering is an effective strategy for high-mobility heteroepitaxial β-Ga₂O₃ films.

Abstract: In this work, high-quality Ge-doped (-201) $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ thin films were heteroepitaxially grown on c-plane sapphire substrates with offcut angles of 0 deg, 2 deg, 6 deg, and 8 deg using low-pressure chemical vapor deposition (LPCVD). Increasing sapphire offcut promoted step-flow growth, resulting in improved terrace alignment, reduced surface roughness, and enhanced crystalline quality. Phase-pure monoclinic $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ with strong (-201) preferential orientation was confirmed by X-ray diffraction and Raman spectroscopy, while X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy revealed near-stoichiometric composition with an O/Ga ratio of 1.48. Electrical transport properties exhibited a strong dependence on substrate offcut angle, with room-temperature Hall mobility increasing from 15 to 117 cm$^2$/V s as the offcut angle increased from 0 deg to 6 deg, across carrier concentrations spanning $1.43 \times 10^{17}$ to $2.75 \times 10^{18}$ cm$^{-3}$. The 6 deg offcut sample achieved a room-temperature mobility of 117 cm$^2$/V s at a carrier concentration of $1.43 \times 10^{17}$ cm$^{-3}$ and a peak low-temperature mobility of 337 cm$^2$/V s at 128 K with a carrier concentration of $8.96 \times 10^{16}$ cm$^{-3}$, representing the highest reported room-temperature and low-temperature mobilities for Ge-doped $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ films grown on sapphire substrates. Carrier concentration and mobility data were analyzed using charge-neutrality and Boltzmann transport models incorporating donor activation together with polar optical phonon, ionized impurity, neutral impurity, acoustic deformation potential, and dislocation scattering mechanisms. The fitting revealed shallow donor activation energies of 12.5-19 meV, a deeper donor level at 80 meV, low acceptor compensation ($< 5 \times 10^{15}$ cm$^{-3}$), and threading dislocation densities on the order of $10^9$ cm$^{-2}$.

</details>


### [23] [Auditing Machine-Learning Models and Their Training Data with Explainability and First-Principles Verification: Application to Spin Hall Conductivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09910)
*Mohammed Mahshook,Rudra Banerjee*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper introduces a model-agnostic audit protocol combining SHAP attribution, counterfactual partial dependence analysis, and Rashomon-style cross-model verification, adjudicated by targeted DFT calculations, to test two untested assumptions in materials ML: that features reflect physics rather than training distribution accidents, and that training labels are correct. Demonstrated on intrinsic spin Hall conductivity, the audit revealed both a spurious p-valence-Pt entanglement and a 30× label error in HfC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard validation never tests two core assumptions of materials ML models: (1) that features reflect true physics rather than training distribution artifacts, and (2) that training labels are correct. These blind spots can produce deceptively accurate models built on spurious correlations or erroneous data.

Method: A model-agnostic audit protocol combining three techniques: SHAP attribution (feature importance), counterfactual partial dependence analysis (what-if scenarios), and Rashomon-style cross-model verification (comparing diverse models for consensus). All findings are adjudicated by targeted density functional theory (DFT) calculations. Demonstrated on a composition-only Random Forest for intrinsic spin Hall conductivity.

Result: (1) The model audit revealed that average p-valence descriptor becomes statistically entangled with Pt content — a spurious correlation from training distribution, not physics. DFT confirmed this by identifying a Pt-free compound (HgOsPb₂) with true SHC nearly 4× the prediction. (2) The data audit exposed a 30× error in the HfC training label, undetectably inherited by any black-box model trained on those data.

Conclusion: The protocol can audit a model and its training data at the cost of a few DFT calculations wherever one element dominates the high-property regime, providing a practical safeguard against physically meaningless features and undetected label errors.

Abstract: Machine-learning models for materials properties rest on two assumptions that standard validation never tests: that a model's features reflect the physics of the property rather than accidents of the training distribution, and that the training labels are themselves correct. We introduce a model-agnostic audit protocol for both, combining SHAP attribution, counterfactual partial dependence analysis, and Rashomon-style cross-model verification, with every finding adjudicated by targeted density functional theory (DFT). Demonstrated on intrinsic spin Hall conductivity using a composition-only Random Forest, the model needs no relaxed crystal structure, reaching accuracy competitive with structure-aware graph networks while remaining applicable to the far larger space of compositions for which no structure has been computed. The model audit reveals that the average p-valence descriptor becomes statistically entangled with Pt content - a property of the learned representation rather than the physics; DFT confirms the consequence, a Pt-free compound (HgOsPb$_2$) whose true SHC is nearly four times the prediction. The data audit exposes a thirtyfold error in the HfC training label, inherited undetectably by every black-box model trained on the same data. The protocol audits a model and its training data for the cost of a few DFT calculations, wherever one element dominates the high-property regime.

</details>


### [24] [Telecom-band Chiral Light Detection through Hidden Giant Third-Order Nonlinear Circular Dichroism in Two-dimensional Halide Perovskite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11037)
*Yuki Takahashi,Daichi Okada,Koshi Oi,Kazuki Mitamura,Takahiko Endo,Yasumitsu Miyata,Taishi Takenobu,Kenichi Yamashita*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates telecom-band circularly polarized light detection using third harmonic generation circular dichroism in chiral 2D perovskites, revealing a giant hidden THG-CD anisotropy that is accessible via polarization-resolved detection, and achieves dissymmetry factors exceeding 1.9.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While chiral NLO effects are well-studied for second-order processes, the role of chirality in third-order NLO processes remains poorly explored, motivating the need to investigate THG-CD in chiral materials.

Method: Polarization-resolved third harmonic generation (THG) measurements were performed on chiral 2D perovskite (R/S-MBACl)2PbI4 to study THG circular dichroism (THG-CD) at telecom wavelengths.

Result: Opposite chiral NLO responses emerge in orthogonal THG polarization channels, which cancel in total-THG detection; separating these hidden contributions yields exceptionally large dissymmetry factors exceeding 1.9 and enables selective extraction of chiral NLO responses with opposite handedness without structural inversion.

Conclusion: Polarization-resolved analysis is essential for evaluating accurate chiral NLO responses, and this platform shows promise for optical information processing, encryption, and anti-counterfeiting at telecommunication wavelengths.

Abstract: Chiral nonlinear optical (NLO) responses enable efficient discrimination of circularly polarized light (CPL) and are attracting increasing interest for optical and optoelectronic technologies. However, studies on NLO properties in chiral materials have largely focused on second-order NLO processes, while the role of chirality in third-order NLO processes remains poorly explored. Here, we demonstrate telecom-band CPL detection by third harmonic generation circular dichroism (THG-CD) in the chiral two-dimensional perovskite (R/S-MBACl)2PbI4 and uncover a giant hidden THG-CD anisotropy that is accessible via polarization-resolved detection. Polarization-resolved THG measurements reveal that opposite chiral NLO responses emerge in orthogonal THG polarization channels. Therefore, these chiral anisotropic contributions largely cancel each other in the total-THG signal detection, leading to underestimation of the THG-CD dissymmetry in conventional evaluations based on total-THG intensity. By separating these hidden chiral contributions, we observe exceptionally large dissymmetry factors exceeding 1.9 and achieve selective extraction of chiral NLO responses with opposite handedness, without any structural chiral inversion. These findings highlight the importance of polarization-resolved analysis for evaluating more accurate chiral NLO responses inherent to the material and provide a promising platform for optical information processing, encryption, and anti-counterfeiting technologies at technologically relevant telecommunication wavelengths.

</details>


### [25] [Improved Heat Dissipation in CsPbBr${_3}$-hBN Heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09914)
*Liudmila Starodubtceva,Ahmed Kadid,Naho Kurahashi,Cedric Kreusel,Jasper Ruhkop,Sebastian Lukas,Ulrich Plachetka,Ralf Heiderhoff,Thomas Riedl,Maryam Mohammadi,Max C. Lemme*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that capping CsPbBr₃ perovskite thin films with few-layer hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) via a semidry transfer method increases thermal conductivity sevenfold (from 0.45 to 3 W/(m·K)), reducing laser-induced heating under high-power excitation without degrading optical properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metal halide perovskites have low thermal conductivity, causing heat accumulation under high excitation levels that limits practical device operation. Integrating thermally conductive, transparent 2D materials could suppress this heat buildup while preserving optical performance.

Method: Planar hot-pressed (PHP) CsPbBr₃ thin films were capped with few-layer 2D hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) via a large-scale semidry transfer method. Thermal conductivity was measured, and heat diffusion simulations were performed to compare heterostructures with bare films.

Result: The hBN-capped PHP CsPbBr₃ heterostructure achieved a thermal conductivity of 3 W/(m·K), ~7× higher than bare perovskite films (0.45 W/(m·K)). The hBN capping layer did not alter morphology or optical properties. Heat diffusion simulations confirmed enhanced heat dissipation.

Conclusion: Capping perovskites with transparent, thermally conductive 2D hBN is an effective approach to enhance heat dissipation, enabling more stable operation under high-power excitation.

Abstract: Metal halide perovskite semiconductors are promising materials for optoelectronic and photonic devices, including solar cells and next-generation coherent light sources. However, their low thermal conductivity limits the practical operation of devices under high excitation levels. Integrating thermally conductive, large band-gap two-dimensional (2D) materials into perovskite devices could suppress heat accumulation, while preserving their optical properties. Here, we show that planar hot-pressed (PHP) cesium lead bromide (CsPbBr${_3}$) thin films capped with few-layer 2D hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) are less affected by laser-induced heating under high-power continuous-wave excitation than uncapped perovskite samples. A large-scale semidry transfer method was developed to integrate 2D hBN onto PHP CsPbBr${_3}$ thin films. The process is chemically and thermally compatible with perovskites. Microscopic and spectroscopic analyses show that the hBN capping layer does not alter the morphology and optical properties of the perovskite thin film. The PHP CsPbBr${_3}$-hBN heterostructure exhibits a thermal conductivity of 3 W/(m * K), approximately seven times higher than that of the bare perovskite films of 0.45 W/(m * K). Heat diffusion simulations confirm enhanced heat dissipation in the heterostructure relative to bare perovskite films. Our experiments demonstrate an effective approach to enhancing heat dissipation in perovskite devices using transparent, thermally conductive 2D materials.

</details>


### [26] [Anomalous Transverse Response and Multi-Field Ferrialtermagnetic-Ferroelectric Valve with CrSb Flakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11222)
*Long Zhang,Xinfeng Chen,Hongfei Liang,Jianting Dong,Fei Zou,Yi Yan,Guangqian Ding,Guoying Gao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper proposes ferrialtermagnetism as a new subclass of altermagnets with nonidentical sublattice Neel vectors, realized in CrSb (110) flakes. It demonstrates large spin splitting, high Neel temperature, and integrates with ferroelectrics to create a multi-field-controlled valve with ultrahigh tunneling magnetoresistance and electroresistance (~10^3-10^4%), high spin filtering (90%), and photocurrent extinction ratio of 283.8.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the altermagnetic family by introducing ferrialtermagnetism, where inequivalent sublattices prevent cancellation of spin splitting, offering intrinsic robustness for spintronic applications.

Method: Realization of ferrialtermagnetism in three-atomic-layer CrSb (110) flakes; integration with ferroelectric Sc2CO2 and Cu spacer to design a ferrialtermagnetic-ferroelectric valve.

Result: Spin splitting of 344 meV, uniaxial magnetic anisotropy, Neel temperature of 657 K; equilibrium TMR and electroresistance ~10^3%, non-equilibrium magnitudes ~10^4% with 90% spin filtering; negative differential resistance, photogalvanic effects, and photocurrent extinction ratio of 283.8.

Conclusion: Ferrialtermagnetism provides a fertile platform for multi-field-controlled, ultracompact, self-powered spintronics and electronics.

Abstract: Altermagnets combine the zero-stray-field of antiferromagnets with the spin polarization of ferromagnets, showing great potential for spintronic applications. Here, we propose ferrialtermagnetism as a distinct subclass of altermagnetic family, where symmetry-inequivalent altermagnetic sublattices possess nonidentical Neel vectors, preventing mutual cancellation of alternating spin splitting and conferring intrinsic robustness against perturbations. This concept is realized in the three-atomic-layer CrSb (110) flakes, which exhibits spin splitting of 344 meV, moderate uniaxial magnetic anisotropy, and high Neel temperature of 657 K. The magneto-optical Kerr and the anomalous Hall effects are observed. Integrating this ferrialtermagnetic CrSb with ferroelectric Sc2CO2 and Cu spacer, we design an ferrialtermagnetic-ferroelectric valve. This device displays equilibrium tunneling magnetoresistance and electroresistance of ~10^3%, and non-equilibrium magnitudes under bias, thermal, or light field reaches ~10^4% with high spin filtering of 90%. The negative differential resistance and photogalvanic effects, and photocurrent extinction ratio of 283.8 are achieved. These findings establish ferrialtermagnetism as a fertile platform for multi-field-controlled, ultracompact, and self-powered spintronics and electronics.

</details>


### [27] [Thermodynamics of hydride formation: Anisotropic size-dependent coupled chemo-thermo-mechanical effects at Ni/NiH interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09977)
*Sourabh Singha,Abhijit Chatterjee*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a simplified chemo-thermo-mechanical enthalpy model to explain the anisotropic growth of Ni hydride in thin films, accounting for hydride fraction, temperature, pressure, size effects, and interface orientation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain the observed anisotropic hydride growth and coherent Ni/NiH interface formation in small Ni nanoparticles under low hydrogen pressure, and to comprehensively couple chemical, mechanical, and thermal effects.

Method: A simplified chemo-thermo-mechanical enthalpy model for Ni/NiH interfaces in thin films is developed, incorporating variables: hydride fraction (x), temperature (T), pressure (P), size effect (l), and α/β interface energy (λ). Two interface orientations, (100) and (111), are investigated.

Result: The model correctly predicts enthalpy and volume changes across length scales from ~1 nm to micron scale and larger.

Conclusion: This work provides a foundation for developing similar enthalpy models for other solid-state hydrogen storage nanostructured materials exhibiting anisotropic hydride growth.

Abstract: Ni nanoparticles are frequently used as catalysts for hydrogenation reactions as well as in hydrogen storage applications. Recently, we have shown that small Ni nanoparticles can absorb hydrogen at < 10 bar pressure to form Ni hydride. During this process, the hydride growth is anisotropic, and a coherent Ni/NiH interface is formed. In order to explain the anisotropy and to comprehensively account for the coupling chemical, mechanical and thermal effects, we develop in this study a simplified chemo-thermo-mechanical enthalpy model for Ni/NiH interfaces in thin films. This model captures the combined influence of extent of hydride formation x, temperature T, pressure P, size effect ($l$), and the $α$/$β$ interface energy $λ$. Two different $α$/$β$ interface orientations, namely (100) and (111), are investigated. The model is shown to correctly predict the enthalpy and volume changes over a wide range of length scales, from atomically thin layers (~1 nm) to micron scale and larger. This work provides the basis for the development of similar enthalpy models for other solid-state hydrogen storage nanostructured materials where anisotropic growth of hydride phases is also observed.

</details>


### [28] [Probing the Nature of Interstitial Anionic Electrons in 2D Electride Ca$_2$N via Landau-Level Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10024)
*Arjyama Bordoloi,Daniel Kaplan,Sobhit Singh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates the magnetic-field response of interstitial anionic electrons (IAEs) in 2D electrides (monolayer Ca₂N), finding that the Landau-level spectrum closely resembles a nearly-free 2D electron gas with only moderate deviations in cyclotron effective mass and g-factor, and that the bands are insensitive to exchange-correlation functional choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the quantum nature of interstitial anionic electrons (IAEs) in low-dimensional electrides under magnetic fields, and to explore magnetic confinement and correlation effects in these systems.

Method: Computed the Landau-level (LL) spectrum of electride bands forming the Fermi surface in monolayer Ca₂N under magnetic fields, and compared results using LDA vs. PBEsol exchange-correlation functionals.

Result: Linear LL evolution with magnetic field resembling a nearly-free 2D electron gas; moderate deviations in cyclotron effective mass and Landé g-factor from free-electron values; insensitivity of band dispersion to exchange-correlation functional choice.

Conclusion: IAEs in 2D electrides retain a remarkably free-electron-like character under magnetic confinement, with minimal influence from local exchange-correlation effects, opening avenues for exploring quantum phenomena in low-dimensional interstitial electronic systems.

Abstract: We investigate the magnetic-field response of interstitial anionic electrons (IAEs) in two-dimensional electrides, using monolayer Ca$_2$N as a prototypical system. By computing the Landau-level (LL) spectrum of the electride bands forming the Fermi surface, we find a linear LL evolution with magnetic field that closely resembles the behavior of a nearly-free 2D electron gas (2DEG). The extracted cyclotron effective mass and Landé g-factor deviate moderately from their free-electron values, indicating that the IAEs retain a remarkably free-electron-like character. Furthermore, the energy dispersion of the electride bands remains insensitive to the choice of exchange-correlation functional (LDA vs.~PBEsol), indicating that local exchange and correlation effects have minimal influence on the IAEs. Overall, our findings provide fundamental insight into the quantum nature of electrides and open new avenues for exploring magnetic confinement, correlation effects, and emergent quantum phenomena in low-dimensional interstitial electronic systems.

</details>


### [29] [Anisotropic representations for E(3)-equivariant machine learning coarse-grained potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10002)
*Varun Shankar,Emil Annevelink*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces an anisotropic machine learning coarse-graining potential using equivariant message-passing neural networks with massive ellipsoidal beads and orientation-dependent features, achieving accurate structural and dynamical reproduction of liquid water with 7-27× speedups while demonstrating that orientation information is essential for faithful CG simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coarse-graining reduces computational cost but often compromises structural fidelity or requires system-specific parameterization. The authors aim to address these limitations by introducing orientation-dependent features that capture directional interactions and shape anisotropy in polar and asymmetric molecules.

Method: The method extends point particle representations to massive ellipsoidal beads with orientation-dependent features using an equivariant message-passing neural network that learns energies, forces, and torques directly from atomistic data.

Result: The model accurately reproduces radial/angular distribution functions and orientation correlations in liquid water; comparison with an isotropic baseline shows orientation information is essential to avoid systematic errors in short/long range order and angular correlations. Simulations achieve 7-27× speedups.

Conclusion: Learned equivariant representations are both feasible and necessary for anisotropic CG modeling, providing a path towards accurate and efficient mesoscopic simulations of complex molecular liquids, polymers, and biomolecular systems.

Abstract: Coarse-graining (CG) lowers the computational cost of atomistic simulations by representing groups of atoms as effective interaction sites, reducing the degrees of freedom of the system but often compromising structural fidelity or requiring system-specific parameterization. Here, we introduce a novel anisotropic machine learning CG potential that extends the point particle representation of atomic nuclei to massive ellipsoidal beads with orientation-dependent features, enabling the learning of energies, forces, and torques directly from atomistic data. The anisotropic representation is physically motivated for polar and asymmetric molecules, where directional interactions and shape anisotropy play important roles in determining structure and dynamics. Using an equivariant message-passing neural network, the model accurately reproduces radial and angular distribution functions as well as relative orientation correlations in liquid water, demonstrating that both translational and rotational dynamics are well captured. Comparison with an isotropic baseline reveals that the lack of orientation information leads to systematic errors in short and long range order and degradation of angular correlations, proving orientation features are essential for accurate coarse-graining. The anisotropic model also exposes rotational structural observables fundamentally inaccessible to isotropic representations, with minimal computational overhead. Even for coarse-graining just three degrees of freedom, CG simulations achieve 7-27$\times$ speedups while preserving structural fidelity, highlighting the efficiency gains of this systemic reduction. This framework establishes the feasibility and necessity of learned equivariant representations for anisotropic CG modeling and provides a path towards accurate and efficient mesoscopic simulations of complex molecular liquids, polymers, and biomolecular systems.

</details>


### [30] [Tiling decomposition multiplicity predicts stability of GaN(0001) surface reconstructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11105)
*Tetsuji Kuboyama,Akira Kusaba,Karol Kawka,Pawel Kempisty*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The GaN(0001)-(6×6) surface structure search is reformulated as a discrete tiling problem under electron counting rules. Exhaustive enumeration yields 416,683 configurations, and a tiling multiplicity rule (n_til) predicts stability, reducing DFT candidate set to just 24 structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional heuristic/Bayesian DFT sampling cannot guarantee coverage of the vast configuration space for semiconductor surface adatom arrangements. The authors aim to replace sampling with exhaustive enumeration to achieve guaranteed completeness in structure prediction.

Method: The problem is formulated as a discrete rhombus tiling problem on the GaN(0001)-(6×6) surface, with electron counting rule constraints. All tilings and EC-compatible adatom arrangements are enumerated (416,683 configurations). Tiling decomposition multiplicity (n_til) is introduced as a stability predictor. A machine-learning interatomic potential validated against 710 DFT structures is used for screening.

Result: 14 Ga placement symmetry classes identified. In 13/14 classes, the configuration maximizing n_til is the most stable; in the 14th class, the n_til-max configuration is only 8.5 meV above the minimum. The rule reduces the candidate set from 416,683 to 24 configurations for DFT evaluation. The local mechanism is avoidance of adjacent bare surface sites.

Conclusion: Enumeration provides coverage guarantees that sampling cannot, enabling stable-structure prediction where DFT enters only at the final ranking step. The tiling multiplicity rule is an effective stability filter for EC-constrained surface systems.

Abstract: The stable adatom configurations of a semiconductor surface have traditionally been sought by sampling: density functional theory (DFT) energies steer a heuristic or Bayesian search through a configuration space far too large to cover. Here we show that, for the GaN(0001)-$(6\times6)$ surface under the electron counting (EC) rule, the search can instead be posed as a discrete tiling problem and solved exhaustively. Enumerating all rhombus tilings of the surface lattice, together with all EC-compatible adatom arrangements built on them, yields the complete catalog of 416,683 configurations at fixed stoichiometry (3 Ga adatoms and 18 H atoms), organized by symmetry into 14 Ga placement classes. The number of tilings compatible with a given configuration, its tiling decomposition multiplicity $n_\mathrm{til}$, predicts stability. Within each class, the configuration maximizing $n_\mathrm{til}$ is the most stable. The rule holds strictly in 13 of the 14 classes; in the remaining class the minimum is itself among the highest-multiplicity configurations, with the $n_\mathrm{til}$-max configuration only 8.5 meV above it; this ordering is reproduced by independent DFT calculations, and the difference is negligible at growth temperature. Stability screening uses a machine-learning interatomic potential validated against 710 DFT-computed structures. The rule reduces the candidate set for first-principles evaluation from 416,683 to 24 configurations, all of which have been evaluated with DFT. Analysis of the rule identifies the local mechanism, the avoidance of adjacent bare surface sites, while the existence of a compatible tiling remains a separate requirement with an energy cost of its own. Enumeration thus provides what sampling cannot: a coverage guarantee, and a route to stable-structure prediction in which first-principles input enters only at the final ranking step.

</details>


### [31] [Alloy engineering of Magnetic phases in two-dimensional Chromium Trihalides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10030)
*Pedro Roberto Lopes Vieira,Daniel D. Rivera,Lucas Martin Farigliano,Fernando P. Sabino,Gustavo Martini Dalpian*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: DFT study of CrX3 (X=Cl, Br, I) compounds and their ternary alloys shows FM ground state, small band gap variation across magnetic phases, pronounced band gap bowing in CrCl3-CrI3 alloys, and thermodynamic favorability of alloy formation at finite temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how alloying in CrX3 two-dimensional magnetic materials can tailor their electronic, magnetic, optical properties, and thermodynamic stability, addressing the intrinsic experimental instability of these compounds.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) study of pure CrX3 compounds and their ternary alloys, analyzing electronic structure, magnetic configurations (FM, AFM-Z, PM), band gaps, magnetic moments, Curie temperatures, mixing enthalpy, and configurational entropy.

Result: FM is the ground state for all pure compounds; AFM-Z and PM are close in energy. Band gap variation across magnetic phases ≤0.16 eV. Magnetic moments on Cr increase from Cl to Br to I. In alloys, FM remains lowest-energy; energy gap to AFM-Z decreases with lower I content. Band gap bowing observed along CrCl3-CrI3 edge. Curie temperatures vary smoothly. Gibbs free energy shows alloy formation is thermodynamically favorable at finite temperatures.

Conclusion: Alloying in CrX3 compounds is a viable route to tune properties while maintaining FM stability, and finite-temperature thermodynamic favorability helps overcome experimental instability challenges.

Abstract: Two-dimensional magnetic materials offer unique opportunities for exploring low-dimensional spin phenomena and next-generation spintronic devices. Chromium trihalides CrX3 (X = Cl, Br, I) belong to an important family of these materials, where alloying opens pathways for tailoring their electronic, magnetic, optical properties, and thermodynamic stability. In this work, we present a density functional theory study of CrX3 compounds and their ternary alloys. Our results show that for the pure compounds, the ground state is ferromagnetic (FM), with the antiferromagnetic-zigzag (AFM-Z) and paramagnetic (PM) phases being close in energy. For these pure systems, the band gap variation among different magnetic phases does not exceed 0.16 eV, and the average magnetic moments on Cr atoms increase from Cl to Br to I. For the alloys, the FM state remains the lowest-energy configuration, but the energy difference towards the AFM-Z phase decreases for compounds with lower iodine concentration. The calculated band gaps reveal a pronounced bowing along the compositional edge connecting CrCl3 and CrI3. The Curie temperatures show a smooth variation across compositions, consistent with the nearly linear behavior of the magnetic exchange parameters. Based on the calculated mixing enthalpy and configurational entropy, the approximate Gibbs free energy indicates that alloy formation becomes thermodynamically favorable at finite temperatures, which is important to overcome the intrinsic experimental instability of these compounds.

</details>


### [32] [Flux Jamming and Bimodal Dynamics in Bounded Spin Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10045)
*Paula Mellado,Hanu Arava*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A quantitative framework using non-homogeneous transfer matrices and Husimi trees predicts bimodal kinetic relaxation in finite square magnetic networks, where trapping intervals are interrupted by avalanche-like episodes, with boundary truncation yielding scale-invariant flux arrest akin to granular jamming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a predictive framework for understanding how kinetic barriers and temperature-dependent relaxation emerge in finite frustrated magnetic networks, bridging equilibrium energy landscape concepts with non-equilibrium phenomena like kinetic arrest and telegraph noise.

Method: Formulates non-homogeneous transfer matrices from adjacency spectra of the underlying graph; uses Husimi tree representation for finite systems; constructs transition probabilities from local Boltzmann factors; analyzes relaxation dynamics across forty generations.

Result: Predicts bimodal kinetic behavior with long trapping intervals within charge-compensated manifolds interrupted by avalanche-like relaxation episodes; low-temperature data from forty generations collapse onto a single power-law scaling curve, indicating boundary truncation alone can produce scale-invariant flux arrest.

Conclusion: The framework connects equilibrium energy landscape concepts to non-equilibrium phenomena, enabling prediction and design of intermittent transport in finite, frustrated networks.

Abstract: We present a quantitative framework for predicting how kinetic barriers governing temperature dependent relaxation arise in finite square magnetic networks. By formulating a series of non homogeneous transfer matrices from the adjacency spectrum of the underlying graph, we show how local coordination shapes the energy landscape and identifies geometric regions that act as bottlenecks for flux transport. Our approach predicts bimodal kinetic behavior, in which long intervals of trapping within charge compensated manifolds are interrupted by sudden, avalanche like relaxation episodes. Representing finite systems with a Husimi tree, we find that low temperature data from forty generations collapse onto a single curve consistent with a power law scaling form, implying that boundary truncation alone can give rise to scale invariant flux arrest, analogous to athermal granular jamming. By employing transition probabilities constructed from local Boltzmann factors, this framework connects equilibrium energy landscape concepts to non equilibrium phenomena, including kinetic arrest and telegraph noise, thereby enabling the prediction and design of intermittent transport in finite, frustrated networks.

</details>


### [33] [Amorphous materials as a frontier challenge for universal interatomic potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11384)
*Natascia L. Fragapane,Volker L. Deringer*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Current mainstream MLIPs struggle with transferability to amorphous systems; this work introduces a benchmarking framework with curated amorphous datasets, reveals limitations, and proposes fine-tuning strategies for disordered phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-trained MLIPs have focused on ordered crystalline structures, and their transferability to non-crystalline (amorphous) solids remains unclear.

Method: Systematic evaluation of current mainstream MLIPs on a curated reference dataset of canonical amorphous systems, with validation of structures and properties.

Result: Identified limitations in transferability of many current pre-trained models to amorphous systems; investigated fine-tuning strategies tailored to disordered phases.

Conclusion: The amorphous state is a central challenge for future universal MLIPs; results facilitate applications in amorphous functional materials and guide design of next-generation training datasets and transferable models.

Abstract: Pre-trained or 'foundational' machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) are now widely used in materials modelling. However, early pre-trained models and benchmarks have largely focused on ordered, crystalline structures, and their transferability to non-crystalline solids remains unclear. Here, we show that the amorphous state is indeed a central challenge for future universal MLIPs, based on a systematic evaluation of current mainstream models in this domain. We introduce a benchmarking framework built on a curated reference dataset of canonical amorphous systems, as well as validation for structures and properties. Our study identifies limitations in the transferability of many current pre-trained models and investigates fine-tuning strategies tailored to disordered phases. Together, our results can facilitate future applications of MLIPs in the fast-growing field of amorphous functional materials, and they provide guidance for designing next-generation training datasets and transferable atomistic models.

</details>


### [34] [Engineering Topology by Design in Two-dimensional Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10049)
*Arjyama Bordoloi,Sobhit Singh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review discusses the paradigm shift from intrinsic spin-orbit coupling-driven 2D topological insulators to van der Waals heterostructure engineering, where interfacial coupling, stacking, twist angle, chemical functionalization, and external stimuli enable topological phases in otherwise trivial materials, expanding the accessible materials landscape.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The traditional approach of relying on strong intrinsic spin-orbit coupling in specific materials severely constrains the materials landscape for 2D topological insulators. The motivation is to overcome this limitation by engineering topological phases in van der Waals heterostructures through interfacial coupling and external modulation, thereby expanding the range of accessible materials.

Method: Engineering topological phases in van der Waals heterostructures by leveraging interfacial coupling, and tuning multiple degrees of freedom (stacking configuration, twist angle, chemical functionalization) combined with external stimuli (electric fields, strain, light-matter coupling) to dynamically and reversibly control topological character.

Result: Realization of 2D topological insulator phases in systems that are otherwise topologically trivial, significantly expanding the materials landscape for dissipationless edge state technologies.

Conclusion: Van der Waals interface engineering combined with external modulation provides a versatile platform for realizing 2D topological insulator phases beyond traditional intrinsic materials, with key milestones reviewed and outstanding challenges and future directions outlined.

Abstract: Two-dimensional topological insulators (2D TIs) have emerged as a cornerstone of next-generation spintronic technologies due to their robust, dissipationless edge states protected by time-reversal symmetry. Initial realizations of 2D TIs have primarily focused on materials with strong intrinsic spin-orbit coupling capable of driving band inversion, an approach that significantly constrains the accessible materials landscape. More recently, a paradigm shift has occurred toward engineering topological phases in van der Waals (vdW) heterostructures, where nontrivial band topology can arise from interfacial coupling rather than relying solely on intrinsic material properties. This framework provides an exceptionally versatile platform with multiple tunable degrees of freedom, including stacking configuration, twist angle, and chemical functionalization, allowing systematic manipulation of the band topology. Furthermore, external stimuli, such as electric fields, strain, and light-matter coupling, enable dynamic and reversible control of the topological character. The combined use of vdW interface engineering and external modulation allows the realization of 2D TI phases even in otherwise topologically trivial systems, substantially expanding the accessible materials landscape. This Research Update reviews key milestones in the development of vdW-engineered 2D topological quantum materials, critically assesses outstanding theoretical and experimental challenges, and outlines promising directions for future breakthroughs.

</details>


### [35] [Catalog of Altermagnetism in Magnetic Wallpaper/Space Groups and Nonsymmorphic Altermagnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10857)
*Congcong Le,Fan Cui,Iao-Fai Io,Moritz Hirschmann,Xianxin Wu,Ching-Kai Chiu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper extends altermagnetism classification to non-centrosymmetric crystals using spinless time-reversal symmetry, catalogs 17 2D and 422 3D altermagnetic groups, and reveals novel hourglass dispersions from nonsymmorphic symmetries beyond the six standard wave types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional altermagnetism only covered centrosymmetric crystals with collinear spin alignment and spin splitting. This work aims to systematically extend the classification to all non-centrosymmetric crystals by introducing a non-spatial global symmetry (spinless time-reversal symmetry) that replaces inversion symmetry.

Method: The authors employ spinless time-reversal symmetry as a substitute for inversion symmetry to preserve spin-state equivalence, then systematically analyze symmetry conditions to catalog altermagnetic orders for all 2D magnetic wallpaper groups and 3D magnetic space groups. They also analyze nonsymmorphic symmetries to reveal hourglass dispersions via compatibility relations between same-spin and opposite-spin degeneracies.

Result: Identified 17 altermagnetic wallpaper groups (12 centrosymmetric, 5 non-centrosymmetric) and 422 altermagnetic space groups (160 centrosymmetric, 262 non-centrosymmetric). Each group is assigned to one of six altermagnetic wave types with distinct BZ spin distributions. Nonsymmorphic symmetries produce hourglass dispersions and new patterns beyond the six wave types, e.g., an i-wave-like spin winding in a tetragonal BZ.

Conclusion: The work extends the symmetry catalog of altermagnetism to non-centrosymmetric crystals and reveals that nonsymmorphic symmetries are essential for realizing altermagnetic band structures beyond the six established wave types, with spinless time-reversal symmetry playing a key role in both aspects.

Abstract: Conventional altermagnetism, characterized by compensated collinear spin alignment and spin splitting, exhibits identical spin states at opposite momenta. In this work, we employ a non-spatial global symmetry $S$, the spinless time-reversal symmetry, which effectively replaces inversion symmetry in preserving the spin-state equivalence; hence, we systematically extend the classification of altermagnetism to all possible non-centrosymmetric crystals. By analyzing the necessary symmetry conditions, we provide a complete catalog of altermagnetic orders for all 2D magnetic wallpaper groups and all 3D magnetic space groups, identifying 17 altermagnetic wallpaper groups (12 centrosymmetric and 5 non-centrosymmetric) and 422 altermagnetic space groups (160 centrosymmetric and 262 non-centrosymmetric). This catalog assigns each altermagnetic wallpaper and space group to one of the six altermagnetic wave types established in the literature and presents its distinct spin distribution in the Brillouin zone (BZ); notably, the low-energy wave-type description does not necessarily extend throughout the full BZ, since the spin-degenerate nodal lines and planes can be unpinned from the high-symmetry planes. Beyond the catalog, nonsymmorphic symmetries further bring new patterns of the altermagnetic BZs through the emergence of hourglass dispersions, which arise from the compatibility relations between two symmetry-protected degenerate manifolds: same-spin and opposite-spin degeneracies. In both the non-centrosymmetric altermagnetism and the emergence of the hourglass dispersion, the spinless time-reversal symmetry plays the key role. Our work extends the symmetry catalog of altermagnetism and reveals that nonsymmorphic symmetries are essential for realizing altermagnetic band structures beyond the six established wave types, such as an $i$-wave-like spin winding in a tetragonal BZ.

</details>


### [36] [Synthesis of Ti2B2Clx MBenes in molten salts from theoretical and experimental perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11550)
*Rodrigo M. Ronchi,Emile Defoy,Andrejs Petruhins,Justinas Palisaitis,Lianghao Yu,Lan Tang,Solenn Reguer,Dominique Thiaudière,Ningjun Chen,Durga Sankar Vavilapalli,David Portehault,Jonas Björk,Per O. Å. Persson,Johanna Rosen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper presents the synthesis of a multilayer Ti2B2Clx MBene via selective etching of Ti2InB2 using a molten salt (ZnCl2) approach. Characterization confirms complete removal of In, replacement by Cl terminations, direct biphasic transformation, and favorable thermodynamics. DFT calculations support experimental findings. The material shows promising Li-ion battery performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore new nanolaminated 2D compounds (MBenes) beyond the known MAX phases and MXenes, motivated by the unique properties of 2D materials.

Method: Molten salt etching of Ti2InB2 using ZnCl2; characterization via STEM, EDX, EELS, in situ XRD; DFT-based computational framework for thermodynamic analysis; Li-ion battery testing.

Result: Successful synthesis of multilayer Ti2B2Clx MBene with Cl termination coverage 1.1 < x < 1.4; direct biphasic transformation without intermediates; negative reaction free energy for MBene formation; no experimental evidence of Ti2ZnB2 formation; stable Li-ion discharge capacity comparable to or better than MAX phases and other borides.

Conclusion: The combined theoretical-experimental approach provides a general methodology for developing new 2D materials and expanding the MBene family.

Abstract: The unique properties and application possibilities of two-dimensional (2D) materials motivates the exploration of different nanolaminated compounds. Here, by using a molten salt approach, we selectively etch Ti2InB2 with ZnCl2 to produce a multilayer (ml) Ti2B2Clx MBene. Scanning transmission electron microscopy, in combination with energy dispersive X-ray, and electron energy loss spectroscopies show that In atoms are completely removed from the precursor upon etching, being replaced by chlorine surface terminations with a coverage 1.1 < x < 1.4. Further, in situ X-ray diffraction indicates a direct biphasic transformation from Ti2InB2 to ml-MBene, with no signs of intermediate phase formation. A computational framework based on density functional theory further corroborates these experimental observations by showing a negative reaction free energy for the formation of ml-MBene, favourable over all competing processes. In addition, A-element substitution into to the 3D Ti2ZnB2 phase is predicted to be endergonic, consistent with the absence of experimental evidence for its formation. Initial Li-ion battery performance evaluation showed a stable discharge capacity similar or better than MAX phases and other borides. Altogether, the theoretical framework combined with materials synthesis and characterization provides a general approach for 2D materials development, for further expansion of the family of 2D materials.

</details>


### [37] [Polarization Rotation Drives a Spin-Topological Transition in Ferroelectric Bismuth Monolayer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10063)
*Jinming Zhai,Lingzhi Cao,Yateng Wang,Huicong Li,Zhilong Yang,Yali Yang,Jiangang He*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper identifies a rotational pathway for in-plane polarization switching in bismuth monolayers with a four-times-lower energy barrier than direct reversal, explaining vortexlike domain textures and revealing a spin-topological transition (spin Chern number Cs=-2 to 0) controllable by uniaxial strain.

  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover the microscopic switching mechanism of ferroelectric Bi monolayer, which remains elusive despite being the first two-dimensional elemental ferroelectric and a platform for coupling polar order with spin-orbit-driven topology.

Method: First-principles lattice dynamics combined with symmetry-adapted mode analysis to investigate polarization switching pathways.

Conclusion: Establishes polarization rotation as the switching mechanism of ferroelectric Bi monolayer and demonstrates an efficient route to electrically and mechanically programmable topology in 2D ferroelectrics.

Abstract: Bismuth monolayer is the first two-dimensional elemental ferroelectric and an appealing platform for coupling polar order to spin-orbit-driven topology. However, its microscopic switching mechanism remains elusive. Here, using first-principles lattice dynamics and symmetry-adapted mode analysis, we identify a previously overlooked rotational pathway for in-plane polarization switching. Its energy barrier is more than four times lower than that of direct reversal, naturally explaining the vortexlike domain textures observed in molecular dynamics simulations. Remarkably, this polarization rotation also drives a spin-topological transition, changing the spin Chern number from $C_s=-2$ to $0$. Directional uniaxial strain further steers the polarization orientation and tunes the associated topological transition. These results establish polarization rotation as the switching mechanism of ferroelectric Bi monolayer and as an efficient route to electrically and mechanically programmable topology in two-dimensional ferroelectrics.

</details>


### [38] [Dynamical Nonrelativistic Spin Splitting via THz Nonlinear Phononics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10091)
*Linding Yuan,ChanJu You,Ankit Disa,James M. Rondinelli*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using DFT and nonlinear phononics, the authors show that THz laser pulses can achieve ultrafast, reversible control of nonrelativistic spin splitting (NRSS) in collinear antiferromagnets on picosecond timescales, demonstrated in NiO and LaFeO₃.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NRSS in collinear antiferromagnets enables high-frequency spintronics immune to stray fields, but dynamic control of NRSS has remained elusive, motivating the search for ultrafast manipulation strategies.

Method: The authors combine density functional theory (DFT) with nonlinear phononics, deriving two symmetry criteria based on phonon/magnetic wavevector compatibility and order-parameter parity to identify Raman-active phonon modes that can activate or amplify NRSS.

Result: Resonant driving of an 11.08 THz infrared-active mode in NiO transiently converts spin-degenerate NiO into an NRSS state (~40 meV time-averaged splitting). In LaFeO₃, selective excitation amplifies existing NRSS by ~100%. Both cases exhibit transient SOC-induced net moments detectable via the magneto-optical Kerr effect.

Conclusion: Nonlinear phononics provides a general route for ultrafast manipulation of spin-split antiferromagnetic phases beyond the reach of static strain, enabling picosecond-timescale reversible control of NRSS.

Abstract: Nonrelativistic spin splitting (NRSS) in collinear antiferromagnets offers a route to high-frequency spintronics immune to stray fields, but its dynamic control has remained elusive. We demonstrate, using density functional theory (DFT) and nonlinear phononics, that THz laser pulses can achieve ultrafast, reversible control of NRSS on picosecond timescales in antiferromagnets. We derive two symmetry criteria, accounting for phonon and magnetic wavevector compatibility and order-parameter parity, to identify which Raman-active phonon modes can activate or amplify NRSS. Applying these rules to NiO and LaFeO$_3$, we show that resonant driving of an infrared-active mode at 11.08 THz transiently converts spin-degenerate NiO into an NRSS state via biquadratic anharmonic coupling, generating a time-averaged spin splitting of $\sim$40 meV. In LaFeO$_3$, selective excitation amplifies the existing NRSS by about 100%. In both cases, the induced spin splitting is accompanied by a transient SOC-induced net moment detectable via the magneto-optical Kerr effect. This framework establishes nonlinear phononics as a general route for ultrafast manipulation of spin-split antiferromagnetic phases well beyond the reach of static strain.

</details>


### [39] [Correlated Plasmonic Excitation in Twisted Nematic Plasmonic Superlattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10122)
*Rui Huang,Jordan Austin-Frank Wilson,Anders Dollard,Nicholas C Fisher,Zixian Fang,John T. Fourkas,Zhiwei Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper reports correlated light-matter interactions in reconfigurable twisted plasmonic superlattices assembled from hybrid Fe3O4 and Au nanorods, where twist-angle-dependent plasmonic excitation emerges from the coupling between collective polarization of nematic phases and anisotropic plasmonic excitation of individual nanorods, enabling magnetically controlled photonic moire superlattices with tunable optical properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Twisted superlattices (e.g., moire lattices) exhibit unique electronic, magnetic, and optical properties due to distinct lattice symmetries and continuous phase transitions, presenting opportunities to study emerging physical phenomena. The authors aim to explore correlated light-matter interactions in such systems, specifically the interplay between collective polarization effects and individual plasmonic excitations.

Method: Hybrid Fe3O4 and Au nanorods are used as building blocks to assemble plasmonic nematic liquid crystals with unidirectional alignment, which are further assembled into moire plasmonic lattices via vertical stacking. Magnetic orientation control enables precise alignment and coding of nematic domains and plasmonic patterns in each sublattice.

Result: A twist-angle-dependent plasmonic excitation is observed in the twisted bilayer, with enhanced transverse and longitudinal plasmonic excitation at 0° and 90° twist angles, respectively. This correlated excitation arises from the coupling between the collective polarization effect of liquid crystal phases and the anisotropic plasmonic excitation of individual nanorods.

Conclusion: The correlated plasmonic excitation and light polarization create reconfigurable photonic moire superlattices with well-defined domain colors, feature sizes, periodicities, symmetries, and dimensions, all tunable by twist angles and displacements in the twisted plasmonic lattices.

Abstract: Superlattices with twisted configurations, such as moire lattices, have recently been extensively exploited for their unique electronic, magnetic, and optical properties. One remarkable feature of nanoscale twisted superlattices is the distinct lattice symmetries and the continuous phase transitions between periodic or aperiodic phases, representing a unique opportunity to study many emerging physical phenomena. Here, we report a correlated light and matter interaction between the collective polarization effect of nematic plasmonic superstructures and the plasmonic excitation of individual constituent nanorods in reconfigurable twisted plasmonic superlattices. Using hybrid Fe3O4 and Au nanorods as building blocks, we assembled plasmonic nematic liquid crystals with unidirectionally aligned nanorods, which could be further assembled into moire plasmonic lattices through a vertical stacking assembly method. A twist angle dependent plasmonic excitation is recognized in the twisted bilayer of two plasmonic superlattices, featuring enhanced transverse and longitudinal plasmonic excitation at a twisting angle of 0 degree and 90 degree, respectively. Such correlated plasmonic excitation in twisted plasmonic superstructures is induced by the correlation between the collective polarization effect of the liquid crystal phases and the anisotropic plasmonic excitation of individual nanorods. The magnetic orientation control allows for precise alignment of hybrid Fe3O4 and Au nanorods in polymer substrates and enables the coding of nematic domains and plasmonic patterns in each sublattice. The correlated plasmonic excitation and light polarization create reconfigurable photonic moire superlattices with well-defined domain colors, feature sizes, periodicities, symmetries, and dimensions determined by twist angles and displacements in the twisted plasmonic lattices.

</details>


### [40] [Chiral, Electronically Decoupled Layers of 1T'-WS2 Topological Insulator via Neutral-Molecule Intercalation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10457)
*Jiaze Xie,Fatmagül Katmer,Fang Yuan,Jaime M. Moya,Guangming Cheng,Connor J. Pollak,Xiaoyu Song,Nirmal Roy,Yakov Bloch,Moshe Ben Shalom,Jennifer Cano,Leslie M. Schoop*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: 2M-WS2 undergoes amine intercalation to produce electronically decoupled 1T' layers, switching between superconducting metal and insulating states, enabling monolayer-like topological insulator behavior and chiral superlattices in bulk crystals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monolayer 1T'-WS2 is predicted to be a 2D topological insulator, but its intrinsic properties are masked by strong interlayer coupling in bulk 2M-WS2, and mechanical exfoliation is hindered, preventing experimental study of monolayer properties.

Method: Simple wet-chemical amine intercalation into 2M-WS2, producing superlattices with neutral molecular spacers that preserve the 1T' intralayer structure while expanding interlayer spacing from 0.5 to 1-4 nm and reconstructing stacking.

Result: Controlled (de)intercalation reversibly switches the system between superconducting metal and insulator with an activation gap matching the isolated monolayer. DFT confirms nontrivial Z2 topology in decoupled layers. Chiral amine intercalation induces chiroptical activity in WS2 electronic transitions.

Conclusion: Challenges the view that group VIB dichalcogenides are inert toward neutral-molecule intercalation, and establishes molecular intercalation as a general chemical route to realize monolayer-like topological insulator physics and chiral van der Waals superlattices in bulk single crystals.

Abstract: Monolayer 1T'-WS2 is predicted to be a two-dimensional topological insulator, but its intrinsic electronic properties are masked by strong interlayer coupling in its metallic and superconducting bulk parent phase, 2M-WS2. Isolating monolayers by mechanical exfoliation is also hindered by this coupling, preventing experimental examination of monolayer properties. Here we show that 2M-WS2 undergoes amine intercalation through a simple wet-chemical reaction, yielding superlattices in which the 1T' layers are structurally preserved but electronically decoupled by neutral molecular spacers. Intercalation expands the interlayer spacing from 0.5 to 1-4 nm and reconstructs the stacking while preserving the intralayer 1T' framework. Controlled (de)intercalation reversibly switches the system between a superconducting metal and an insulator with an activation gap matching that of the isolated monolayer. Density functional theory indicates that the electronically decoupled layers retain the nontrivial Z2 topology of the monolayer. Chiral amine intercalation further induces chiroptical activity in WS2 electronic transitions. Overall, the successful intercalation challenges the long-held view that group VIB dichalcogenides are inert toward neutral-molecule intercalation and demonstrates molecular intercalation as a general chemical route for realizing monolayer-like topological-insulator physics and enabling chiral van der Waals superlattices in bulk single crystals.

</details>


### [41] [Chiral charge density waves in transition metal dichalcogenide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10182)
*Ettore Carpene*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The chirality in 1T-TiSe2's CDW phase is not due to a phase shift (which is energetically prohibitive), but rather a metastable monoclinic stacking order that breaks mirror/inversion symmetries. The CDW has dual nature (bond-modulated electronic charge + atom-centered lattice distortion), enabling light-induced chiral domain selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the debate over the microscopic origin of chirality observed in the CDW phase of 1T-TiSe2.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) and symmetry analysis.

Result: Phase-shifted chiral states are energetically prohibitive. Instead, a nearly degenerate metastable CDW phase with monoclinic symmetry (breaking mirror and inversion symmetries) is identified. The CDW has dual nature: bond-modulated electronic charge redistribution + atom-centered transverse lattice distortion, enabling a specific optical coupling mechanism.

Conclusion: "Spontaneous" chirality observed in local probes is a statistical distribution of CDW phase stacking domains. Light-induced gyrotropic order is achievable via circularly polarized light breaking energetic degeneracy between chiral domains.

Abstract: The emergence of chirality in the charge density wave (CDW) phase of 1$T$-TiSe$_2$ has recently attracted significant attention, yet its microscopic origin remains debated. The prevailing interpretation attributes chirality to a relative phase shift between the charge density components. Here, using density functional theory and symmetry analysis, it is demonstrated that such phase-shifted states entail prohibitive energy costs, rendering them unlikely candidates for the ground state.
