Instead, a nearly degenerate metastable CDW phase stacking order with monoclinic symmetry is identified, that naturally breaks mirror and inversion symmetries. The CDW in 1$T$-TiSe$_2$ possesses a dual physical nature: the electronic charge redistribution is intrinsically bond-modulated, while the periodic lattice distortion is atom-centered transverse. This spatial separation allows a specific optical coupling mechanism where circularly polarized light breaks the energetic degeneracy between chiral domains. These results reconcile conflicting experimental observations, identifying the "spontaneous" chirality observed in local probes as a statistical distribution of CDW phase stacking domains, while establishing the mechanism for macroscopic, light-induced gyrotropic order.

</details>


### [42] [Self-Consistent Phonon Spectral Functions and Thermal Transport Beyond the Quasiparticle Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10211)
*Yi Xia*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A self-consistent spectral function framework is introduced that represents phonons by their full frequency distribution, self-consistently determining self-energy real/imaginary parts without an artificial smearing parameter. Applied to HgTe, it broadens phonon spectra, activates new scattering channels, and reduces thermal conductivity by ~5× to match experiment, bridging quasiparticle perturbation theory and dynamical simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard first-principles phonon linewidth calculations assume phonon quasiparticles with sharply defined frequencies and rely on externally chosen smearing parameters for energy conservation, limiting accuracy in materials with strong anharmonicity.

Method: A self-consistent spectral function framework where each phonon is represented by its full frequency distribution, which is used to dress the internal lines of the three-phonon bubble self-energy. The real and imaginary parts of the self-energy are determined self-consistently on equal footing, eliminating the need for an external smearing parameter.

Result: Applied to zincblende HgTe: self-consistency substantially broadens acoustic and optical phonon spectra, activates previously inaccessible scattering channels, and reduces lattice thermal conductivity by approximately a factor of five to match experimental values with correct temperature dependence, without invoking higher-order interactions. Mode-resolved spectral functions were validated against molecular dynamics power spectra.

Conclusion: Self-consistent spectral functions serve as a practical bridge between quasiparticle perturbation theory and fully dynamical simulations, and the framework is readily generalizable to higher-order anharmonic processes.

Abstract: Anharmonic lattice dynamics shapes phonon spectra and thermal transport, yet first-principles linewidth calculations typically assume phonon quasiparticles with sharply defined frequencies. Here, we introduce a self-consistent spectral function framework that represents each phonon by its full frequency distribution and uses the resulting spectra to dress the internal lines of the three-phonon bubble self-energy. The method self-consistently determines the real and imaginary parts of the self-energy on equal footing, eliminates externally chosen smearing parameter for enforcing energy conservation, and extends lattice dynamics and thermal transport calculations beyond the quasiparticle approximation. Applied to zincblende HgTe, self-consistency substantially broadens acoustic and optical phonon spectra, activates scattering channels inaccessible in the one-shot quasiparticle calculation, and reduces the lattice thermal conductivity by approximately a factor of five to the experimental scale while reproducing its temperature dependence, without explicitly invoking higher-order interactions. Mode-resolved spectral functions are further validated against the power spectra from direct molecular dynamics simulations. These results establish self-consistent spectral functions as a practical bridge between quasiparticle perturbation theory and fully dynamical simulations, and provide a framework readily generalizable to higher-order anharmonic processes.

</details>


### [43] [Enabling temperature controlled in-situ vapor dosing for lab source X-ray reflectivity measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10339)
*Erin E. Dunphy,Mara A. Fischer,Sasha R. Neefe,Devin L. Shaffer,Anthony P. Straub,Michael F. Toney,J. Will Medlin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper presents a novel X-ray Reflectivity (XRR) transmission cell designed for in-house diffractometers, enabling in situ vapor dosing and temperature-dependent experiments. Demonstrated through water adsorption in polyamide membranes and temperature-induced restructuring of polystyrene brushes, the cell achieves 90-95% saturated vapor pressures and resolves Ångstrom-scale structural changes from 25-200°C, offering a streamlined, vacuum-sealed alternative to synchrotron-dependent setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To broaden the accessibility of XRR measurements for studying buried interfaces by developing a cell that works on in-house diffractometers rather than being limited to synchrotron facilities, enabling operando and in situ studies of thin films, membranes, and batteries in more laboratory settings.

Method: Design and construction of an XRR transmission cell with vapor dosing manifold and temperature control, validated through two case studies: (1) water adsorption into polyamide membranes on silicon under controlled vapor pressure (0-250 mbar, up to 90-95% saturation), and (2) temperature-dependent restructuring of polystyrene pseudo brushes on alumina (25-200°C).

Result: The vapor manifold achieved 90-95% of saturated vapor pressures; measurements spanned 0-250 mbar suitable for adsorption isotherms. Heating studies (25-200°C) resolved Ångstrom-scale structural changes in surface-bound polymers. The cell enables straightforward probe-molecule exchange, vacuum-sealed operation, and variable temperature control on a laboratory diffractometer.

Conclusion: The developed XRR transmission cell provides a novel, streamlined approach for temperature-controlled vapor dosing on laboratory diffractometers, making operando and in situ XRR measurements more accessible beyond synchrotron facilities, with capabilities for adsorption isotherms and thermal restructuring studies at high resolution.

Abstract: X-ray Reflectivity (XRR) is a valuable technique for probing buried interfaces in complex systems relevant to thin-film, membrane, and battery applications, among others. However, many operando and in situ reflectivity cells are designed for use at synchrotron facilities, limiting the broader accessibility of these measurements. We present an XRR transmission cell that enables in situ vapor dosing and temperature-dependent experiments on in-house diffractometers. We demonstrate its capabilities with two case studies: the adsorption of water into polyamide (PA) membranes on silicon and temperature-dependent restructuring of polystyrene (PS) pseudo brushes on alumina. Vapor dosing allows for controlled release of vapor into the cell, allowing operation across a wide range of conditions from rough vacuum to saturation. We demonstrate that the manifold can reach 90-95% of saturated pressures, with the measurements presented here spanning 0-250 mbar, which is desirable for adsorption isotherms. Heating studies performed between 25 and 200C demonstrate the ability to resolve Ångstrom scale structural changes in a surface bound polymer. These results establish a novel streamlined approach to temperature controlled vapor dosing on a laboratory diffractometer, offering straightforward probe-molecule exchange, vacuum-sealed operations, and variable temperature capabilities.

</details>


### [44] [Capturing the calendering U-shape in lithium-ion electrode thermal conductivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11521)
*Julius Störk*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper develops a calendering-aware thermal conductivity model for lithium-ion electrodes that captures the non-monotonic U-shaped behavior observed in graphite anodes (initial decrease then recovery) and the monotonic response in NMC cathodes, reducing error from 31.1% to 4.5% across 27 calendering states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calendering affects electrode thermal conductivity non-monotonically (especially in graphite anodes), but conventional effective-medium models cannot reproduce this U-shaped behavior, motivating the need for a process-aware thermal transport model.

Method: The authors extend the Zehner-Bauer-Schlünder model by combining a Knudsen-corrected porous-medium baseline with a compression-indexed contact contribution. For graphite electrodes, the model accounts for competing effects of increased particle contact and reorientation-induced reduction of through-plane heat pathways. For isotropic NMC cathodes, it captures process-dependent contact-network evolution.

Result: Across 27 calendering states (thin/thick graphite anodes, NMC622 and NMC811 cathodes), the model reduces mean absolute percentage error from 31.1% (zero-fit reference) to 4.5%.

Conclusion: Incorporating process-dependent microstructural evolution is essential to capture the measured conductivity minimum; further validation across additional formulations, thicknesses, and chemistries is needed to assess transferability.

Abstract: Calendering is a key manufacturing step in lithium-ion electrode production, increasing volumetric energy density by reducing electrode porosity. Its effect on through-plane effective thermal conductivity, however, can be non-monotonic: measurements of graphite-based anodes show an initial decrease in thermal conductivity during early calendering followed by recovery at higher compaction. Conventional porosity-based effective-medium closures cannot reproduce this U-shaped behaviour. We develop a calendering-aware extension of the Zehner--Bauer--Schlünder model that combines a Knudsen-corrected porous-medium baseline with a compression-indexed contact contribution. For graphite electrodes, the model represents the competing effects of increasing particle contact and calendering-induced reorientation of anisotropic graphite particles, which initially reduces favourable through-plane heat-transport pathways. For quasi-isotropic NMC cathodes, the observed response is instead captured through process-dependent contact-network evolution. Across 27 calendering states spanning thin and thick graphite anodes and NMC622 and NMC811 cathodes, the proposed closure reduces the mean absolute percentage error from 31.1% for the zero-fit reference model to 4.5%. The result shows that incorporating process-dependent microstructural evolution is necessary to capture the measured conductivity minimum. Validation across additional electrode formulations, thicknesses, and chemistries remains necessary to assess transferability.

</details>


### [45] [Oxygen-nonstoichiometry-driven phase transition in $\mathrm{Sr}_{1-x}\mathrm{Nd}_{x}\mathrm{CoO}_{3-δ}$ ($x = 0.1, 0.2, 0.3$) perovskites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10346)
*Nina Tereshko,Roman Lanovsky,Olivier Toulemonde,Maxim Bushinsky,Stanislav Savvin,Vadim Sikolenko,Lingyan Xu,Aleksandr Nikitin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper studies how oxygen content affects the structure, magnetism, and electrical transport in Nd-doped SrCoO3-δ. Increasing oxygen induces a structural transition from layered I4/mmm to orthorhombic Pmmm, accompanied by a crossover from weak ferromagnetic G-type antiferromagnetism to ferromagnetism and a sharp resistivity drop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically investigate the interplay between oxygen nonstoichiometry, crystal structure, and magnetic/electrotransport properties in Sr1-xNdxCoO3-δ, and to clarify whether the layered "314" structure is driven by oxygen vacancy ordering or A-site cation ordering.

Method: High-resolution neutron powder diffraction combined with synchrotron x-ray powder diffraction on Sr1-xNdxCoO3-δ (x = 0.1, 0.2, 0.3) compositions.

Result: Increasing oxygen content drives a structural transition from layered I4/mmm (2ap × 2ap × 4ap) to oxygen-deficient orthorhombic Pmmm (ap × ap × 2ap), accompanied by a crossover from G-type antiferromagnetism with a weak ferromagnetic component to a ferromagnetic state, and a drastic resistivity decrease.

Conclusion: The layered "314" structure is stabilized by oxygen vacancy ordering, not A-site cation ordering. The oxidized compounds represent an intermediate orthorhombic state preceding fully stoichiometric phases. Property evolution is explained by Co spin state changes, enhanced Co 3d-O 2p orbital overlap upon oxygenation, and a magnetically inhomogeneous ferromagnetic state with residual vacancies and mixed Co3+/Co4+ valence.

Abstract: We report a systematic study of the interplay between oxygen nonstoichiometry, crystal structure, and magnetic/electrotransport properties in $\mathrm{Sr}_{1-x}\mathrm{Nd}_{x}\mathrm{CoO}_{3-δ}$ ($x = 0.1, 0.2, 0.3$). High-resolution neutron powder diffraction combined with synchrotron x-ray powder diffraction reveals that increasing the oxygen content induces a structural transition from a layered $I4/mmm$ ($2a_p \times 2a_p \times 4a_p$) to an oxygen-deficient orthorhombic $Pmmm$ ($a_p \times a_p \times 2a_p$) phases with preferential oxygen-vacancy occupation. This transition is accompanied by a crossover from G-type antiferromagnetic with a weak ferromagnetic component to a ferromagnetic state, and a drastic decay in resistivity. The evolution of the magnetic and transport properties is discussed in terms of changes in the Co spin state, enhanced Co $3d$ - O $2p$ orbital overlap upon oxygen uptake, and a magnetically inhomogeneous ferromagnetic state associated with residual oxygen vacancies and mixed $\mathrm{Co}^{3+}/\mathrm{Co}^{4+}$ valence. Our findings experimentally confirm that the stabilization of the layered "314" structure is driven by the presence and ordering of oxygen vacancies rather than A-site cation ordering, whereas the oxygen-deficient oxidized compounds represent an intermediate orthorhombic state preceding fully stoichiometric phases.

</details>


### [46] [Anomalous Compressibility and Electronic Robustness of Metallic Delafossite PdCoO$_2$ under Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10361)
*Cheng Peng,Pahuni Jain,Tyler Harper,Chris Leighton,Weiwei Xie*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: PdCoO₂ exhibits anomalous anisotropic compression under pressure (a-axis contracts more than c-axis, opposite to typical layered materials), yet retains its rhombohedral structure, rigid bonding framework, and ultrahigh metallic conductivity due to robust bonding that preserves the Pd-derived nearly-free-electron band.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the exceptional ultrahigh conductivity of PdCoO₂ responds to compression and to resolve the apparent conflict between its layered structure and anomalous anisotropic compressibility.

Method: High-pressure single-crystal X-ray diffraction up to 10 GPa combined with first-principles calculations, including Crystal Orbital Hamilton Population (COHP) analysis and Boltzmann transport calculations.

Result: No structural phase transition up to 10 GPa; anisotropic compression with a-axis contracting more than c-axis; minimal changes in Pd-O and Co-O local coordination; no destabilizing Pd antibonding states emerge; Pd metallic band and quasi-2D Fermi surface essentially unchanged; in-plane conductivity nearly pressure-independent; out-of-plane conductivity decreases modestly, increasing transport anisotropy slightly.

Conclusion: The anomalous compressibility of PdCoO₂ originates from its robust chemical bonding network, which preserves both the crystal structure and the highly conductive Pd-derived metallic state under pressure.

Abstract: The layered delafossite PdCoO$_2$ is an exceptional oxide metal whose ultrahigh conductivity arises from a Pd-derived nearly-free-electron band. Here, high-pressure single-crystal X-ray diffraction combined with first-principles calculations is used to investigate its structural, bonding, and electronic evolution up to 10 GPa. PdCoO$_2$ retains the rhombohedral R-3m structure throughout the investigated pressure range, with no structural phase transition. The lattice exhibits an unusual anisotropic compression, with the in-plane a-axis contracting more strongly than the stacking c-axis, opposite to the behavior of most layered materials. Despite this anomalous compressibility, only minor changes are observed in the local Pd-O and Co-O coordination environments, indicating a remarkably rigid bonding framework. Crystal orbital Hamilton population analysis reveals only subtle strengthening of the existing bonding interactions, without the emergence of destabilizing Pd-related antibonding states. Consistent with these findings, the Pd-derived metallic band and quasi-two-dimensional Fermi surface remain essentially unchanged under compression. Boltzmann transport calculations further show that the in-plane conductivity is nearly pressure-independent, whereas the out-of-plane conductivity decreases modestly, resulting in a slight increase in transport anisotropy. These results demonstrate that the anomalous compressibility of PdCoO$_2$ originates from its robust chemical bonding network, which preserves both the crystal structure and the highly conductive Pd-derived metallic state under pressure.

</details>


### [47] [Why Do Thick MOCVD-Grown beta-Ga2O3 Epilayers on (001) Substrates Crack: Crystallographic Origin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10378)
*Martin Frentrup,Indraneel Sanyal,Dan Lamb,Ciaran P. Llewelyn,Jonathan Evans,Owen J. Guy,Mike Jennings,Saptarsi Ghosh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study investigates MOCVD growth of thick beta-Ga2O3 epilayers on (001) substrates, revealing that epilayers adopt a (-401)-oriented structure with tensile strain (~4.1%) from lattice mismatch driving crack formation above ~1.8 μm thickness, attributed to surface faceting during annealing under oxygen-rich conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Thick, defect-free epitaxial layers grown by industry-standard techniques are essential for adopting fully vertical power devices based on ultra wide bandgap Ga2O3, but MOCVD growth on large-diameter (001) substrates is underexplored and the origins of surface roughening and cracking are not fully understood.

Method: Systematic MOCVD growth of beta-Ga2O3 epilayers at ~3.5 μm/h with thicknesses from 0.3 to 3.5 μm on (001) substrates; characterized by surface morphology analysis, high-resolution X-ray diffraction, and rocking curve analysis to assess structural evolution.

Result: Epilayers show smooth but striated surfaces with nanoscale roughness increasing beyond coalescence; cracks form at ~1.8 μm thickness. Despite (001) substrates, epilayers are predominantly (-401)-oriented from early growth. In-plane twist exceeds tilt, both decreasing with thickness. Lattice mismatch along epilayer [104] direction imposes ~4.1% tensile strain, driving perpendicular crack formation.

Conclusion: The observed epitaxial relationship likely results from faceted reconstruction of the (001) substrate during annealing, driven by surface energy minimization under oxygen-rich MOCVD conditions, explaining the cracking and roughening behavior.

Abstract: Thick, defect free epitaxial layers grown using industry standard techniques are a fundamental requirement for the widespread adoption of fully vertical power devices based on ultra wide bandgap gallium oxide (Ga2O3). However, metal-organic chemical vapour deposition (MOCVD) of such layers on native beta-Ga2O3 substrates with the largest diameter (001) orientation remains relatively unexplored, and the origins of the reported surface roughening and cracking with increasing thickness are not yet fully understood. To address this, we report a systematic study of MOCVD grown beta-Ga2O3 epilayers deposited at growth rates of ~3.5 um/h, with thicknesses from 0.3 to 3.5 um. The epilayers exhibit a relatively smooth but striated surface morphology, with progressively increasing nanometre-scale roughness beyond coalescence and crack formation observed from ~1.8 um thickness. High resolution X ray diffraction reveals that, despite growth on (001) substrates, the epilayers adopt a predominantly (-401)-oriented structure from the earliest stages of growth. Rocking curve analysis further indicates a higher degree of in-plane twist than tilt, both decreasing with increasing epilayer thickness. While the epilayer and substrate are lattice-matched along the [010] in plane direction, the epitaxial alignment in the orthogonal epilayer [104] in plane direction imposes, in theory, a maximum tensile in-plane strain of approximately +4.1% arising from the underlying lattice mismatch, thereby driving crack formation perpendicular to this direction. Our results suggest that this epitaxial relationship is likely associated with faceted reconstruction of the (001) substrate surface during annealing, driven by the minimisation of surface energy under oxygen-rich MOCVD growth conditions.

</details>


### [48] [The evolution of AI from image interpretation toward scientific inference in nanoparticle electron microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10388)
*Evropi Toulkeridou,Jiafei Li,Leonardo Lari,Panagiotis Grammatikopoulos*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review examines how AI, ML, and DL methods are transforming electron microscopy for nanoparticle characterization, covering techniques from conventional ML to foundation models across TEM, HRTEM, STEM, and in situ TEM, along with integration with simulations and autonomous experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing complexity and scale of nanoparticle characterization data from advanced electron microscopy techniques necessitates AI-driven approaches to move beyond descriptive imaging toward quantitative, data-driven structural interpretation, dynamic analysis, and scientific inference.

Method: The review systematically categorizes AI methodologies applied to nanoparticle electron microscopy challenges (detection, segmentation, morphology quantification, atomic-resolution restoration, defect identification, 2D-to-3D inference, and dynamic analysis), spanning conventional ML, CNNs, transformers, self-supervised learning, foundation models, multimodal AI, and physics-informed learning.

Result: The review provides a critical assessment of advantages, limitations, benchmarking, and data requirements of current AI methodologies, and discusses integration pathways with simulations, metadata, closed-loop experimentation, and autonomous discovery.

Conclusion: AI, particularly emerging foundation models and autonomous experimentation frameworks, is positioned as a key enabler for next-generation nanoparticle characterization and accelerated materials discovery when integrated across computer vision, materials informatics, and electron microscopy.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is transforming electron microscopy by enabling quantitative analysis of increasingly large and complex datasets for nanoparticle characterization. Recent advances in machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL) have expanded microscopy from a descriptive imaging technique into a data-driven platform for structural interpretation, dynamic analysis, and scientific inference. This review examines AI methodologies for nanoparticle electron microscopy, focusing on transmission electron microscopy (TEM), high-resolution transmission electron microscopy (HRTEM), scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM), and in situ TEM. The discussion is organized around the principal challenges in nanoparticle characterization, including particle detection, segmentation, morphology quantification, atomic-resolution restoration, defect identification, two-dimensional-to-three-dimensional structural inference, and analysis of dynamic processes in situ. We review computational approaches from conventional ML and convolutional neural networks to transformer architectures, self-supervised learning, foundation models, multimodal AI, and physics-informed learning. We further discuss integrating microscopy data with simulations, metadata, and autonomous experimentation to relate nanoparticle structure, dynamics, synthesis conditions, and functional properties. The advantages, limitations, benchmarking, and data requirements of current methodologies are critically assessed. Finally, emerging opportunities for foundation models, AI-guided microscopy, closed-loop experimentation, and autonomous materials discovery are discussed. By integrating advances across computer vision, materials informatics, and electron microscopy, this review highlights the role of AI in next-generation nanoparticle characterization and accelerated materials discovery.

</details>


### [49] [Connecting Diffuse Scattering to Atomic-Site-Resolved Occupancy and Displacement Fields through Fourier Filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10440)
*Maksim Eremenko,Victor Krayzman,Matthew G. Tucker,Igor Levin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: MOSAIC is a computational framework that uses Fourier filtering of phase-bearing scattering amplitudes to identify atomic motifs responsible for diffuse scattering features in large-scale structural models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local structural correlations are encoded in diffuse scattering, but identifying the specific atomic motifs that produce particular diffuse scattering features is challenging.

Method: The approach applies a Fourier filter to phase-bearing scattering amplitudes over selected reciprocal-space regions to produce maps of atomic displacements and site occupancies responsible for those scattering features.

Result: The method effectively interrogates the nature and spatial distributions of interatomic correlations in large-scale structural models.

Conclusion: MOSAIC is effective for analyzing structural models from Reverse Monte Carlo, molecular dynamics, Monte Carlo simulations, or 2D projections from atomic-resolution electron microscopy.

Abstract: Local structural correlations are encoded in diffuse scattering, but identifying atomic motifs that produce specific diffuse features can be challenging. We introduce MOSAIC, a computational framework for this task when an atomistic configuration is available and a phase-bearing scattering amplitude can be calculated. Our approach relies on applying the Fourier filter to this amplitude over the reciprocal-space regions encompassing the scattering features of interest to obtain maps of atomic displacements and site occupancies responsible for those features. The method is effective in interrogating the nature and spatial distributions of interatomic correlations in large-scale structural models, such as obtained using Reverse Monte Carlo refinements from experimental data, molecular dynamics, or Monte Carlo simulations, or 2D structural projections derived from atomic-resolution electron microscopy images.

</details>


### [50] [Surface Variables Description of Axion Topological Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10509)
*Francisco J. Solis,Hugo García-Compeán*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops surface variable integral equations and numerical methods (boundary finite elements) to study the magnetoelectric response of axion topological insulators under static electric and magnetic fields, providing exact solutions for axisymmetric cases and numerical results for spheres, hollow spheres, and toroids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the macroscopic quasi-static magnetoelectric response of topological insulators, which are described by axion electrodynamics, and to develop a general framework for handling heterogeneous systems with multiple homogeneous materials.

Method: Surface variable techniques adapted from soft condensed matter physics, integral equation formulation for effective surface charge and current densities, exact analytic solutions for axial-symmetric cases, boundary finite element method for general geometries, and a variational principle for recovering surface equations.

Result: Exact analytic solutions for axial-symmetric topological insulator configurations; numerical solutions via boundary finite element method for spheres, hollow spheres, and toroidal surfaces.

Conclusion: The surface variable approach and boundary finite element method provide an effective framework for computing the magnetoelectric response of topological insulators of arbitrary geometry, with a variational principle underlying the surface equations.

Abstract: We study the response of axion topological materials under the presence of external static electric and magnetic fields. We focus on the macroscopic quasi-static magnetoelectric response of topological insulators. We use techniques based on surface variables that have been previously employed in soft condensed matter problems for the description of heterogeneous systems composed of multiple homogeneous materials. A complete description of the whole system is written in terms of surface degrees of freedom, which in this case correspond to effective surface charge and surface current densities. We obtain a set of integral equations satisfied by these variables. We present exact analytic solutions for axial-symmetric cases. We develop a numerical method for the solution of the integral equations by means of a boundary finite element method. We apply the numerical method to topological insulators with different geometries such as spheres, hollow spheres and toroidal surfaces. We show that that a variational principle can be used to recover the surface variables equations.

</details>


### [51] [Anisotropic hot carrier relaxation mediated by electron phonon scattering in TiN thin films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11237)
*Hemant Verma,Tzu-Yu Peng,Shyr-Shyan Yeh,Sheng-Chieh Huang,Pritam Sardar,Yang-Hao Chan,Yu-Jung Lu,Chao-Cheng Kaun*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study demonstrates that crystallographic orientation ([100], [110], [111]) significantly affects electron-phonon relaxation dynamics in TiN thin films, with [111] orientation showing the slowest scattering (15.96 fs) and cooling (110 fs) times, offering a practical route to tune energy relaxation in refractory plasmonic materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how crystallographic orientation influences ultrafast electron-phonon mediated energy relaxation in transition-metal nitride thin films, specifically TiN, to enable orientation-based tuning of energy relaxation for plasmonic and energy-conversion applications.

Method: First-principles calculations combined with maximally localized Wannier functions to evaluate e-ph quasiparticle scattering lifetimes, plus ultrafast pump-probe transient absorption spectroscopy on quasi-epitaxial, orientation-controlled TiN thin films grown on MgO substrates.

Result: TiN [111] orientation exhibits longer e-ph scattering lifetime (15.96 fs) than [100] (13.69 fs) and [110] (11.12 fs); similarly, [111] films show slower cooling time (110 fs) versus [100] (90 fs) and [110] (80 fs), with consistent trends between single-event scattering and collective cooling.

Conclusion: Crystallographic orientation is a practical and powerful means to tune e-ph-governed relaxation in TiN thin films, providing essential design guidelines for refractory plasmonic and energy-conversion platforms.

Abstract: Crystal orientations can shape the ultrafast energy relaxations of transition-metal nitride thin films. Here, we investigate the orientation-dependent electron-phonon (e-ph) mediated relaxation in titanium nitride (TiN) thin films along the [100], [110], and [111] directions by combining first-principles calculations with ultrafast pump-probe transient absorption spectroscopy. Using maximally localized Wannier functions, we evaluate e-ph quasiparticle scattering lifetimes near the Fermi level and identify a clear anisotropy: The TiN [111] orientation exhibits a longer e-ph scattering lifetime (15.96 fs) than [100] (13.69 fs) and [110] (11.12 fs), indicating reduced intrinsic e-ph scattering strength. Furthermore, we grew quasi-epitaxial, orientation-controlled TiN thin films on MgO substrates. Pump-probe measurements reveals that the population-level relaxation (hot-electron cooling) time also depends on orientations, with [111] films showing a significantly slower decay (110 fs) than [100] (90 fs) and [110] (80 fs). We emphasize that the calculated few-femtosecond scattering lifetimes and the measured few-hundred-femtosecond cooling time respectively represent single-event scattering and collective cooling, yet they exhibit consistent trends. These results demonstrate that crystallographic orientation provides a practical and powerful route to tune e-ph-governed relaxation in TiN thin films, offering essential design guidelines for refractory plasmonic and energy-conversion platforms.

</details>


### [52] [Nonlinear Tellegen limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10584)
*Abhinava Chatterjee,Nikhil Kalyanapuram*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper identifies a field-tunable nonlinear Tellegen limit for magnetoelectric media, deriving from nonlinear magnetoelectric coupling, which bounds Faraday rotation and induces electromagnetic instability at critical fields determined by magnetic point group symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the concept of the Tellegen limit (fundamental electromagnetic stability bound) from linear to nonlinear magnetoelectric media, and to explore field-tunable electromagnetic instabilities.

Method: Theoretical analysis of nonlinear magnetoelectric coupling; demonstration in two material systems: a d-wave altermagnet and an M-type hexagonal ferrite, with phase diagrams based on magnetic point group symmetry.

Result: A new field-tunable nonlinear Tellegen limit is identified; Faraday rotation grows linearly with applied field up to a universal maximum; electromagnetic instability occurs at a symmetry-determined critical field.

Conclusion: Nonlinear magnetoelectric coupling enables field-controlled electromagnetic stability, with the magnetic point group symmetry dictating both the tensor structure and the instability threshold.

Abstract: The Tellegen limit is the fundamental electromagnetic stability bound on magnetoelectric media. We show that nonlinear magnetoelectric coupling gives rise to a new Tellegen limit, which we term the nonlinear Tellegen limit. Unlike the linear Tellegen limit, which is fixed by material parameters, the nonlinear Tellegen limit is field-tunable -- a static electric or magnetic field drives the system toward electromagnetic instability at a material-specific critical field determined by the magnetic point group symmetry. The approach to this limit is accompanied by a field-tunable Faraday rotation that grows linearly with the applied field and is bounded from above by a universal maximum set by the nonlinear Tellegen limit -- beyond which the medium becomes electromagnetically unstable. We demonstrate the nonlinear Tellegen limit and the field-space stability phase diagram in two magnetically ordered material systems -- a d-wave altermagnet and an M-type hexagonal ferrite -- showing that the symmetry of the magnetic point group governs both the structure of the nonlinear magnetoelectric tensor and the resulting electromagnetic instability.

</details>


### [53] [Spinel Ferrite-Based Materials for Electrochemical Applications: Synthesis, Applications, and Future Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10823)
*Sakhi Tiwari,Ashish Dubey,Pragyanand Prajapati,Akhilesh Kumar Singh,Ashutosh Kumar,Jai Singh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review summarizes the latest progress in spinel ferrite (MFe2O4) based materials for electrochemical applications, focusing on their tunable properties via morphology control, doping, and compositing for gas sensors and supercapacitors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spinel ferrites exhibit versatile electrochemical properties but lack a consolidated review on their design strategies specifically for gas sensing and supercapacitor applications in the context of sustainability.

Method: The paper is a comprehensive review covering synthesis, morphology control, carbon-based composite incorporation, compositional substitution, doping, and nanostructuring techniques to enhance spinel ferrite electrode performance.

Result: Spinel ferrites show excellent electrochemical behavior due to multiple oxidation states, high surface area, and chemical stability; their performance is effectively tunable via nanostructuring and compositional engineering.

Conclusion: This review serves as a resource for researchers working on spinel ferrite-based electrodes for gas sensors and supercapacitors, two key applications for sustainable development.

Abstract: Spinel ferrites have shown wide range of applications in various fields, including supercapacitors, Li-ion batteries, water splitting, chemical sensors, catalytic activity, high-frequency magnetic devices, and biomedical, hyperthermia, drug delivery applications., etc. This review focuses on the latest progress and trends on design of spinel ferrites (MFe2O4; M = Divalent transition metal ion) based materials for electrochemical applications. Spinel ferrites exhibit good chemical stability, high surface area and excellent electrochemical behaviour with their multiple oxidation states, making them suitable for the detection of a wide range of gaseous analytes including volatile organic compounds, heavy metal ions, biomolecules, and environmental pollutants. It also makes spinel ferrites a great choice for efficient energy storage and utility in supercapacitors. The electrochemical performance of spinel ferrite-based electrode materials can be effectively tuned via morphology control, incorporation of carbon-based materials, compositional substitution, doping and forming composition systems, especially in nanostructured form. This review will serve as a comprehensive resource for researchers interested in the synthesis and various enhancement techniques for electrode material composition of electrochemical device applications specifically, gas sensors and supercapacitors, which are two of the highest emerging functional applications for the new sustainability directed world, utilizing these advanced materials.

</details>


### [54] [Strain-controlled crystalline--amorphous transition and flat-band tuning in buckled silicon kagome](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11790)
*Chenhaoyue Wang,Amartya S. Banerjee*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Proposes a buckled silicon kagome lattice (SiKL)—an unfunctionalized 2D monolayer with a flat band near the Fermi level—showing that biaxial strain controls lattice dynamics, electronic dispersion, and finite-temperature metastability, enabling strain-tuned flat-band physics in an elemental material.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To realize electronic flat bands in an elemental two-dimensional material where strong electron interactions can compete with suppressed kinetic energy, avoiding the complexity of passivated or hybrid-lattice silicon kagome proposals.

Method: DFT-based phonon analysis, ab initio molecular dynamics (AIMD) at 315 K, and classical MD simulations of large (36×36) sheets; strain-dependent band structure and phonon calculations; exploratory Ag(111) substrate modeling.

Result: Two nearly degenerate buckled forms exist; the high-buckling form retains a partially flat kagome-derived band. Biaxial tension narrows the bandwidth, hardens soft phonons, and raises the density-of-states peak toward the Fermi level. Strain controls a crystalline–amorphous transition with first-order-like collapse at higher strains (Tc ~600 K at 10% strain). Epitaxial mismatch on Ag(111) could supply comparable tension.

Conclusion: Buckled SiKL is a candidate platform for strain-controlled flat-band and electronic correlation physics, offering elemental composition and strain-only tunability of thermal metastability, bond rearrangement, and near-Fermi flat-band properties.

Abstract: Electronic flat bands in an elemental two-dimensional material provide an attractive setting for electron interactions competing with suppressed kinetic energy. Here we propose a buckled silicon kagome lattice (SiKL), an unfunctionalized six-atom monolayer of bond-linked Si$_3$ triangles and dodecagonal pores. Its planar parent hosts a dispersionless Kohn--Sham band near the Fermi level but is unstable to out-of-plane distortions. Following three soft zone-centre phonons and relaxing displaced structures yields two nearly degenerate buckled forms. The high-buckling form retains a partially flat kagome-derived band near the Fermi level. Biaxial tension controls lattice dynamics and electronic dispersion: at 10% strain, the bandwidth decreases significantly, the density-of-states peak approaches the Fermi level, and the softest phonon hardens. At 315 K, $6\times6$ ab initio MD shows the unstrained network disordering while the strained network remains ordered, indicating finite-temperature metastability. Fifty-nanosecond classical MD of $36\times36$ sheets reveals a strain-controlled crystalline--amorphous transition and local-bonding crossover near 2% strain. Low-strain trajectories show gradual, two-stage disordering; higher strains undergo an abrupt, first-order-like collapse, with the transition temperature reaching approximately 600 K at 10% strain. An exploratory Ag(111) substrate model suggests epitaxial mismatch could supply comparable tension, retain a narrow SiKL band, and preserve crystalline order above room temperature. Unlike passivated or hybrid-lattice silicon kagome proposals aimed mainly at conventional semiconductors, SiKL is elemental and uses strain alone to couple thermal metastability, bond rearrangement, and near-Fermi flat-band tuning. Buckled SiKL is a candidate platform for strain-controlled flat-band and electronic correlation physics.

</details>


### [55] [A Cluster-Based Model of the Spectrum of Erbium-Doped GdVO$_4$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10924)
*Zachary H. Roberts,Masaya Hiraishi,Luke S. Trainor,Jevon J. Longdell*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A cluster-based model for Er:GdVO$_4$ describes rare-earth ion coupling to nearest neighbor gadolinium ions and a mean field, using fewer and more physically meaningful parameters than previous models, and successfully reproduces the experimental optical spectrum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To better understand and predict the enriched optical spectrum observed when rare-earth ions are doped into antiferromagnetic crystals, specifically Er:GdVO$_4$, with a simpler and more physically grounded model.

Method: A cluster-based model where the erbium ion couples directly to its four nearest neighbor gadolinium ions, which then couple to the mean field of the rest of the crystal lattice.

Result: The model uses fewer parameters with clearer physical origins compared to previous models, and achieves good agreement with the experimental optical spectrum of Er:GdVO$_4$.

Conclusion: The model captures the most important interactions in the system and may be useful for predicting microwave-to-optical transduction in future experiments.

Abstract: Experimental observations of rare-earth ions doped into an antiferromagnetic crystal show an enriched optical spectrum. In this paper we present a cluster-based model to describe erbium ions doped into a gadolinium vanadate (Er:GdVO$_4$) host crystal, wherein the erbium ion couples directly to its four nearest neighbour gadolinium ions, which in turn couple to the mean field of the rest of the crystal. Compared to previous models in the literature, the parameters used to fit this model are fewer in number, with clearer physical origins. Agreement with the experimentally observed optical spectrum of Er:GdVO$_4$ suggests that our model succeeds in capturing the most important interactions of the system, suggesting that it may be useful for predicting microwave-to-optical transduction in future experiments.

</details>


### [56] [Measurement of Kerr rotation using a variable-angle polarizer method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11002)
*Shusmita Podder Pooza,Hailey Cossey,Dipanjan Mazumdar*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors develop an accurate method to measure the magneto-optical Kerr effect (MOKE) by fitting analytical expressions of orthogonal light components to intensity and MOKE signal measured at multiple polarizer angles on a cobalt thin film, yielding precise Kerr rotations (0.46 mrad for p-polarized, 0.65 mrad for s-polarized) that eliminate uncertainties inherent in single-point measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate measurement of the Kerr rotation in MOKE remains challenging due to its small magnitude and complex dependence on optical parameters; conventional single-point measurements near the null configuration suffer from inherent uncertainties.

Method: Longitudinal MOKE measurements were performed on p- and s-polarized laser light at various polarizer angles on a cobalt thin film. The orthogonal components of reflected polarized light were measured using a Wollaston prism. Analytical expressions were fitted to the average intensity and MOKE signal across different polarizer angles to extract Kerr rotations.

Result: p-Kerr rotation = 0.46 milliradians; s-Kerr rotation = 0.65 milliradians (633 nm laser, 45° incidence angle). These values agree well with estimated values from literature data for cobalt.

Conclusion: The multi-angle fitting method provides highly accurate Kerr rotation measurements and eliminates inherent uncertainties of single-point measurements.

Abstract: The magneto-optical Kerr effect (MOKE) occurs when polarized light reflects from a magnetized surface, causing a small change in the polarization angle and state. Measurement of the rotation in the angle (Kerr rotation) is well established and typically performed close to the null configuration in a polarizer-analyzer geometry. However, accurate measurement always remains a challenge and as the effect depends intricately on several optical parameters. Here we performed a series of longitudinal magneto-optical Kerr effect (MOKE) measurements on $p$ and $s$ polarized laser light at various polarizer angles on a Cobalt thin film and measured the orthogonal components of the reflected polarized light using a Wollaston prism. Analytical expressions for the orthogonal light components were fitted to the average intensity and the MOKE signal measured at different polarizer angles to obtain the Kerr roations. Our analysis yielded a $p (s)$-Kerr rotation of 0.46 (0.65) milliradians for a 633~nm laser at 45$^\circ$ angle of incidence, which agrees very well with our estimated value for Co using available literature data. Apart from being very accurate, the advantage of the process is that it eliminates the inherent uncertainties in single-point measurements of the Kerr rotation.

</details>


### [57] [Neural-network-based reconstruction of spin and orbital angular momentum from X-ray magnetic circular dichroism spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11058)
*Tetsuro Ueno*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a neural-network-based inverse problem approach to reconstruct spin and orbital angular momentum directly from full X-ray magnetic circular dichroism (XMCD) spectral line shapes, overcoming limitations of conventional sum-rule analyses that rely on integrated spectral intensities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional XMCD analysis based on sum rules relies on integrated spectral intensities and becomes insufficient when multiple parameters (e.g., crystal-field splitting, spin-orbit coupling, exchange field) influence the spectral line shape, necessitating a more comprehensive approach.

Method: Formulate XMCD analysis as an inverse problem, train a neural network on many-body multiplet calculations of Fe, Co, and Ni L₂,₃-edge XAS and XMCD spectra with systematically varied key parameters (crystal-field splitting, spin-orbit coupling, exchange field), and map spectral line shapes to expectation values ⟨S_z⟩ and ⟨L_z⟩.

Result: The neural network achieves accurate and unbiased reconstruction of spin and orbital angular momentum from full spectral line shapes, validated on strictly test-only data, establishing a proof of concept for data-driven inverse reconstruction from XAS and XMCD spectra.

Conclusion: Exploiting full XAS and XMCD line shapes provides access to information beyond conventional sum-rule analyses while remaining consistent with established theoretical frameworks.

Abstract: X-ray magnetic circular dichroism (XMCD) is a powerful probe of element-specific spin and orbital angular momentum. Conventional analyses based on sum rules, however, rely on integrated spectral intensities and can become insufficient when multiple parameters influence the spectral line shape. Here, we formulate XMCD analysis as an inverse problem and develop a neural-network (NN) based approach to reconstruct spin and orbital angular momentum directly from full spectral line shapes. Using many-body multiplet calculations of Fe, Co, and Ni $L_{2,3}$-edge X-ray absorption spectra (XAS) and XMCD spectra as a physically well-defined training dataset, we systematically vary key parameters including crystal-field splitting, spin--orbit coupling, and exchange field. The NN is trained to map spectral line shapes onto the expectation values of spin and orbital angular momentm $\langle S_z \rangle$ and $\langle L_z \rangle$, and validated using strictly test-only data. The results demonstrate accurate and unbiased reconstruction, establishing a proof of concept for data-driven inverse reconstruction from XAS and XMCD spectra. These findings show that exploiting the full XAS and XMCD line shapes provide access to information beyond conventional sum-rule analyses while remaining consistent with established theoretical frameworks.

</details>


### [58] [Effect of annealing temperature on the structure and properties of co-sputtered Fe-Mn-Sn films near 2:1:1 ratio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11077)
*Lance Griswold,Dipanjan Mazumdar*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates the thin-film synthesis of Fe₂MnSn (a Kagome magnet with high Curie temperature and magnetic anisotropy) in the Fe-Mn-Sn system near the 2:1:1 ratio, showing that phase-pure Fe₂MnSn only stabilizes in a narrow temperature window around 580°C, with binary phases (Fe₃Sn₂, Fe₅Sn₃) dominating at lower temperatures (400-550°C) and elemental Mn appearing above 600°C. The best electrical, magnetic, and magneto-optical properties correlate with Fe₂MnSn dominance, but significant disordered growth below 100 nm highlights poor thickness scaling behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ternary alloys like Fe₂MnSn have tunable properties and potential for spintronics applications, but are challenging to synthesize phase-pure due to competing binary and elemental phases. The study aims to understand the temperature-dependent phase evolution and stability window for Fe₂MnSn thin films.

Method: Elemental Fe, Mn, and Sn targets were pre-calibrated to ~2:1:1 ratio and co-sputtered at room temperature onto substrates, followed by post-deposition annealing at temperatures ranging from 400°C to 700°C. Structural characterization was performed to identify phase evolution as a function of temperature.

Result: Two binary hexagonal phases (Fe₃Sn₂ and Fe₅Sn₃) and elemental Fe stabilize between 400-550°C, disappearing at 580°C where Fe₂MnSn becomes the only stable phase. Elemental Mn emerges above 600°C and dominates by 750°C. Electrical, magnetic, and magneto-optical properties correlate with structural findings, with best properties when Fe₂MnSn is dominant. Significant disordered growth was observed below 100 nm thickness.

Conclusion: Phase-pure Fe₂MnSn synthesis requires precise temperature control around 580°C, highlighting the difficulty in growing ternary alloys. Poor thickness scaling behavior (disordered growth below 100 nm) poses additional challenges for practical thin-film applications.

Abstract: Research in recent years has focused on the thin-film synthesis of high-quality ternary alloys, identified for their tunable properties and potential in spintronics (e.g., Heusler alloys, Kagome magnets). In a previous study, we identified the conditions for stabilizing Fe$_2$MnSn, a Kagome magnet with a high Curie temperature and magnetic anisotropy. However, ternary phases such as Fe$_2$MnSn are challenging to synthesize and stabilize within a narrow temperature window, as binary and elemental phases can also form during the growth process. To highlight these observations, we investigated the thin film phases in the Fe-Mn-Sn system near the 2:1:1 ratio as a function of annealing temperature, ranging from 400 to 700\degree C. The elemental Fe, Mn, and Sn targets were pre-calibrated to a close to 2:1:1 ratio and co-sputtered at room temperature, followed by annealing. Two binary hexagonal structures, Fe$_3$Sn$_2$ and Fe$_5$Sn$_3$, along with the elemental Fe phase, are stabilized between 400-550\degree C, but disappear at 580\degree C, where Fe$_2$MnSn is the only stable phase. Elemental Mn phase starts to appear starting from 600\degree C, and becomes dominant by 750\degree C. Electrical, magnetic and magneto-optical properties are observed to correlate with the structural findings and the best properties are observed in the temperature range where Fe$_2$MnSn is the dominant phase. In general, our study highlights the difficulty in growing phase-pure ternary alloys such as Fe$_2$MnSn, which is very strongly based on precise temperature conditions. We also observed significant disordered growth below 100 nm for Fe$_2$MnSn, implying poor thickness scaling behavior.

</details>


### [59] [A Generalized Frank-Bilby Equation for Interfaces in Crystalline Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11176)
*Dongsong Tao,Luchan Zhang,David J. Srolovitz,Yang Xiang,Jian Han*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper generalizes the classical Frank-Bilby equation to a generalized Frank-Bilby equation (GFBE) that includes both dislocation and step components of interfacial defects (disconnections), enabling more complete description of interface structures in crystalline materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical Frank-Bilby equation cannot capture disconnections (defects with both dislocation and step character) in interfaces, limiting its applicability to many interfaces of practical interest.

Method: A generalized Frank-Bilby equation (GFBE) is derived that explicitly incorporates both dislocation and step components of interfacial line defects.

Result: The GFBE successfully describes interface structures in terms of disconnection networks, demonstrated on several representative interface systems.

Conclusion: The GFBE extends the classical FBE to account for both dislocation and step character of interfacial defects, providing a more complete framework for predicting interface structures in crystalline materials.

Abstract: The classical Frank-Bilby equation (FBE) is commonly used to predict the structure of interfaces in crystalline materials in terms of interfacial dislocation networks. However, in general, the line defects in interfaces are disconnections, possessing both dislocation and step character, which are not captured by the classical FBE. As a result, the FBE cannot fully describe the structure of most interfaces of practical interest. To address this issue, we derive a generalized Frank-Bilby equation (GFBE) that explicitly incorporates both dislocation and step components of interfacial defects. We demonstrate its application to several representative interface systems.

</details>


### [60] [Electric field induced Berry curvature dipole in quasi-one-dimensional Bi$_4$I$_4$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11188)
*Amiya Mondal,Awadhesh Narayan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses first-principles calculations to investigate the electric-field-tunable nonlinear Hall effect in few-layer Bi₄I₄ (α and β phases), showing that bilayer structures exhibit significantly greater Berry curvature dipole tunability via an out-of-plane electric field compared to monolayers, establishing quasi-1D bismuth halogenides as a platform for engineering nonlinear Hall response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore electric-field-tunable nonlinear Hall response in few-layer Bi₄I₄ as a platform for engineering quantum geometry effects, leveraging the time-reversal symmetric nonlinear Hall effect as a probe.

Method: Comprehensive first-principles calculations combined with symmetry analysis to track the Berry curvature dipole tensor evolution from monolayer to bilayer under out-of-plane electric fields in both α and β phases of Bi₄I₄.

Result: The monolayer shows rigid band structure and modest BCD tunability, while the bilayer exhibits substantial field-induced band modifications including Rashba splitting and gap closure (β phase), leading to significantly enhanced BCD magnitude.

Conclusion: Quasi-one-dimensional bismuth halogenides (Bi₄I₄) are a promising platform for engineering nonlinear Hall response via electric field control.

Abstract: The nonlinear Hall effect in time-reversal symmetric materials offers a powerful probe into quantum geometry. Here, we investigate the electric-field-tunable nonlinear Hall response in few-layer $\text{Bi}_4\text{I}_4$ using comprehensive first-principles calculations across both its $α$ and $β$ phases. Guided by symmetry analysis, we track the evolution of the Berry curvature dipole (BCD) tensor from the monolayer to the bilayer configuration under an out-of-plane electric field. While the monolayer features a highly rigid band structure and modest BCD tunability, the bilayer architecture exhibits substantial field-induced band modifications, including a progressive Rashba splitting and eventual gap closure in the $β$ phase. Crucially, this field-tunability allows substantial enhancement of the BCD magnitude relative to the monolayer counterpart. Our findings establish quasi-one-dimensional bismuth halogenides as a promising platform for engineering nonlinear Hall response.

</details>


### [61] [Temperature dependence of charge-to-spin conversion in rhombohedral (110) bismuth thin film](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11189)
*K. Tatsuoka,N. Fukumoto,S. Sakamoto,S. Miwa,Y. Fuseya,J. Fujimoto,R. Ohshima,J. Puebla,Y. Ando,M. Shiraishi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study investigates temperature-dependent charge-to-spin conversion in epitaxial Bi(110)/Ni bilayers, finding that spin Hall conductivity and spin diffusion length increase as temperature decreases. The results indicate that spin scattering is governed by the Elliott-Yafet mechanism and charge-to-spin conversion primarily arises from skew scattering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The large spin Hall effect in rhombohedral (110) bismuth has been observed, but its detailed physical mechanism remains unclear and debated.

Method: Temperature dependence of charge-to-spin conversion in an epitaxial Bi(110)/Ni bilayer system was investigated using the second harmonic Hall method.

Result: Both spin Hall conductivity and spin diffusion length increase with decreasing temperature.

Conclusion: Spin scattering in (110) bismuth is dominated by the Elliott-Yafet mechanism, and charge-to-spin conversion is mainly attributed to skew scattering.

Abstract: The amplitude of charge-to-spin conversion, namely the spin Hall effect (SHE), in bismuth (Bi) strongly depends on its crystal orientation. The conversion efficiency at room temperature in rhombohedral (110) bismuth is notably large as expected from its large spin-orbit interaction, and such a large SHE is ascribed to the large effective g-factor in bismuth [N. Fukumoto et al., Proc. Nat. Acad. Sci. 120, e2215030120 (2023)]. Despite the successful observation of the large conversion efficiency, a more detailed physical mechanism of the SHE in (110) bismuth is still elusive and under debate. In this work, we investigate the temperature dependence of charge-to-spin conversion in an epitaxial Bi(110)/Ni bilayer system using the second harmonic Hall method, revealing that both spin Hall conductivity and spin diffusion length augment with decreasing temperature. This finding suggests that spin scattering in (110) bismuth is dominated by the Elliott-Yafet mechanism, and the charge-to-spin conversion is mainly attributed to skew scattering.

</details>


### [62] [Impact of Electronic Energy Dissipation on Primary Radiation Damage Formation in Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11253)
*Nadezda Korepanova,Rafael Nuñez-Palacio,Andrea E. Sand*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study uses molecular dynamics with a two-temperature model to show that the functional form of ion-electron coupling critically affects defect production, clustering, and recombination in silicon radiation damage, with outcomes also depending on the interatomic potential choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how ion-electron coupling influences radiation damage formation in silicon, as accurate modeling of electronic effects is essential for reliable predictions of defect evolution under irradiation.

Method: Molecular dynamics simulations within a two-temperature model, using a threshold-free coupling approach based on local electron density, comparing two coupling functions and two interatomic potentials across a range of primary knock-on atom energies.

Result: The functional form of ion-electron coupling critically determines defect production efficiency, clustering, and recombination; the coupling's impact on defect recombination during cascade cooling depends on the choice of interatomic potential.

Conclusion: Both ion-electron coupling and interatomic potentials must be physically grounded for reliable radiation damage predictions; the coupling functional form cannot be treated as a minor parameter.

Abstract: In this work, we investigate the role of ion-electron coupling in simulations of radiation damage formation in silicon using molecular dynamics simulations within a two-temperature model. We explore predictions of a threshold-free approach to the coupling that accounts for both the electronic stopping and electron-phonon coupling using a local electron density-based formalism. We compare two different coupling functions across a range of primary knock-on atom energies using two interatomic potentials. Our results demonstrate that the functional form of the ion-electron coupling plays a critical role in determining defect production efficiency, clustering, and recombination, and must therefore be carefully considered for accurate modeling of radiation damage formation. Furthermore, we find that the impact of the coupling in particular on the recombination of defects during the cooling phase of the cascade depends on the choice of interatomic potential, emphasizing the importance of physically grounded descriptions for both electronic effects and atom-atom interactions for reliable radiation damage predictions.

</details>


### [63] [PAC Studio Machine Learning: Human-in-the-Loop Analysis of TDPAC Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11298)
*Thien Thanh Dang,Doru Constantin Lupascu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: PAC Studio ML is a Python desktop environment that integrates machine learning with physics-based modeling to assist, not replace, experts in the ill-conditioned inverse problem of time-differential perturbed angular correlation (TDPAC/PAC) analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PAC analysis is an ill-conditioned inverse problem where site count, interaction type, hyperfine parameters, damping, and initialization choices produce competing solutions, making conventional fitting challenging and reproducibility poor.

Method: The software integrates a Hamiltonian-based forward PAC model, synthetic training libraries, feature extraction, one-/two-/three-site ML predictors, direct parameter prediction, Auto sites model-family screening, ML-seeded nonlinear least-squares refinement, visualization, benchmarking, diagnostics, model-card reporting, and export tools.

Result: Held-out synthetic tests demonstrate proof of operation and unequal recoverability of PAC parameters. BiFeO3 case studies showcase conventional, direct-ML, ML-seeded, and Auto sites workflows as software demonstrations.

Conclusion: PAC Studio ML is positioned as a supporting tool that improves workflow speed and diagnostic transparency, while final model choice, physical constraints, and materials interpretation remain the researcher's responsibility.

Abstract: Time-differential perturbed angular correlation (TDPAC or PAC) analysis is an ill-conditioned inverse problem in which site count, interaction type, correlated hyperfine parameters, damping, and initialization choices can produce competing numerical solutions. This software paper presents PAC Studio ML, a human-in-the-loop Python desktop environment for physics-informed inverse analysis of PAC spectra. The software integrates a Hamiltonian-based forward PAC model, user-defined synthetic training libraries, feature extraction, one-, two-, and three-site machine-learning predictors, direct parameter prediction, Auto sites model-family screening, ML-seeded nonlinear least-squares refinement, visualization, benchmarking, diagnostics, model-card reporting, and export tools. The ML component is designed to support, not replace, conventional fitting and expert interpretation by accelerating parameter exploration, suggesting plausible initialization regions, comparing site-count hypotheses, and improving reproducibility. Held-out synthetic tests demonstrate proof of operation and illustrate the unequal recoverability of PAC parameters in difficult inverse problems. Selected BiFeO3 examples demonstrate conventional, direct-ML, ML-seeded, and Auto sites workflows as software case studies, not as a complete experimental validation corpus. PAC Studio ML is therefore positioned as a supporting tool for expert PAC analysis: it improves workflow speed and diagnostic transparency while final model choice, physical constraints, and materials interpretation remain the responsibility of the researcher.

</details>


### [64] [Exchange topology and criticality in ferrite and chromium spinels: a unified Monte Carlo analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11729)
*Keltoum Khallouq,Ayoub El Maazouzi,Kamal Boumhara,Rachid Masrour*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A unified Monte Carlo analysis of ferrites and chromium spinels introduces two new reduced quantities (θ_CW/T_C and t*) that reveal distinct ordering regimes: ferrites exhibit near-mean-field Ising behavior, while chromium compounds show a progression from Ising to Heisenberg to strongly frustrated antiferromagnetic ordering, with a dimensionality crossover in Fe₃O₄ thin films.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To place the exchange interactions, ordering temperatures, critical exponents, hysteresis, and magnetocaloric properties of diverse magnetic spinel families (inverse ferrites and chromium spinels/chromates) on a common theoretical footing, and to identify the role of frustration and spin symmetry in determining their critical behavior.

Method: Unified analysis of Metropolis Monte Carlo simulations across two families: (1) inverse ferrites Fe³⁺_A[M²⁺Fe³⁺]_B O₄ (M = Co, Cu, Fe, Ni) with two chemically distinct sublattices coupled by superexchange; (2) chromium spinels A Cr₂ X₄ (A = Zn, Cd, Hg; X = S, Se) and breathing-lattice chromates Li M Cr₄ O₈ (M = Ga, In) with a single Cr³⁺ species on a corner-sharing tetrahedral network. Introduced two reduced quantities: θ_CW/T_C for ferrites and t* = k_B T_C / [J₁ S(S+1)] for chromium compounds.

Result: Ferrites cluster at θ_CW/T_C = 0.94–1.19 near the mean-field value of 1, indicating dominant unfrustrated A–B superexchange, with critical exponents (β = 0.20–0.26, γ = 1.23–1.27, δ = 4.76–4.78) consistent with the 3D Ising universality class. Chromium systems split into three regimes: t* ≈ 1.4–1.9 (Ising-treated sulfides), t* ≈ 0.99 (Heisenberg-treated selenides), and t* ≈ 0.24–0.25 (antiferromagnetic breathing chromates), quantifying T_C suppression by continuous spin symmetry and geometric frustration. Finite-thickness Fe₃O₄ simulations show a dimensionality crossover between 2 and 4 unit cells.

Conclusion: The principal open problem is the Ising-versus-Heisenberg dependence of the predicted universality class in frustrated chromites.

Abstract: We report a unified analysis of Metropolis Monte Carlo results for two families of magnetic spinels: inverse ferrites Fe$^{3+}_{A}$[M$^{2+}$Fe$^{3+}$]$_{B}$O$_4$ (M = Co, Cu, Fe, Ni), where superexchange couples two chemically distinct sublattices, and chromium spinels $A$Cr$_2X_4$ ($A$ = Zn, Cd, Hg, $X$ = S, Se) together with the breathing-lattice chromates Li$M$Cr$_4$O$_8$ ($M$ = Ga, In), where a single Cr$^{3+}$ species occupies a corner-sharing tetrahedral network. Placing the exchange constants, transition temperatures, critical exponents, hysteresis, and magnetocaloric responses of these systems on a common footing, we introduce two reduced quantities not previously reported: the ratio $θ_{\mathrm{CW}}/T_C$ for the ferrites and the normalized ordering scale $t^{*}=k_BT_C/[J_1S(S+1)]$ for the chromium compounds. The ferrites cluster in the range $θ_{\mathrm{CW}}/T_C = 0.94$-$1.19$, close to the mean-field expectation of unity and the signature of dominant, unfrustrated A-B superexchange, and their exponents ($β= 0.20$-$0.26$, $γ= 1.23$-$1.27$, $δ= 4.76$-$4.78$) follow the three-dimensional Ising class. The chromium systems split into three regimes: $t^{*} \approx 1.4$-$1.9$ for Ising-treated sulfides, $t^{*} \approx 0.99$ for Heisenberg-treated selenides, and $t^{*} \approx 0.24$-$0.25$ for the antiferromagnetic breathing chromates, quantifying the combined suppression of $T_C$ by continuous spin symmetry and by geometric frustration. Finite-thickness simulations of Fe$_3$O$_4$ resolve a dimensionality crossover between two and four unit cells. We identify the Ising-versus-Heisenberg dependence of the predicted universality class in frustrated chromites as the principal open problem.

</details>


### [65] [Direct writing of individual quantum dots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11864)
*Weikun Zhu,Natalie Ngoh,Shelly Ben-David,Maxwell Conte,Teddy Hsieh,Sarah O. Spector,Tara Sverko,Patricia Jastrzebska-Perfect,Will Jack,Jinwoo Sim,Peter F. Satterthwaite,Farnaz Niroui*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper reports direct writing of perovskite quantum dots with single-emitter resolution using a thermal scanning probe method, enabling site-selective synthesis of CsPbI3 single-photon emitters with <25 nm spatial control, high single-photon purity (98%), and on-demand coupling to photonic cavities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perovskite nanocrystals are promising single-photon sources but suffer from stochastic dispersion in solution, making deterministic integration with photonic structures challenging. Conventional top-down fabrication methods lack the resolution and material compatibility needed for heterogeneous integration.

Method: A thermal scanning probe method induces nanoscale-confined formation volume for site-selective synthesis of perovskite QDs, achieving atomic-scale single QDs with spectral tunability and <25 nm spatial control. The emitters are integrated with photonic cavities for on-demand coupling.

Result: High-yield arrays of CsPbI3 single-photon emitters with narrow linewidths, single-photon purity up to 98% at room temperature, and measured enhancement in spontaneous emission rate due to cavity coupling.

Conclusion: The work combines atomic-scale chemical synthesis tunability with additive manufacturing spatial control, opening new emitter engineering strategies to overcome integration obstacles and realize the potential of colloidal materials for quantum technologies.

Abstract: Quantum light sources capable of generating single photons are fundamental building blocks for photonic quantum technologies. In the ongoing search for an ideal quantum emitter, inorganic halide perovskite nanocrystals have emerged as a promising source of single photons. Their unique optical response, with an unmatched ease of synthetic tunability, stands out amongst the competing platforms. However, their stochastic dispersion in solution challenges the deterministic and stable integration of individual emitters with photonic structures that is required for practical technologies. Notably, resolution and material compatibility constraints make conventional top-down fabrication processes insufficient for such heterogeneous integration. Here, we report direct writing of perovskite quantum dots (QDs) with individual-emitter resolution. By inducing a nanoscale-confined formation volume using a thermal scanning probe method, we achieve site-selective synthesis down to a single atomic-scale QD with spectral tunability and < 25 nm spatial control. As a result, we demonstrate high-yield arrays of CsPbI3 single-photon emitters with narrow linewidths and high single-photon purity up to 98% at room temperature, performance consistent with that of their state-of-the-art colloidal counterparts. Through such deterministic control, we uniquely realize the precise, on-demand coupling of these emitters to photonic cavities, as evidenced by a measured enhancement in the spontaneous emission rate. This represents a key advancement toward addressing the longstanding integration obstacles of these materials. Overall, by combining the atomic-scale tunability of chemical synthesis with the spatial control of additive manufacturing, our work opens new emitter engineering strategies to realize the untapped potential of colloidal materials for next-generation quantum technologies.

</details>
