Opposite-sign intralayer OAM-dependent coupling immediately converts the zero-indirect-gap semimetal into a metal, in which the global spectrum is metallic while the layer--resolved pseudo-spin Chern number remains well defined as long as the direct gap at each crystal momentum and the pseudo-spin gap remain open. The model also exhibits asymmetric boundary states: in the semimetallic regime, one edge hosts perfectly flat bands, whereas the opposite edge supports gapless counter-propagating modes forming a one-dimensional Dirac cone. An edge-localized interlayer coupling gaps only the counter-propagating edge states, leaving the flat-band edge essentially intact, while intralayer OAM-dependent coupling bends the exact flat band into a dispersive boundary mode without affecting the gapped Dirac edge. These results open a route toward the controlled engineering of layer--resolved topological gapless phases in synthetic and quantum materials.

</details>


### [94] [Spectroscopy of low-lying valley states in hot Si/SiGe quantum dots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11048)
*Connor Nasseraddin,Heun Mo Yoo,Tanner M. Janda,Jason R. Petta*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper compares pulsed-gate spectroscopy (PGS) and detuning axis spectroscopy (DAPS) for measuring valley splittings in Si/SiGe quantum devices up to 700 mK. DAPS outperforms PGS, resolving splittings as small as ~86 μeV versus ~210 μeV for PGS, enabling efficient high-throughput characterization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-lying valley states in Si hinder the development of large-scale spin-based quantum processors. Rapid prototyping of novel Si/SiGe heterostructures and gate stacks is needed to identify pathways for increasing valley splitting.

Method: The authors compare pulsed-gate spectroscopy (PGS) and detuning axis spectroscopy (DAPS) at temperatures up to 700 mK.

Result: DAPS resolves valley splittings as small as ~86 μeV, while PGS achieves only ~210 μeV resolution. DAPS outperforms PGS across the temperature range studied.

Conclusion: DAPS can be used to efficiently extract valley splittings at elevated temperatures in high-throughput cryostats, making it well-suited for rapid material and device screening.

Abstract: The presence of low-lying valley states in Si may hinder the development of large-scale spin-based quantum processors. Rapid prototyping of novel Si/SiGe heterostructures and gate stacks will be central to identifying pathways that increase the valley splitting. We compare the performance of pulsed-gate spectroscopy (PGS) and detuning axis spectroscopy (DAPS) at temperatures up to 700 mK. We find that DAPS outperforms PGS, with DAPS resolving valley splittings as small as ~86 $μ$eV, while the energy resolution of PGS is only ~210~$μ$eV. Our work demonstrates that DAPS can be used to efficiently extract valley splittings at elevated temperatures in high throughput cryostats.

</details>


### [95] [Tellurium Metasurface Beam Splitter with Pulse Laser-Controlled Anisotropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11265)
*Takuto Hiraoka,Mizuho Matoba,Yuta Kobayashi,Arata Mitsuzuka,Masashi Kawaguchi,Haruyuki Sakurai,Kuniaki Konishi,Masamitsu Hayashi*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A lithography-free method for creating reconfigurable metasurfaces by using pulsed lasers to program the crystal orientation of tellurium, enabling rewritable optical anisotropy without conventional nanofabrication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a simpler, rewritable approach to fabricating metasurfaces that avoids conventional nanofabrication processes while enabling dynamic reconfiguration of optical functionality.

Method: Spatial control of the crystallographic c-axis orientation in tellurium using pulse laser irradiation, creating laser-programmable optical anisotropy without lithography.

Result: Demonstrated a Te metasurface beam splitter via laser-written optical-axis patterning, with experimental optical response matching theoretical predictions and numerical simulations.

Conclusion: This lithography-free, rewritable approach offers a promising platform for non-resonant active metasurfaces and reconfigurable flat-optics applications.

Abstract: Laser-programmable optical anisotropy offers a new route to developing reconfigurable metasurfaces without conventional nanofabrication processes. Here, we demonstrate a lithography-free approach based on spatial control of the crystallographic $c$ axis orientation in tellurium (Te) using pulse laser irradiation. As a proof of concept, we demonstrate a Te metasurface beam splitter by laser-written optical-axis patterning and experimentally confirm that its optical response is in good agreement with theoretical predictions and numerical simulations. By directly programming the local optical anisotropy, this method enables a simple fabrication process while offering the possibility of rewriting and dynamically reconfiguring device functionality. These features make this approach a promising platform for non-resonant active metasurfaces and other reconfigurable flat-optics applications.

</details>


### [96] [Mode-locking instability and multiple soliton formation in GaN polariton waveguide cavities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11278)
*O. Bahrova,V. Develay,H. Souissi,C. Brimont,L. Doyennette,B. Alloing,E. Cambril,S. Bouchoule,T. Ackemann,J. Zuniga-Perez,D. Solnyshkov,G. Malpuech,T. Guillet*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates multi-soliton formation in 1D ridge polariton waveguides, showing that tuning the gain position (via pumping laser and excitonic reservoir) enables switching between single and multiple soliton regimes, with the splitting mechanism governed by exciton reservoir dynamics as confirmed by Gross-Pitaevskii equation simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and control the emergence of multi-soliton regimes in out-of-equilibrium polariton systems, specifically in 1D ridge polariton waveguides of different lengths.

Method: Varying the position of the gain (pumping laser and associated excitonic reservoir) in 1D ridge polariton waveguides of two different lengths, and solving the Gross-Pitaevskii equations of the coupled exciton-photon system to quantitatively reproduce the observed soliton dynamics.

Result: By varying the gain position, the soliton formation regime can be tuned between single and multiple solitons. The soliton splitting mechanism is governed by the exciton reservoir dynamics.

Conclusion: The gain position is a key control parameter for tuning soliton multiplicity in polariton waveguides, and reservoir-mediated dynamics underlies the soliton splitting process.

Abstract: We study the emergence of multi-soliton regimes in 1D ridge polariton waveguides of two different lengths. We show that by varying the position of the gain, which in out-of-equilibrium polariton systems is provided by the pumping laser and its associated excitonic reservoir, it is possible to tune the regime of soliton formation between single and multiple solitons. This soliton dynamics can be quantitatively reproduced by solving the Gross-Pitaevskii equations of the coupled exciton-photon system, which show that the soliton splitting mechanism is governed by the exciton reservoir dynamics.

</details>


### [97] [Extended Landau--Lifshitz equation for nanomagnets: a path-integral derivation of surface-induced magnetization nutation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11420)
*H. Kachkachi,P. Thibaudeau*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper derives an extended Landau-Lifshitz equation for nanomagnets with surface anisotropy by starting from an atomistic spin Hamiltonian, using spin coherent-state path integrals, and adiabatically eliminating fast transverse spin-misalignment fluctuations. The resulting effective dynamics include renormalized Zeeman and anisotropy fields, plus nutation- and damping-like corrections that shift the ferromagnetic resonance frequency and linewidth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a microscopic, first-principles derivation of how surface anisotropy in nanomagnets gives rise to magnetization nutation and corrections to standard macrospin dynamics (Landau-Lifshitz equation), which cannot be captured by purely phenomenological or continuum theories.

Method: The authors use the spin coherent-state path integral formalism starting from an atomistic spin Hamiltonian. They first derive a continuum Euclidean action (including Wess-Zumino-Witten/Berry phase, exchange, Zeeman, and core/surface anisotropy). Then they decompose magnetization into a macrospin component and transverse fluctuations, expand the action to quadratic order in fluctuations, and adiabatically eliminate the fast transverse modes using static Green's functions, yielding a closed extended Landau-Lifshitz equation.

Result: An extended Landau-Lifshitz equation for the macrospin with effective-field corrections from spin misalignment, including renormalized Zeeman and anisotropy fields and additional nutation- and damping-like contributions. The predicted shifts in ferromagnetic resonance frequency and linewidth are measurable with standard GHz spectrometers.

Conclusion: The work establishes a microscopic foundation for surface-induced magnetization nutation in nanomagnets and provides a framework for estimating corrections to precession frequency and damping. The adiabatic-elimination mechanism is expected to generalize to any slow magnetic variable coupled to a fast-fluctuating bath.

Abstract: An effective dynamical equation for the magnetization of a nanomagnet with surface anisotropy is derived from an atomistic spin Hamiltonian using the spin coherent-state path integral formalism. The derivation proceeds in two steps. First, the continuum Euclidean action for the many-spin nanomagnet is obtained, including the Wess--Zumino--Witten (Berry phase) term, as well as exchange, Zeeman, and core/surface anisotropy contributions. Second, the local magnetization density is decomposed into a slowly varying macrospin component and transverse spin-misalignment fluctuations driven by surface effects.
  A systematic expansion of the action is then performed up to quadratic order in the transverse-fluctuation variables. Under the adiabatic approximation, in which transverse modes relax much faster than the macrospin, these modes are eliminated by using their static Green's function solution. This results in a closed, extended Landau--Lifshitz equation for the macrospin, featuring an effective field with nontrivial corrections from spin misalignment. These corrections renormalize both the Zeeman and anisotropy fields and introduce additional terms that act as nutation- and damping-like contributions. ...
  Together, these results establish a microscopic foundation for surface-induced magnetization nutation in nanomagnets and provide a framework to estimate corrections to the precession frequency and effective damping. The corresponding shift in the ferromagnetic-resonance frequency and linewidth is measurable with standard GHz spectrometers, and the underlying adiabatic-elimination mechanism is expected to generalize to any slow magnetic variable coupled to a bath of fast-fluctuating modes.

</details>


### [98] [Theory of phonon-induced spin relaxation in a structured phononic reservoir](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11479)
*Raseeb F. Haroon,Paweł Machnikowski*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper develops a theory for electron spin relaxation in structured phononic reservoirs, combining Markovian and non-Markovian open quantum system theory with finite-element simulations. Using a quantum dot in a phononic waveguide as a model system, it reveals complex relaxation behavior: rates are typically ~10x higher than in bulk, but can be suppressed by many orders of magnitude due to phononic band gaps and symmetry-based selection rules. Singularities arise at van Hove points, for which power-law scaling and non-Markovian dynamics (polaronic dressing) are derived.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding electron spin relaxation in structured phononic reservoirs is crucial for spin dynamics in hybrid mechanical systems and for designing devices that integrate spin degrees of freedom with photonic and phononic architectures, where the phonon density of states is modulated in the spectral range corresponding to moderate magnetic fields.

Method: The authors combine Markovian and non-Markovian open quantum system theory with finite-element simulations to model spin relaxation in a quantum dot embedded in a phononic waveguide, accounting for structured phonon density of states.

Result: Spin relaxation in structured phononic environments is far more complex than in bulk. Relaxation rates are generally an order of magnitude higher, but can be suppressed by many orders of magnitude due to phononic band gaps and symmetry-based selection rules. At the boundaries of these regimes, van Hove singularities cause singularities in relaxation rates.

Conclusion: The authors develop power-law scaling for singularities at van Hove points and propose a non-Markovian description of the dynamics, revealing polaronic dressing of the spin into slow acoustic modes.

Abstract: By combining Markovian and non-Markovian open quantum system theory with finite-element simulations, we develop a theory of electron spin relaxation in a structured phononic reservoir. This problem is crucial for understanding spin dynamics in hybrid systems involving mechanical modes, as well as for the design of devices combining spin degrees of freedom with photonic and phononic architectures, where the phonon density of states is modulated in the relevant spectral range corresponding to moderate magnetic fields. Taking a QD in a phononic waveguide as a representative and technologically relevant example, we show that spin relaxation in such environments is much more complex than in bulk. While the relaxation rates are typically an order of magnitude higher than in bulk, there are parameter windows where the relaxation is suppressed by many orders of magnitude due to gaps in mode dispersion and selection rules imposed by mode symmetry. At the border between these two sectors, van Hove singularities in phonon dispersion lead to singularities in relaxation rates, for which we develop power-law scaling and propose a non-Markovian description of the dynamics, revealing polaronic dressing of the spin into slow acoustic modes.

</details>


### [99] [Purcell enhanced and blinking free single photons from InAs/GaAs quantum dots in deterministically placed circular Bragg gratings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11495)
*Peter Gschwandtner,Quirin Buchinger,Krishna Chand Maurya,Mohamed Helal,Barbara Souza Damasceno,Ravindra Kumar,Hyemin Kim,Ievgen Brytavskyi,Silke Kuhn,Arne Ludwig,Dirk Reuter,Yong-Hoon Cho,Tobias Huber-Loyola,Sven Höfling*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates a bright, blinking-free, charge-tunable single-photon source at ~900 nm using InAs quantum dots in a p-i-n GaAs membrane with a modified circular Bragg grating resonator, achieving high Purcell factor (18.0), high photon extraction efficiency (68.1%), low multi-photon emission (g²(0) < 0.017), and precise charge-state control from X⁻ to X³⁻.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop efficient, deterministic, and tunable single-photon sources essential for quantum communication, quantum repeaters, and photonic quantum computing.

Method: InAs quantum dots embedded in a p-i-n doped GaAs membrane with a modified circular Bragg grating (CBG) resonator that incorporates fourfold symmetric bridges in a labyrinth-like geometry for electrical contact.

Result: Negative trion lifetime of 44.3 ps (Purcell factor 18.0), PEE of 68.1%, blinking-free operation with g²(0) < 0.017, and tunable charge-state control from X⁻ to X³⁻ via vertical diode bias.

Conclusion: The architecture provides high efficiency, high repetition rates, and deterministic charge control, meeting key requirements for next-generation quantum network hardware.

Abstract: The development of efficient, deterministic, and tunable single-photon sources is a cornerstone for the realization of long-distance quantum communication, quantum repeaters, and photonic quantum computing technologies. In this study, we demonstrate a bright, charge-tunable single-photon source in the 900 nm wavelength range based on InAs quantum dots (QDs) embedded in a p-i-n doped GaAs membrane, which shows blinking free emission.
  We use a modified circular Bragg grating (CBG) as a micro-resonator. By adding fourfold symmetric bridges in a labyrinth-like geometry, we provide a conductive pathway to the central disk, thereby enabling electrical contact to the QD while maintaining high Purcell enhancement and photon extraction efficiency (PEE). For the negative trion (X-), we demonstrate a lifetime of $44.3 \pm 0.2$ ps - corresponding to a Purcell factor of $18.0 \pm 0.7$ and a PEE of $68.1% \pm 3.1$ %. Furthermore, the device is blinking-free with very low multi-photon contribution, evidenced by a second-order autocorrelation value $g(2)(0) < 0.017 \pm 0.015$. By applying a vertical diode bias, we demonstrate precise charge-state control, resolving distinct emission plateaus ranging from the single negatively charged trion ($X^-$) to triply negatively charged excitons ($X^{3-}$).
  These results showcase a robust architecture that simultaneously provides high efficiency, high repetition rates, and deterministic charge control, fulfilling key requirements for the next generation of quantum network hardware.

</details>


### [100] [High-field Josephson effect enabled by a moiré Hofstadter spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11721)
*A. Díez-Carlón,M. Cárdenes Wuttig,N. Wei,D. Ivanov,P. Altpeter,P. Hakonen,K. Watanabe,T. Taniguchi,L. I. Glazman,D. K. Efetov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Moiré-engineered graphene Josephson junctions enable phase-coherent superconducting interference up to 6 T in the Hofstadter-butterfly regime, far beyond conventional limits, by converting Landau levels with quenched group velocity into dispersive magnetic Bloch bands that support extended Andreev trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic fields typically suppress phase-coherent Josephson transport, limiting superconducting interferometry to low fields. The authors aim to overcome this constraint using moiré-engineered graphene Josephson junctions.

Method: Balistic graphene/hBN junctions were used to establish phase-coherent Andreev transport via Fabry-Pérot oscillations and Fraunhofer interference across the primary Dirac cone and reconstructed moiré minibands. Phase-coherent Josephson interference was measured up to 6 T and compared with Hofstadter-spectrum calculations.

Result: Phase-coherent Josephson interference was demonstrated up to 6 T in the fractal Hofstadter-butterfly regime, well beyond the range expected for conventional ballistic graphene junctions. The moiré potential transforms Landau levels with quenched group velocity into dispersive magnetic Bloch bands with finite quasiparticle group velocity.

Conclusion: Hofstadter minibands can stabilize phase-coherent superconductivity deep into the quantum Hall regime, establishing a new platform for high-field superconducting interferometry.

Abstract: Magnetic fields generally suppress phase-coherent Josephson transport, limiting superconducting interferometry to relatively low fields. Here we show that moiré-engineered graphene Josephson junctions can overcome this constraint. Using ballistic graphene/hBN junctions, we establish phase-coherent Andreev transport through Fabry-Pérot oscillations and Fraunhofer interference that persist across both the primary Dirac cone and reconstructed moiré minibands. We then demonstrate phase-coherent Josephson interference up to 6 T in the fractal Hofstadter-butterfly regime, well beyond the range expected for conventional ballistic graphene junctions. Comparison with Hofstadter-spectrum calculations reveals that superconductivity survives where the moiré potential transforms Landau levels with quenched group velocity into dispersive magnetic Bloch bands with finite quasiparticle group velocity, enabling extended electron-hole Andreev trajectories across the junction. Our results show that Hofstadter minibands can stabilize phase-coherent superconductivity deep into the parameter domain conventionally associated with the quantum Hall regime, establishing a new platform for high-field superconducting interferometry.

</details>


### [101] [Robust Spin Qubit Coupler via Minimal Kitaev Chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11554)
*Jiaan Qi,Hongqi Xu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes using a minimal Kitaev chain as a robust coupling module between spin qubits, leveraging Andreev bound states to mediate long-distance anisotropic exchange coupling. By tuning the chemical potential, a sweet spot is achieved that provides both strong coupling and first-order noise immunity, yielding over 200-fold improvement in coherence time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While minimal Kitaev chains are known to host unprotected Majorana zero modes, their potential role for spin qubits has been underappreciated. Recent breakthroughs in fine control of transport behaviors motivate using them as a coupling module.

Method: The hybrid segment's Andreev bound states (ABSs) mediate long-distance, anisotropic exchange coupling between spin qubits. The chemical potential of ABS is used to selectively control coupling strength and response to local perturbations.

Result: A unique sweet spot is identified that simultaneously enables strong coupling and first-order immunity against charge noise. The protected qubit encoded on the minimal Kitaev chain at this sweet spot achieves over 200-fold improvement in decoherence time.

Conclusion: The minimal Kitaev chain serves as an effective and robust coupling module for spin qubits, with the chemical potential providing a simple control knob and a noise-immune sweet spot that dramatically enhances qubit coherence.

Abstract: While a minimal Kitaev chain is promised to host unprotected Majorana zero modes, its role for spin qubits is relatively underappreciated. Following recent breakthroughs in the fine control of transport behaviors, we propose to use minimal Kitaev chain as a robust coupling module between spin qubits. Long-distance, anisotropic exchange coupling can be mediated by the Andreev bound states (ABSs) in the hybrid segment. The chemical potential of ABS gives a simple way to selectively control the coupling strength and its response to local perturbations. Moreover, this additional control degree of freedom creates a unique sweet spot, allowing both strong coupling and first-order immunity against charge noise. The protected qubit encoded on the minimal Kitaev chain at the sweet spot is shown to boast over 200 fold improvement in decoherence time.

</details>


### [102] [Discovery of a symmetry-driven electronic cascade in a $d$-wave altermagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11567)
*Zhouyi Yin,Zheng Shi,Shuxuan Zhang,Changchao Liu,Xingkai Cheng,Fayuan Zhang,Han-Bin Deng,Jia-Xin Yin,Chaoyu Chen,Guang-Han Cao,Junwei Liu,Yue Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Using phase-resolved STM, the paper reveals a symmetry-driven cascade of charge-order instabilities in the d-wave altermagnet Rb₁₋δV₂Te₂O, where a primary density wave triggers nematic and off-axis modulations, forming an intertwined charge-multipole state explained by Landau theory with bilinear coupling to octupolar partners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether and how crystal symmetry in altermagnets organizes collective electronic order beyond band structure formation, a largely unexplored question.

Method: Phase-resolved scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) on Rb₁₋δV₂Te₂O, combined with doping and temperature evolution studies, analyzed within a Landau theoretical framework.

Result: A hierarchy of coupled density-wave instabilities was observed: a primary density wave, a nematic component, and an off-axis modulation with geometrically tied wave vectors. Phase-resolved spectroscopy revealed separate contrast-inversion energies and branch-selective spectral-weight redistribution in the off-axis mode.

Conclusion: Altermagnetic symmetry drives a cascade of charge orders beyond band formation, with mutual stabilization via bilinear coupling to octupolar partners, offering a new perspective on emergent many-body states in correlated quantum materials.

Abstract: Altermagnets host magnetic compensation together with non-relativistic spin-split bands, a coexistence enabled by crystal symmetry. Yet whether and how crystal symmetry organizes collective electronic order remains largely unexplored. Here we uncover a symmetry-driven cascade of finite-$q$ charge order in a $d$-wave altermagnet Rb$_{1-δ}$V$_2$Te$_2$O, using phase-resolved scanning tunneling microscopy. A primary density-wave instability drives an initial electronic reconstruction, followed by the emergence of a nematic component and an off-axis modulation with wave vectors geometrically tied to the preceding orders. Phase-resolved spectroscopy distinguishes these components through separate contrast-inversion energies and maps out branch-selective spectral-weight redistribution within the off-axis mode. Together with doping and temperature evolution, these observations establish a highly coordinated hierarchy of coupled density-wave instabilities, consistent with successive symmetry lowering. This multi-component hierarchy can be well described in the Landau framework through sequential softening of the charge orders, where bilinear coupling to their compatible octupolar partners at the lower-symmetry stages enables mutual stabilization within an intertwined charge-multipole state. Such a transparent realization of a charge-order cascade shows how altermagnetic symmetry can extend beyond band formation to organize collective electronic order, offering a new perspective on emergent many-body states in correlated quantum materials.

</details>


### [103] [Anomalous Dissipation in Current Biased Josephson Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11647)
*Johannes Hauff,Niklas Gaiser,Joachim Ankerhold,Dominik Maile*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper theoretically discovers a new phase diffusive regime in a current-biased Josephson junction when embedded in a circuit with anomalous dissipation (a resistor-capacitor parallel branch). The anomalous dissipation strongly impacts quantum escape from the zero-voltage state at low temperatures, substantially enhancing quantum tunneling and leading to short voltage pulses with high re-trapping probability. The results have implications for microwave photon detection and dissipative quantum annealing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how embedding a Josephson junction in a circuit environment with anomalous dissipation (RC shunt) modifies its phase dynamics, particularly the switching/escape process out of the zero-voltage state, and to understand the impact on quantum tunneling at low temperatures.

Method: Theoretical analysis modeling the Josephson junction switching as the escape of a fictitious phase-particle from a metastable well. The circuit includes a resistor-capacitor parallel branch that introduces anomalous dissipation coupling to the junction charge. Both thermal activation and quantum tunneling regimes are studied.

Result: Anomalous dissipation substantially enhances quantum escape processes at low temperatures for realistic circuit parameters. The escape is followed by a short voltage pulse with high re-trapping probability, defining a new phase diffusive regime.

Conclusion: This class of circuits may be useful for microwave photon detection and dissipative quantum annealing. The analysis also provides a general framework for engineering dissipative dynamics in nonlinear systems using anomalous environments.

Abstract: A new phase diffusive regime in a current biased Josephson junction is theoretically explored which originates from embedding the junction in a circuit environment with anomalous dissipation. This is realized by placing parallel to the junction a resistor in series with a capacitor such that electromagnetic fluctuations effectively couple also to the charge of the junction. This leads to rich Josephson dynamics, in particular for the switching of the junction out of a zero voltage state. Modelled as the escape process of a fictitious phase-particle out of a metastable well, a detailed study reveals that anomalous dissipation has a strong impact at low temperatures when quantum tunneling dominates against thermal activation. As a manifestation, a regime is found, where for realistic circuit parameters the quantum escape process is substantially enhanced, followed by a short voltage pulse and re-trapping with high probability. This class of circuits may be leveraged for detecting microwave photons or dissipative quantum annealing processes. In addition, the analysis provides a general framework for engineering dissipative dynamics in nonlinear systems using anomalous environments.

</details>


### [104] [Thermodynamic bound on the Fano factor of a coherent thermoelectric heat engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11704)
*Nahual Sobrino,Rosario Fazio,Matteo Acciai*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper derives universal lower bounds on the Fano factor for thermoelectric transport, showing F>1/2 for fermionic heat engines and F>1 for bosonic/classical carriers, revealing that thermodynamic uncertainty relation violations are more restricted for heat engines than generic quantum processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the thermodynamic uncertainty relation for quantum thermoelectric transport and determine whether quantum advantages (violations of classical TUR) are universally available for heat-engine operations.

Method: Analytical derivation of Fano factor bounds for fermionic, bosonic, and classical carriers in coherent thermoelectric transport, with numerical evidence showing these bounds are tight and saturable by properly designed transmissions.

Result: Fermionic heat engines have a universal Fano factor lower bound F>1/2; bosonic and classical carriers have F>1. Classical TUR violations are more restricted for heat engines than generic processes.

Conclusion: Quantum advantages (TUR violations) are far more constrained for thermoelectric heat engines than previously thought, with clear distinctions between fermionic and bosonic/classical carriers.

Abstract: We show that for fermionic coherent thermoelectric transport, selecting heat-engine operation yields a thermodynamic uncertainty relation for the charge current that imposes a universal lower limit of $F > 1/2$ on the corresponding Fano factor. We find that violations of the thermodynamic uncertainty relation for classical Markov processes, typically associated with a quantum advantage, are far more restricted for heat engines than what is allowed by a generic thermodynamic process. For bosonic and classical carriers, the minimum Fano factor increases to $F > 1$, and the thermodynamic uncertainty relation for classical Markov processes is never violated. We provide numerical evidence that all the obtained bounds are tight and can be saturated by properly designed transmissions.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [105] [Interwoven long-range order induced by random fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10337)
*Jeremiah Bender,Thomas Vojta*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new type of long-range ordered phase called an "implectic" phase, induced by random-field disorder coupling to a composite vestigial order parameter, where spontaneous symmetry breaking occurs on a background of interwoven domain structure. The existence is demonstrated via Monte Carlo simulations in a layered J1-J2 Ising magnet, with applications in charge/spin density wave systems, frustrated magnets, cuprates, iron-based superconductors, and ultracold atoms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and characterize a novel type of long-range ordered phase that arises from impurities and defects locally breaking order-parameter symmetries, specifically through random-field disorder coupling to composite vestigial order parameters.

Method: Large-scale Monte Carlo simulations of a layered J1-J2 Ising magnet are used to explicitly demonstrate the existence of the proposed implectic phase.

Result: The existence of the implectic phase is confirmed, characterized by spontaneous symmetry breaking on a background of spatially interwoven domain structure created by random fields.

Conclusion: The implectic phase represents a distinct class of long-range order with broad applicability, and the authors discuss potential manifestations in systems with charge and spin density wave order, frustrated magnets, cuprate and iron-based superconductors, and ultracold atoms.

Abstract: We propose a distinct type of long-range ordered phase that can occur in classical and quantum many-particle systems. It is induced by impurities and defects that locally break a subset of the order-parameter symmetries, i.e., by random-field disorder that couples to a composite vestigial order parameter. The proposed ``implectic'' phase is characterized by spontaneous symmetry breaking on the background of the spatially interwoven domain structure created by the random fields. We explicitly demonstrate the existence of this phase in a layered $J_1-J_2$ Ising magnet by means of large-scale Monte Carlo simulations. We then discuss numerous potential applications in systems featuring charge and spin density wave order including frustrated magnets, cuprate and iron-based superconductors, and ultracold atoms.

</details>


### [106] [Spectral Signatures of Replica Symmetry Breaking in Optimization-Induced Random Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10513)
*Isaac Pérez Castillo*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper studies matrix ensembles induced by Gibbs measures in glassy systems, showing that a single matrix's spectrum has a universal bulk that masks glassy geometry, while the difference of two matrices from independent thermal samples reveals the glassy Gibbs geometry through mutual overlap distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether the spectrum of optimization-induced matrix ensembles inherits the underlying glassy Gibbs geometry from the quenched disorder that generates them.

Method: Analysis of a dense tensor optimization model using Parisi theory and Monte Carlo simulations, comparing spectra of single induced matrices versus differences of two matrices from independent thermal samples in the same disorder.

Result: A single induced matrix has a universal leading bulk that washes out glassy organization. The difference of two matrices from independent thermal samples in the same disorder produces a spectrum that encodes the glassy Gibbs geometry via mutual overlap distributions.

Conclusion: The glassy Gibbs geometry is not visible in the spectrum of a single induced matrix but becomes explicit in the difference spectrum, with Parisi theory and Monte Carlo confirming this mechanism across simple and glassy phases.

Abstract: We study optimization-induced matrix ensembles generated by Gibbs measures. The same quenched disorder that weights configurations also supplies the matrix entries observed on them. For glassy Gibbs measures this raises a natural question: does the induced spectrum inherit the underlying glassy Gibbs geometry? In a dense tensor optimization model we find a selective answer. A single induced matrix has a universal leading bulk that washes out the glassy organization. The difference of two matrices built from independent thermal samples in the same disorder does not: its spectrum gives an explicit image of the glassy Gibbs geometry, encoded by the distribution of mutual overlaps between samples. Parisi theory and Monte Carlo confirm this mechanism across simple and glassy phases.

</details>


### [107] [Learning as a Geometric Phase Transition: Renormalization Group Flow and Anisotropic Symmetry Breaking in Deep Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10770)
*G. Le Pera,C. Nordio*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper frames feature learning in deep neural networks as a geometric critical phenomenon using a lifted tensor-product metric. Learning is an instability of a target-isotropic fixed point, with Dyson expansions and Callan-Symanzik flow derived. Beta-functions emerge from asynchronous gradient updates creating metric strains. Depth RG flow governs transport and balance of curvature-like defects, producing heavy-tailed spectra via scale-free counting. Links to temporal stochastic training dynamics and weight Gram matrices are established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a geometric and renormalization-group perspective on feature learning in deep networks, moving beyond scalar overlap measures to capture the entangled forward-backward propagation structure.

Method: Formulates a lifted tensor-product learning metric (N_{0,L}) that encodes forward pullback survival and backward push-forward visibility. Identifies a target-isotropic fixed point whose instability signals learning. Derives discrete Dyson expansions for local anisotropic insertions and continuous Callan-Symanzik flow. Identifies beta-functions from microscopic kinematics: asynchronous gradient updates produce synchronous metric strains, whose traceless symmetric components act as curvature-like defects. A Wilsonian depth RG flow governs transport, balance, and coarse-grained irrelevance of these defects.

Result: Heavy-tailed spectra arise, under a scale-free counting hypothesis, as the spectrum of the target-active lifted geometry, with exponent addition in the matched pullback-push-forward sector. The depth RG picture is related to temporal stochastic training dynamics and the kinematic imprint on empirical weight Gram matrices.

Conclusion: Feature learning can be understood as a geometric critical phenomenon governed by an RG flow of curvature-like defects in the lifted metric, connecting microscopic gradient dynamics to macroscopic spectral properties and training dynamics.

Abstract: We formulate feature learning as a geometric critical phenomenon of the lifted tensor-product learning metric. The central object is not a scalar overlap, but the target-active geometry of \[ \mathcal N_{0,L}=\frac1N\sum_{r=1}^{L}Σ_{r\to L}\otimes T_{0\to r-1}, \] which entangles forward pullback survival with backward push-forward visibility. The neutral phase is target-isotropic: after restriction to endpoint target-active states and trace normalization, the lifted metric is proportional to the identity. Learning corresponds to an instability of this target-isotropic fixed point and to the emergence of traceless target-aligned eigentensors. We derive discrete Dyson expansions for local anisotropic insertions and their continuous Callan--Symanzik flow. Crucially, before constructing the full temporal mean-field theory, we identify the local spatial source of the $β$-functions directly from microscopic kinematics: asynchronous gradient updates generate synchronous metric strains, whose target-active symmetric traceless components act as curvature-like defects. The Wilsonian depth RG flow is then governed by the transport, balance, and coarse-grained irrelevance of these defects. Heavy-tailed spectra arise, under a scale-free counting hypothesis, as the spectrum of the target-active lifted geometry, with exponent addition in the matched pullback--push-forward sector. Finally, we relate this depth RG picture to temporal stochastic training dynamics and to the kinematic imprint of the learned channel on empirical weight Gram matrices.

</details>


### [108] [Analytical mobility edge in nonreciprocal quasiperiodic lattices with next-nearest-neighbor hopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10996)
*Wenmin Wang,Xiaosen Yang,Xianqi Tong*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper derives an analytical expression for the mobility edge in a 1D non-Hermitian Aubry-André model with nonreciprocal nearest-neighbor and next-nearest-neighbor hopping, showing that nonreciprocity renormalizes hopping amplitudes and alters the parabolic mobility edge, while spectral winding numbers bracket the coexistence region of extended and localized states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how nonreciprocal hopping (nearest-neighbor and next-nearest-neighbor) affects localization transitions and mobility edges in non-Hermitian quasiperiodic systems, and to connect these transitions to spectral topology.

Method: Extended Fermi-surface point-matching method to nonreciprocal hopping; derived closed-form expression for energy-dependent mobility edge; exact diagonalization for verification; spectral winding number analysis.

Result: The mobility edge is a single parabola in the energy–potential plane. Nearest-neighbor nonreciprocity rigidly shifts the boundary toward stronger potentials; next-nearest-neighbor nonreciprocity reduces curvature, broadening the coexistence window. Exact diagonalization confirms the analytical boundary. Spectral winding numbers at band edges bracket the mixed phase region.

Conclusion: Provides a compact analytical framework linking energy-dependent localization, spectral topology, and nonreciprocity in quasiperiodic lattices, directly testable in photonic, atomic, and electrical-circuit platforms.

Abstract: We investigate localization transitions and spectral topology in a one-dimensional non-Hermitian generalization of the Aubry-André model in which both the nearest-neighbor and the next-nearest-neighbor hopping amplitudes are nonreciprocal. By extending the Fermi-surface point-matching method to nonreciprocal hopping, we derive a closed-form expression for the energy-dependent mobility edge in which the two nonreciprocity parameters are absorbed into exponentially renormalized effective hopping amplitudes. The mobility edge forms a single parabola in the energy--potential plane: nearest-neighbor nonreciprocity rigidly shifts the localization boundary toward stronger potentials, whereas next-nearest-neighbor nonreciprocity reduces the curvature of the boundary and thereby broadens the energy window in which extended and localized states coexist. Exact diagonalization confirms the analytical boundary for purely nearest-neighbor, purely next-nearest-neighbor, and combined nonreciprocity, and recovers the known Hermitian mobility edge in the reciprocal limit. We further analyze the spectral topology under periodic boundary conditions and show that the spectral winding numbers evaluated at base energies near the two band edges directly bracket the mixed phase: the winding number at the lower band edge drops when the mobility edge enters the spectrum and the first localized states appear, while the winding number at the upper band edge drops when the last extended states localize, delineating the full potential-strength window over which extended and localized states coexist. These results provide a compact analytical framework that connects energy-dependent localization, spectral topology, and nonreciprocity in quasiperiodic lattices, and they are directly testable in photonic, atomic, and electrical-circuit platforms.

</details>


### [109] [Emergent quantum chaos from correlations on a random graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11662)
*Mrinal Sarkar,Valerio Pagni,Tilman Enss,Nicolò Defenu*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Long-range random bonds on a 1D lattice alone create quantum chaos and a localization transition in a noninteracting Hamiltonian, with GOE statistics at small σ and localization at larger σ, transitioning around σ_c ≈ 0.80–0.85, distinct from standard models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether sparse long-range random bonds (without on-site disorder or interactions) can generate quantum-chaotic spectral correlations and induce a localization transition in a single-particle system.

Method: A 1D ring model with Bernoulli-distributed long-range bonds (probability p_ij = d_{ij}^{-(1+σ)}), no on-site disorder, analyzed via level statistics (GOE vs Poisson) and compared with a Gaussian field theory retaining only mean/variance.

Result: Quantum chaos (GOE) at small σ, localization (Poisson) at larger σ, transition at σ_c ≈ 0.80–0.85, far above the mean hopping summability threshold (σ=0); Gaussian theory predicts σ=1, indicating higher cumulants are relevant.

Conclusion: The model belongs to a distinct universality class, differing from power-law random banded matrix and standard Anderson transition models.

Abstract: This work demonstrates that sparse long-range random bonds on a one-dimensional lattice alone can generate quantum-chaotic spectral correlations and also drive a localization transition in a noninteracting single-particle Hamiltonian. The model is a one-dimensional ring in which each pair of sites is connected independently with a probability $p_{ij}= d_{ij}^{-(1+σ)}$. Each bond carries identical unit hopping and on-site disorder is absent. Despite the absence of on-site disorder and interaction, the model displays quantum chaotic spectra with Gaussian orthogonal ensemble (GOE) level statistics at small $σ$ and localized eigenstates with Poisson statistics at larger $σ$. The transition occurs in the range $ 0.80 \lesssim σ_c \lesssim 0.85$, far above the summability threshold of the mean hopping profile ($σ=0$). A Gaussian field theory retaining only the mean and variance of the Bernoulli bonds instead predicts a threshold at $σ=1$, suggesting that higher cumulants are infrared-relevant. Our findings hint towards a universality class that is distinct from both the power-law random banded matrix model and the standard Anderson transition.

</details>


<div id='physics.comp-ph'></div>

# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [110] [Stochastic simulations of nonlinear reaction-diffusion equations using an exponential integrator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10065)
*Elliot J. Carr*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A novel stochastic simulation method for nonlinear reaction-diffusion equations using exponential integrator time discretization, which yields valid probabilities without strict time step constraints, demonstrated on Porous-Fisher models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the strict time step conditions required by commonly-employed time discretisation schemes when generating stochastic simulations from deterministic reaction-diffusion equations.

Method: Discretises reaction-diffusion equations in space and time using an exponential integrator for temporal discretisation, interpreting coefficients in the discretised system as probabilities for movement and reaction events. The matrix exponential entries define valid probabilities without restrictive time step constraints.

Result: The method produces valid probabilities without strict time step conditions, and simulation results for one- and two-dimensional Porous-Fisher type models confirm its veracity across multiple test problems.

Conclusion: The exponential integrator-based approach provides a more flexible and less restrictive stochastic simulation framework for nonlinear reaction-diffusion equations compared to traditional time discretisation schemes.

Abstract: Stochastic simulations can be generated from deterministic reaction-diffusion equations by discretising in space and time and interpreting coefficients in the resulting system of discretised equations as probabilities governing movement and reaction events. In this paper, we present a novel variant of this approach for nonlinear reaction-diffusion equations that employs an exponential integrator when discretising in time. The proposed method yields valid probabilities, defined by the entries of appropriate matrix functions, without the strict conditions on the time step required by a commonly-employed time discretisation scheme. Simulation results presented for one and two dimensional Porous-Fisher type models demonstrate the veracity of the method across several test problems.

</details>


### [111] [Functional Expansion Tallies of Matrix Operators for Prediction for Integrated Autocorrelation Time in Batch Monte Carlo: an Analytic 2D Scattering Chain Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10758)
*Jilang Miao,Guillaume L. Giudicelli,William Reed Kendrick*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates functional expansion tallies as a reduced-basis method for predicting inter-cycle correlations in Monte Carlo transport, demonstrating using an analytic benchmark that cosine and polynomial bases can estimate autocorrelation time efficiently with lower bias and cost than conventional discrete binning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a reduced-basis representation using functional expansion tallies that can efficiently predict inter-cycle correlations in Monte Carlo criticality calculations, avoiding the need to construct large discrete transition matrices.

Method: The authors compare a conventional discrete-cell Markov-chain estimator with a Galerkin reduced-order model built from Monte Carlo tallies of basis-function products, using an analytic two-dimensional isotropic scattering-chain benchmark with reflective boundaries. Cosine and polynomial bases are tested for convergence.

Result: The cosine basis converges rapidly to the exact result; polynomial bases show systematic convergence with increasing order. The reduced-basis approach achieves lower bias at comparable or lower solve cost compared to discrete binning.

Conclusion: Functional-expansion representations can provide an efficient path toward correlation prediction, uncertainty quantification, and future variance-reduction methods in Monte Carlo criticality calculations.

Abstract: We investigate functional expansion tallies as a reduced-basis representation for predicting inter-cycle correlations in Monte Carlo transport. Using an analytic two-dimensional isotropic scattering-chain benchmark with reflective boundaries, we compare a conventional discrete-cell Markov-chain estimator with a Galerkin reduced-order model built directly from Monte Carlo tallies of basis-function products. The reduced model estimates integrated autocorrelation time without first constructing a large discrete transition matrix. For the benchmark problem, the cosine basis converges rapidly to the exact result, while polynomial bases show systematic convergence with increasing order. Compared with discrete binning, the reduced-basis approach achieves lower bias at comparable or lower solve cost, suggesting that functional-expansion representations can provide an efficient path toward correlation prediction, uncertainty quantification, and future variance-reduction methods in Monte Carlo criticality calculations.

</details>


### [112] [Structure-preserving variational neural fields: Uncertainty-quantified reduced-order modeling of nonlinear conservation laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10965)
*Aviral Prakash,Marc L. Klasky*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a variational latent neural field framework that simultaneously provides uncertainty quantification and exact conservation-law preservation for reduced-order models of parameterized physical systems governed by nonlinear conservation laws. Three variants are proposed (IRS-UQ, PI-IRS-UQ, ECLEIRS-UQ), with the latter embedding solution dynamics within a conservation-law manifold via divergence-free representation. Numerical experiments on 1-D advection, 2-D Euler, and 2-D shallow water equations show accurate predictions with uncertainty estimates, robustness to sparse/noisy data, and improved robustness from conservation-structure preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing latent dynamics models for accelerating simulations of parameterized physical systems governed by nonlinear conservation laws suffer from two key limitations: they lack uncertainty estimates for predictions and do not guarantee adherence to underlying conservation laws. While these issues have been addressed separately, no unified framework exists that simultaneously provides uncertainty quantification and exact conservation-law preservation.

Method: A variational latent neural field framework integrating Gaussian process-inspired surrogates for predictive confidence estimation, with three variants: IRS-UQ (unconstrained), PI-IRS-UQ (physics-informed), and ECLEIRS-UQ (conservation-structure-preserving). Exact conservation preservation is achieved by embedding solution dynamics within a conservation-law manifold using a space-time divergence-free representation of the solution-flux field.

Result: The framework provides accurate predictions with uncertainty estimates and remains robust to sparse and noisy training data. Conservation-structure preserving latent representations (ECLEIRS-UQ) improve robustness to degraded training data while maintaining competitive predictive accuracy and uncertainty quantification capability compared to the other two variants.

Conclusion: The proposed variational latent neural field framework successfully unifies uncertainty quantification and exact conservation-law preservation for reduced-order modeling of parameterized nonlinear conservation laws, with the conservation-structure-preserving variant offering superior robustness under data degradation.

Abstract: Reduced-order models, such as latent dynamics models, are becoming mainstream for accelerating simulations for parameterized physical systems governed by nonlinear conservation laws. However, most existing latent dynamics frameworks suffer from two important limitations: they do not provide uncertainty estimates for model predictions, and they do not guarantee adherence to the underlying conservation laws. While these challenges have been addressed separately in prior work, a unified framework that simultaneously provides uncertainty quantification and exact conservation-law preservation remains largely unexplored. In this work, we develop a variational latent neural field framework that integrates Gaussian process-inspired surrogates, enabling estimation of predictive confidence for both in-distribution and out-of-distribution parameter regimes. Three variants of the framework are considered: IRS-UQ, PI-IRS-UQ, and ECLEIRS-UQ, corresponding to unconstrained, physics-informed, and conservation-structure-preserving formulations, respectively. Exact conservation-structure preservation is achieved by embedding the solution dynamics within a conservation-law manifold through a space-time divergence-free representation of the solution-flux field. We demonstrate the applicability of the framework through three numerical experiments: 1) 1-D advection, 2) 2-D Euler and 3) 2-D shallow water equations in parameterized settings. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the proposed approach provides accurate predictions together with uncertainty estimates, while remaining robust to sparse and noisy training data. Comparisons between the proposed three approaches show that conservation-structure preserving latent representations improve robustness to degraded training data while maintaining competitive predictive accuracy and uncertainty quantification capability.

</details>


### [113] [Understanding Chemical Short-Range Order in CoNiV via Mode Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10775)
*Jilang Miao,Miaomiao Jin*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Using machine-learned interatomic potentials and molecular dynamics, this study reveals that chemical short-range order (SRO) in equiatomic fcc NiCoV evolves more slowly than the radial structure, with dominant V-V avoidance in the first shell and V-V enrichment in the second shell (L1₂-like ordering), and identifies three collective ordering modes via PCA of bond probabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chemical short-range order (SRO) in multicomponent alloys influences properties but is often assumed to converge concurrently with the radial distribution function (RDF). The authors aim to test whether scalar RDF convergence truly indicates equilibrium SRO, using NiCoV as a model system.

Method: Machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP) for molecular-dynamics simulations of equiatomic fcc NiCoV; analysis via radial distribution functions, shell-resolved Warren-Cowley parameters, bond probabilities, lagged Jensen-Shannon divergence, and principal component analysis (PCA) of per-replica bond probabilities.

Result: V-V avoidance in the first coordination shell and V-V enrichment in the second shell, consistent with L1₂-like ordering; bond statistics relax slower than the RDF; PCA reveals three collective modes: V-sublattice ordering amplitude, Ni-Co redistribution, and Co-V exchange-like mode.

Conclusion: Scalar RDF convergence can mask continued chemical relaxation; shell-resolved bond statistics offer a compact and sensitive route to track SRO development in multicomponent alloys.

Abstract: We analyze chemical short-range order in equiatomic fcc NiCoV using molecular-dynamics snapshots generated with a machine-learned interatomic potential. Radial distribution functions identify stable coordination shells, while shell-resolved Warren-Cowley parameters and bond probabilities reveal continued chemical ordering after the radial structure has largely converged. The dominant signal is V-V avoidance in the first shell and V-V enrichment in the second shell, consistent with an L1$_2$-like local ordering tendency, while the third-shell response remains weak. Lagged Jensen-Shannon diagnostics show that bond statistics relax more slowly than the RDF. Principal component analysis of per-replica-centered bond probabilities resolves three collective modes: a V-sublattice ordering amplitude, a Ni-Co redistribution mode, and a Co-V exchange-like mode. These results show that scalar RDF convergence can miss slow chemical relaxation, and that shell-resolved bond statistics provide a compact route for tracking SRO development in multicomponent alloys.

</details>


### [114] [Compact and Stable Representation of Real-Frequency Spectral Functions for Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11190)
*Xinyang Dong,Ido Zemach,Lei Zhang,Guy Cohen,Emanuel Gull*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a compact moment representation for real-frequency Green's functions, hybridization functions, and self-energies using Cayley-mapped trigonometric moments, enabling efficient machine learning with physical constraints, benchmarked on DMFT and multi-orbital impurity models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the inefficiency of dense frequency grids and the ill-posed analytic continuation problems in Matsubara approaches when applying machine learning to real-frequency Green's functions and related quantities.

Method: A compact representation using Cayley-mapped trigonometric moments with Jacobian inclusion, preserving spectral-weight normalization, tying moments to positive matrix-valued spectral measures, and enabling pole representation via ESPRIT; combined with a graph-attention neural network using FiLM conditioning.

Result: Accuracy matching or exceeding direct frequency-domain learning across single-orbital DMFT, antiferromagnetic DMFT, and two-orbital impurity models; reliable density and staggered magnetization reproduction; stable self-energy reconstruction via Dyson equation inversion; accurate matrix-valued spectra recovery with orbital mixing.

Conclusion: The proposed moment representation provides a fixed-dimensional, physically constrained learning target that is both compact and stable, suitable for real-frequency quantum impurity problems in machine learning pipelines.

Abstract: We introduce a compact and stable moment representation for real-frequency Green's functions, hybridization functions, and self-energies for machine-learning applications, avoiding the inefficiency of dense frequency grids as well as the ill-posed analytic continuation of Matsubara approaches. The representation is constructed from Cayley-mapped trigonometric moments with the Jacobian included, which preserve spectral-weight normalization, tie the moment sequence to a positive matrix-valued spectral measure, and admit a systematic route to a pole representation via ESPRIT. This provides a fixed-dimensional learning target in which physical constraints such as normalization and positivity can be imposed directly. Using a graph-attention neural network with FiLM conditioning, we benchmark the representation on single-orbital DMFT, antiferromagnetic DMFT, and a two-orbital impurity model. The results demonstrate accuracy matching or exceeding that of direct frequency-domain learning, reliable reproduction of the density and staggered magnetization, stable self-energy reconstruction through Dyson equation inversion, and accurate recovery of matrix-valued spectra with orbital mixing.

</details>


### [115] [Uncertainty-Aware Structure-Property Mapping of Spinodoid Metamaterials via Heteroscedastic Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11209)
*Minwoo Park,Junseo Park,Mingyu Lee,Hugon Lee,Hanbin Cho,Ikjin Lee,Seunghwa Ryu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces an uncertainty-aware framework for spinodoid metamaterials that treats cone-angle descriptors as stochastic inputs, uses heteroscedastic Gaussian process regression to infer input-dependent property distributions, and demonstrates that accounting for aleatoric uncertainty is critical for reliability-based design optimization to meet prescribed reliability targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional structure-property mapping for spinodoid metamaterials treats cone-angle descriptors as deterministic representations of effective stiffness, ignoring the inherent stochastic nature of Gaussian random field-based topology generation where identical descriptors yield different morphology realizations and property scatter.

Method: The authors propose an uncertainty-aware framework using heteroscedastic Gaussian process regression (GPR) that infers input-dependent predictive uncertainty from sparse one-realization-per-point data without requiring empirical variance labels at every design point.

Result: Stiffness scatter varies across tensor components based on mechanically active directions; parameter sets with identical mean stiffness can exhibit different aleatoric uncertainty; deterministic optimization is highly susceptible to constraint violation under morphology-induced variability; homoscedastic RBDO fails to meet reliability targets, while heteroscedastic formulation succeeds.

Conclusion: Uncertainty-aware surrogate modeling is essential for reliability-aware inverse design of spinodoid metamaterials, establishing heteroscedastic GPR as a necessary tool for capturing morphology-induced variability in structure-property mappings.

Abstract: Spinodoid metamaterials offer a broad, tunable design space for anisotropic mechanical properties, yet their structure-property relationships are commonly treated as representative mappings from cone-angle descriptors to single effective stiffness values. This deterministic view overlooks the stochastic nature of Gaussian random field (GRF)-based topology generation, where identical cone-angle descriptors can produce different morphology realizations and property scatter. Here, we present an uncertainty-aware structure-property mapping framework that reinterprets cone-angle descriptors as stochastic descriptors associated with input-dependent property distributions. Using heteroscedastic Gaussian process regression (GPR), the framework infers input-dependent predictive uncertainty from sparse one-realization-per-point data without requiring empirical variance labels at every design point. The results show that stiffness scatter differs across tensor components according to each component's mechanically active directions, and that parameter sets yielding identical mean stiffness can carry different aleatoric uncertainty. Applying this uncertainty to reliability-based design optimization (RBDO), we show that a deterministic optimum is highly susceptible to constraint violation once morphology-induced variability is considered, and that a homoscedastic RBDO formulation fails to meet the prescribed reliability target - only the heteroscedastic formulation satisfies the reliability target under the heteroscedastic uncertainty evaluation. This establishes uncertainty-aware surrogate modeling as essential for reliability-aware inverse design of spinodoid metamaterials; extending the framework to nonlinear responses remains for future work.

</details>


### [116] [Toward AI-Agent-Driven Particle Transport Simulations: Implementation of AI-Assisted Workflows for PHITS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11309)
*Tatsuhiko Sato,Shintaro Hashimoto,Tatsuhiko Ogawa,Takuya Furuta,Yuho Hirata,Seiki Ohnishi,Yasuhito Sakaki,Nobuhiro Shigyo*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a code-side strategy to integrate AI assistants and AI agents with the PHITS Monte Carlo particle transport code by creating two AI-ready resources (a RAG knowledge base and a compact agent reference), demonstrating that general-purpose AI tools can handle complex PHITS workflows including input editing, simulation execution, error inspection, and result analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monte Carlo particle transport codes like PHITS are powerful but require substantial expertise in input preparation, execution, and result analysis; the authors aim to lower this barrier by enabling general-purpose AI tools to assist with these tasks without requiring dedicated code-specific applications.

Method: Two complementary AI-ready resources were prepared from manuals, lectures, sample inputs, utility info, and cautions: (1) a bundled knowledge base for RAG-based assistants loaded into NotebookLM, and (2) a compact agent reference for AI agents like Codex and Claude Code, combined with PHITS-specific policies and execution rules. Five demonstration tasks covered input modification, repeated simulations, parameter optimization, program compilation, post-processing, and result interpretation.

Result: AI agents successfully handled complex PHITS workflows when provided with appropriate resources and rules. Key practical lessons include the necessity of precise prompts, human verification, well-documented sample files, explicit execution policies, and command-line-accessible tools.

Conclusion: Bundling AI-ready resources with particle transport codes enables the use of general-purpose AI tools for complex workflows without requiring dedicated code-specific applications, suggesting a viable path for making Monte Carlo codes more accessible.

Abstract: Monte Carlo particle transport codes are powerful tools, but their use requires substantial knowledge of input preparation, execution, and result analysis. In this study, we present a code-side strategy for applying existing AI assistants and AI agents to PHITS. Two complementary sets of AI-ready resources were prepared from manuals, lecture materials, sample inputs, utility information, and developer-curated cautions: a bundled knowledge base for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)-based assistants and a compact agent reference for direct use by AI agents. The knowledge base was loaded into NotebookLM to provide conversational PHITS support, while the agent reference was combined with PHITS-specific policies and execution rules to enable Codex and Claude Code to edit input files, execute calculations, inspect errors, analyze results, and assist with source-code modification and compilation. Five demonstration tasks covered input modification, repeated simulations, parameter optimization, program compilation, post-processing, and result interpretation. The results showed that AI agents could handle complex PHITS workflows when appropriate resources and rules were provided. Practical lessons included precise prompts, human verification, well-documented sample files, explicit execution policies, and command-line-accessible tools. These findings support bundling AI-ready resources with particle transport codes to enable the use of general-purpose AI tools without requiring dedicated code-specific applications.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [117] [Geometric Universality and Thermodynamic Microstructure of Real Fluids in a Unified Entropic Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10076)
*Carlos E. Romero-Figueroa,Jose Miguel Ladino,Sasha A. Zaldivar,Hernando Quevedo*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified entropic framework for real fluids encompassing van der Waals, Berthelot, Redlich-Kwong, and Peng-Robinson equations of state, uses Geometrothermodynamics (GTD) to analyze curvature singularities and zero-curvature states, proposes dimensionless critical-amplitude ratios as universal classification parameters, and validates the geometric scaling behavior with Bayesian/MCMC methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a unified thermodynamic description of real fluids using an entropic framework that encompasses multiple classical equations of state, and to explore the microscopic interactions and critical phenomena through the lens of Geometrothermodynamics.

Method: The authors employ Geometrothermodynamics (GTD) to compute the scalar curvature R of the equilibrium manifold, introduce dimensionless critical-amplitude ratios Q^i_j, and use Bayesian inference with Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods to reconstruct zero-curvature curves statistically.

Result: Curvature singularities accurately reproduce macroscopic critical phenomena; vanishing curvature R=0 identifies states where attractive and repulsive forces balance; critical-amplitude ratios exhibit logarithmic size dependence but are universal invariants; posterior distributions from MCMC support the consistency of geometric scaling behavior.

Conclusion: The GTD manifold encodes non-trivial thermodynamic information, and the proposed critical-amplitude ratios serve as robust classification parameters for different molecular species based on criticality strength.

Abstract: We introduce a unified entropic framework for real fluids that encompasses the van der Waals, Berthelot, Redlich Kwong, and Peng Robinson equations of state within a common thermodynamic description. The corresponding microscopic interactions are then explored using Geometrothermodynamics, GTD, through the scalar curvature $mathcal{R}$ of the equilibrium manifold. We show that curvature singularities accurately reproduce macroscopic critical phenomena, while vanishing curvature $\mathcal{R}=0$ identifies specific thermodynamic states where attractive and repulsive intermolecular forces effectively balance. Furthermore, we introduce a set of dimensionless critical-amplitude ratios $Q^i_{j}$, which reveal universal geometric features of the critical regime. Although individual critical amplitudes exhibit a logarithmic dependence on the system size, these invariant ratios organize different molecular species according to the strength of criticality and encode universal scaling features, suggesting their potential as robust classification parameters. Finally, employing Bayesian inference and Markov Chain Monte Carlo, MCMC methods, we statistically reconstruct the zero-curvature curves. The posterior distributions support the consistency of the geometric scaling behavior, demonstrating that the GTD manifold encodes non-trivial information about the underlying thermodynamical models.

</details>


### [118] [Revisiting the non-equilibrium phase transitions of the continuous-trait Axelrod model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10381)
*Sandro M. Reia,Paulo R. A. Campos,José F. Fontanari*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper re-examines phase transitions in the continuous-trait Axelrod model, showing that the previously claimed continuous behavior for F=2 is a finite-size artifact. Using median scaling and distribution analysis, they find a genuinely hybrid transition with a small latent jump at criticality, while F=3 exhibits a standard first-order transition. The framework is validated on the discrete Poisson variant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve inconsistencies in the literature regarding the nature of phase transitions in the continuous-trait Axelrod model—specifically whether the transition is hybrid or purely continuous—by addressing finite-size masking effects that obscured the true discontinuous nature of the ordered phase.

Method: The authors shift analysis from mean domain density μ to the median \tildeμ and examine full probability distributions P(μ) across simulation runs. They apply finite-size scaling and power-law analysis near criticality for F=2 and F=3 continuous-trait Axelrod models, and extend the framework to the discrete Poisson variant.

Result: For F=2, the transition is hybrid with a latent jump μ_c ≈ 0.089 at d_c ≈ 0.0784 and a mean-field exponent β ≈ 1/2. For F=3, the transition is a traditional first-order (non-hybrid) transition. The discrete Poisson variant confirms known results: continuous for F=2 and discontinuous non-hybrid for F=3.

Conclusion: The continuous-trait Axelrod model exhibits dimensionality-dependent transition types. The hybrid nature for F=2 is genuine but was previously masked by finite-size effects. The work provides a unified classification scheme for phase transitions across Axelrod-like systems.

Abstract: We investigate the non-equilibrium phase transitions of the continuous-trait Axelrod model, an agent-based framework where individual culture is represented by a vector of $F$ continuous features confined to the interval $(0,1)$. Local interactions are governed by a metric similarity threshold $d$, which acts as a continuous control parameter of social tolerance. The dynamics inevitably freeze into one of two absorbing configuration classes: an ordered, homogeneous monocultural state at high tolerance, or a highly fragmented, disordered state at low tolerance. While previous studies characterized the transition as hybrid based on the continuous behavior of the domain density $μ$ alongside a discontinuous jump in the largest domain fraction $ρ$, we show that this apparent continuity is an artifact of severe finite-size masking effects. By shifting the methodological focus to the scaling of the median $\tildeμ$ and analyzing the full probability distributions $P(μ)$, we unveil a clear bimodal structure with disjoint maxima across independent simulation runs. Our results reveal that for $F=2$, the system undergoes a genuinely hybrid transition in the contemporary sense, featuring a tiny but finite latent jump ($μ_c \approx 0.089$) at the critical threshold $d_c \approx 0.0784$ while scaling toward it from below via a non-analytical power law with a mean-field exponent $β\approx 1/2$. Conversely, for $F=3$, the higher trait-space dimensionality suppresses local fluctuations, yielding a traditional, non-hybrid first-order transition. We apply this framework to the alternative discrete Poisson variant of the model, successfully confirming its known continuous transition for $F=2$ and discontinuous, non-hybrid transition for $F=3$, thereby establishing a unified characterization of phase transitions in Axelrod-like systems.

</details>


### [119] [A Cascade of Volterra-Operator BBP Transitions in a Correlated Wigner Matrix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10503)
*Masato Hisakado*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A Wigner-type random matrix with row-column correlated entries shows that, despite bulk spectral moments matching the semicircle law, the correlation matrix produces a countable family of outlier eigenvalues converging to singular values of a Volterra operator (from Brownian motion's Karhunen–Loève expansion). Each singular value drives an independent BBP transition, creating a discrete hierarchy of critical points—rather than a single transition—as coupling increases, generalizable to broader correlation structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how off-diagonal correlations (via a shared row-column random factor) affect the eigenvalue structure of large random matrices beyond the standard Wigner ensemble, especially the emergence of outlier eigenvalues and multiple phase transitions.

Method: Analytical derivation of the correlation matrix's spectral decomposition, linking it to the Karhunen–Loève expansion of Brownian motion (Volterra integral operator). Numerical diagonalization confirms the top twenty singular values to within 1% accuracy. BBP theory is applied per singular value to analyze the transitions as coupling strength increases.

Result: Bulk spectral moments match the semicircle law, but the correlation matrix has a vanishing bulk plus a countable family of outliers. These outliers converge to singular values of a Volterra operator. Each singular value triggers an independent BBP transition, yielding a discrete hierarchy of critical points where eigenvalues detach from the semicircle edge one by one.

Conclusion: Row-column correlations in Wigner-type matrices produce a countable set of outlier eigenvalues and multiple BBP transitions, generalizing to broader correlation families where the critical hierarchy is set by the spectrum of an associated compact integral operator.

Abstract: We study a Wigner-type random matrix in which the off-diagonal correlation between entries is generated by a random factor shared among all entries in a given row and column, with the coupling strength held fixed as the matrix size grows. Although the bulk spectral moments remain those of the pure semicircle law, we show that the underlying correlation matrix decomposes into a vanishing bulk together with a countable family of outlier eigenvalues that, at fixed rank $k$, converge to the singular values of a compact Volterra (cumulative-sum) integral operator -- obtained in closed form via the classical Karhunen--Loève expansion of Brownian motion and confirmed numerically to better than one percent across the top twenty such values. Each singular value drives an independent Baik--Ben Arous--Péché (BBP) transition as the coupling strength increases, producing an evenly spaced, discrete hierarchy of critical points -- rather than a single transition -- at each of which one further eigenvalue detaches from the semicircle edge, in close agreement with direct diagonalization. We show that this mechanism generalizes to a broader family of correlation structures, with the critical hierarchy in every case set by the spectrum of an associated compact integral operator.

</details>
