### [120] [Dimensional and Spin Interpolation for the O$(n)$ Model: From Exact Anchors to RG-Improved Critical Exponents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10865)
*Kumar Ghosh*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A two-axis interpolation framework treating spatial dimension D and spin-component number n as continuous parameters is developed for the O(n) universality family. It yields accurate predictions for 3D Ising critical couplings and exponents without adjustable parameters, and clarifies conditions for interpolation success based on monotonicity of observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a unified, predictive framework for critical phenomena that interpolates between known exact solutions (e.g., Onsager at D=2, mean-field at D→∞, spherical limit for n→∞) along both the spatial dimension axis and the spin-component axis, while identifying structural conditions under which such interpolation is valid.

Method: Two-axis interpolation: (1) Spatial axis: anchors between D=2 (Onsager) and D→∞ (mean-field) using Wilson-Fisher-constrained polynomial interpolation. (2) Spin axis: anchors between n=1 (Ising) and n→∞ (spherical model) using monotonicity criterion and perturbative 1/n² expansion.

Result: 3D Ising: K_c=0.2204 (benchmark 0.22165), ν=2/3, β=31/96, η=35/864 (benchmarks: 0.6299, 0.3265, 0.0362). O(3): ν=0.7493 (benchmark 0.7112), β=0.3797 (0.3689), γ=1.489 (1.396). O(2.5): ν=0.7143 predicted.

Conclusion: Dimensional and spin interpolation is a unified, predictive approach to critical phenomena. Success requires monotonicity of observables between anchor points (satisfied by ν(n) but violated by K_c(n)).

Abstract: We develop a two-axis interpolation framework for the O$(n)$ universality family, treating the spatial dimension $D$ and the spin-component number $n$ as independent continuous parameters connecting exact limiting solutions. On the spatial axis, anchoring between the Onsager solution at $D=2$ and mean-field theory at $D\to\infty$ yields a closed-form prediction for the 3D Ising critical coupling that agrees well with Monte Carlo benchmarks $K_c = 0.2204$ (benchmark: $0.22165$) with no adjustable parameters. Wilson--Fisher-constrained polynomial interpolation gives $ν=2/3$, $β=31/96$, and $η=35/864$ at $D=3$ (benchmarks: $0.6299$, $0.3265$, $0.0362$), and reproduces conformal-bootstrap results across $3 \le D < 4$. On the spin axis, we establish a necessary compatibility criterion: two-anchor interpolation succeeds only for observables that vary monotonically between the anchor values. The critical coupling $K_c(n)$ violates this criterion because the Heisenberg value falls below the spherical limit, whereas the correlation-length exponent $ν(n)$ satisfies it. A perturbative $1/n^2$ expansion yields $ν(3) = 0.7493$ (benchmark: $0.7112$), and propagation through exact scaling relations gives $β(3) = 0.3797$ (benchmark: $0.3689$) and $γ(3) = 1.489$ (benchmark: $1.396$), without introducing additional parameters. The framework naturally extends to non-integer spin, producing the prediction $ν(2.5) = 0.7143$ for the O$(2.5)$ universality class. These results establish dimensional and spin interpolation as a unified and predictive approach to critical phenomena, while clarifying the structural conditions under which interpolation succeeds.

</details>


### [121] [Finite-time Scaling of the surface special transition in a 3D classical Heisenberg model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11066)
*Dongxu Liu,Zhe Wang,Shuai Yin,Zheng Yan*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper studies nonequilibrium driving across the special surface phase transition in the 3D classical Heisenberg model with open boundaries. Using Monte Carlo simulations of four protocols, it finds that when driving from the extraordinary-log critical state, the finite-time scaling relation acquires a logarithmic correction: $M^{2}_{s}\propto R^{(1+η_{s})/r_{s}}[\log(LR^{1/η_{s}})]^{-q}$, extending boundary finite-time scaling beyond conventional power-law initial conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand nonequilibrium driven dynamics across the special surface phase transition in the 3D Heisenberg model, particularly how extraordinary-log boundary critical states (characterized by logarithmic decay of correlations) alter finite-time scaling behavior compared to conventional power-law critical states.

Method: Monte Carlo simulations of four driving protocols: (1) temperature heating across the special transition, (2) temperature cooling across the special transition, (3) surface-coupling ramps from the ordinary critical state into the special point, and (4) surface-coupling ramps from the extraordinary-log critical state into the special point.

Result: When driving from the extraordinary-log critical state, the large-rate scaling relation acquires a logarithmic correction: $M^{2}_{s}\propto R^{(1+η_{s})/r_{s}}[\log(LR^{1/η_{s}})]^{-q}$, where $R$ is driving rate, $L$ system size, $η_{s}$ surface anomalous dimension, $r_{s}$ scaling dimension of $R$, and $q$ exponent governing logarithmic boundary criticality. Excellent data collapse over wide ranges of system sizes and driving rates was achieved.

Conclusion: Extraordinary-log initial states alter nonequilibrium critical scaling, extending boundary finite-time scaling beyond conventional power-law initial conditions. The logarithmic correction form follows from the general FTS framework by incorporating logarithmic initial-state memory.

Abstract: We investigate nonequilibrium driven dynamics across the special surface phase transition in the three-dimensional classical Heisenberg model with open boundaries, where tuning the surface coupling gives access to an extraordinary-log boundary critical state characterized by logarithmic, rather than power-law, decay of correlations. Using Monte Carlo simulations, we realize four driving protocols: temperature heating and cooling across the special transition, and surface-coupling ramps from the ordinary and extraordinary-log critical states into the special point. For temperature-driven protocols, the surface order parameter obeys a generalization of the finite-time scaling (FTS) and the Kibble-Zurek mechanism. The central finding emerges when the system is driven from the extraordinary-log critical state: the large-rate scaling relation acquires a logarithmic correction and takes the novel form $M^{2}_{s}\propto R^{(1+η_{s})/r_{s}}[\log(LR^{1/η_{s}})]^{-q}$ , where $R$ is the driving rate, $L$ the system size, $η_{s}$ the surface anomalous dimension, $r_{s}$ the scaling dimension of $R$, and $q$ the exponent governing the logarithmic boundary criticality. We demonstrate that this form follows from the general FTS framework by incorporating the logarithmic initial-state memory, and we achieve excellent data collapse over a wide range of system sizes and driving rates. Our results establish that extraordinary-log initial states alter nonequilibrium critical scaling, extending boundary FTS beyond conventional power-law initial conditions.

</details>


### [122] [Harnessing finite-size effects to gauge aging in the $2D$ Ising model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11524)
*Dustin Warkotsch,Malte Henkel,Wolfhard Janke*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The relaxation of the 2D Ising model is studied through the two-time autocorrelator C(t,s). Finite-size effects cause saturation with a plateau scaling algebraically with waiting time s and lattice size L, enabling precise estimates of exponents λ and z from small lattices. Smooth domain walls act like premature finite-size effects, extending the analysis to non-equilibrium systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand relaxation behavior towards equilibrium in the 2D Ising model, focusing on finite-size effects in the two-time autocorrelator, and to accurately extract critical exponents using deliberately small lattices.

Method: Analyzes the two-time autocorrelator C(t,s) in the 2D Ising model with nearest-neighbor interactions. Uses scaling relations of the plateau height C_{\infty}^{(2)}(s,L) with waiting time s and lattice size L to estimate the autocorrelation exponent λ and dynamical exponent z. Treats smooth domain walls analogously to lattice boundaries to understand their effect as premature finite-size phenomena.

Result: Derived precise estimates for the autocorrelation exponent λ and dynamical exponent z from small lattices using scaling relations. Showed that smooth domain walls can be treated as finite-size effects, extending the ansatz to non-equilibrium systems.

Conclusion: Finite-size effects in the 2D Ising model can be exploited to accurately extract critical exponents from small systems, and domain wall effects can be modeled as premature finite-size phenomena, broadening the applicability of the approach.

Abstract: The relaxation behavior towards equilibrium of the $2D$ Ising model with nearest-neighbor interactions has been studied with focus on the two-time autocorrelator $C(t,s)$. Finite-size effects affecting the growing magnetic domains lead to the saturation of $C(t,s)$ with a distinct plateau of height $C_{\infty}^{(2)}(s,L)$ scaling algebraically with waiting time $s$ and lattice size $L$. These scaling relations are used to produce precise estimates for the autocorrelation exponent $λ$ and dynamical exponent $z$ with deliberately small lattices. Treating smooth domain walls in a similar manner to the lattice boundaries, their effect on $C(t,s)$ can be understood as premature finite-size phenomenon, extending our ansatz to systems not yet in equilibrium.

</details>


### [123] [Thermal phase transitions in a mixed-spin Ising model on the Lieb lattice: Exact results beyond zero magnetic field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11661)
*Jozef Strecka,Katarina Karlova*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper exactly solves a mixed spin-1/2 and spin-1 Ising model on a decorated square (Lieb) lattice with single-ion anisotropy and magnetic field by mapping it to an effective spin-1/2 Ising model. The ground-state analysis reveals three phases (ferrimagnetic, disordered, ferromagnetic), with a dome-shaped discontinuous transition boundary terminated by Ising critical points. Reentrant phase transitions are found and verified by Monte Carlo simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the ground-state and finite-temperature properties of a mixed spin-1/2 and spin-1 Ising model on a decorated square (Lieb) lattice with uniaxial single-ion anisotropy and magnetic field, and to identify the exact solvable parameter regime.

Method: Generalized decoration-iteration transformation mapping the model exactly onto an effective spin-1/2 Ising model on a square lattice with effective nearest-neighbor interaction and effective field. Classical Monte Carlo simulations for independent verification.

Result: Three ground-state phases identified: ferrimagnetic (FRI), disordered (DP), and ferromagnetic (FM). Dome-shaped surface of discontinuous thermal phase transitions between FRI and DP phases terminated by Ising-type critical points. Two consecutive discontinuous thermally-induced reentrant phase transitions DP-FRI-DP found in a narrow parameter region.

Conclusion: The model is exactly solvable for finite magnetic fields when the effective field vanishes. Both continuous and discontinuous thermal phase transitions belong to this exactly solvable regime. Analytical predictions, including reentrance and field-/thermally-driven transitions, are verified by Monte Carlo simulations.

Abstract: We investigate the ground-state and finite-temperature properties of a mixed spin-1/2 and spin-1 Ising model on a decorated square (Lieb) lattice incorporating a uniaxial single-ion anisotropy and magnetic field. By employing the generalized decoration-iteration transformation, the model is mapped exactly onto an effective spin-1/2 Ising model on the square lattice characterized by an effective nearest-neighbor interaction and an effective field. The studied model consequently becomes exactly solvable even for finite values of the applied magnetic field whenever the effective field vanishes. The ground-state analysis reveals three distinct phases: ferrimagnetic phase (FRI), disordered phase (DP), and ferromagnetic (FM) phase. The ground-state boundary between FRI and DP phases gives rise to a dome-shaped surface of discontinuous thermal phase transitions, which is terminated by a line of Ising-type critical points associated with continuous thermal phase transitions. Both continuous and discontinuous thermal phase transitions belong to the exactly solvable parameter regime defined by a vanishing effective field in spite of the fact that the applied magnetic field is finite. Two consecutive discontinuous thermally-induced reentrant phase transitions DP-FRI-DP are identified in a narrow parameter region. The exact analytical predictions including reentrance, field- and thermally-driven phase transitions are independently verified by classical Monte Carlo simulations.

</details>


### [124] [Still life in a classic Blume-Capel model: pseudo-transitions in a spin-1 diamond chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11669)
*Elham Shahhosseini Shahrabadi,Majid Moradi,Jozef Strecka*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Using the transfer-matrix method, the study exactly analyzes a spin-1 Blume-Capel diamond chain in a magnetic field, revealing entropically-driven pseudo-transitions—abrupt but continuous changes in magnetization and entropy with sharp peaks in susceptibility and specific heat—near phase boundaries and triple points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the class of one-dimensional spin systems exhibiting pseudo-transitions by investigating whether such phenomena can emerge in the classical spin-1 Blume-Capel model.

Method: Exact transfer-matrix method applied to a spin-1 Blume-Capel diamond chain in a magnetic field, with construction of ground-state phase diagrams and finite-temperature analysis near phase boundaries and triple points.

Result: Discovery of entropically-driven pseudo-transitions: abrupt but continuous changes in magnetization and entropy resembling jumps, with exceptionally sharp but finite peaks in magnetic susceptibility and specific heat, originating from an extremely small energy gap between a nondegenerate ground state and macroscopically degenerate low-lying excited states.

Conclusion: Pseudo-transitions can emerge in the classical spin-1 Blume-Capel model, thereby broadening the family of one-dimensional spin systems known to display such phenomena.

Abstract: We exactly investigate the ground-state, magnetic, and thermodynamic properties of a spin-1 Blume-Capel diamond chain in a magnetic field by means of the transfer-matrix method. After establishing the complete ground-state phase diagram and characterizing each ground-state spin configuration, we examine the finite-temperature behavior in the vicinity of selected phase boundaries and triple points. It is demonstrated that an extremely small energy gap between a nondegenerate ground state and competing macroscopically degenerate low-lying excited states gives rise to entropically-driven pseudo-transitions. These pseudo-transitions manifest themselves through abrupt but continuous changes in the magnetization and entropy resembling discontinuous jumps, while the magnetic susceptibility and specific heat exhibit exceptionally sharp yet finite peaks resembling divergences. Our results provide the exact evidence that pseudo-transitions can also emerge in the classical spin-1 Blume-Capel model and thereby extend the class of one-dimensional spin systems known to display pseudo-transitions.

</details>


### [125] [Slow is fast: raising barriers to accelerate thermal relaxation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11877)
*Yubo Wang,Zhiyue Lu*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Accelerating relaxation in reversible systems requires more than just lowering all barriers (open gates); optimal finite-time control uses noncommutative, time-dependent scheduling (bang-bang) that leverages eigenvector rotation to outperform static landscapes, achieving significantly faster relaxation. Conversely, delaying relaxation also benefits from similar scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the intuitive strategy of lowering all kinetic barriers (open all gates) is truly optimal for accelerating relaxation to equilibrium, and to explore counter-intuitive finite-time control strategies beyond this intuition.

Method: The authors analyze relaxation at four levels: local conductance, f-divergence speed, eigenvalue optimization, and eigenvector rotation. They employ noncommutative generator scheduling, demonstrate bang-bang optimality, and prove a no-go theorem that commuting generators collapse to static control.

Result: The all-open-gate strategy is optimal at three levels (conductance, f-divergence, eigenvalues) but not at the fourth (eigenvector rotation). The best-found bang-bang schedule, including counter-gating (transiently raising barriers), reduces terminal residual by a factor of 130 vs. all-open and 7 vs. best static landscape. Noncommutativity is necessary for improvement.

Conclusion: Barrier control can accelerate or delay relaxation without performing work, merely re-timing a fixed dissipation budget. Noncommutative, time-dependent scheduling provides a powerful new lever for controlling relaxation dynamics in finite time.

Abstract: For a reversible system relaxing to equilibrium, the obvious fastest strategy is to lower all kinetic barriers (open all gates). We find that such intuition holds at three levels: the all-open-gate strategy achieves the highest local conductance, it maximizes the instantaneous speed of approach in every $f$-divergence, and it simultaneously maximizes all relaxation eigenvalues. Nevertheless, we show that a counter-intuitive finite-time optimum lies beyond this intuition and operates at a fourth level, invisible to all three: eigenvector rotation. Noncommutativity enables timed schedules to reproject residual amplitudes across relaxation modes, thereby achieving faster relaxation. Optimal schedules are bang--bang. In our illustrative example, the best-found schedule also employs counter-gating, transiently raising selected barriers, and reduces the terminal residual by a factor of $130$ relative to all-open, and by $7$ relative to the best static landscape. A no-go theorem shows that noncommutativity is necessary: commuting generators collapse every schedule to a static time-averaged landscape, worse than the intuitive static control. In the reverse problem, the dual schedule preserves nonequilibrium free energy far more effectively than intuitively keeping all barriers at maximum heights. Whether accelerating or delaying relaxation, barrier control performs no work on the reduced Markov system; it only re-times a fixed total dissipation budget.

</details>


### [126] [A Fokker-Planck approach to a stochastic multiplicative wealth model with taxation and redistribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11755)
*Iago Nascimento Barros,Marcelo Lobato Martins,Celia Anteneodo*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A Fokker-Planck framework for wealth distribution dynamics under multiplicative growth, taxation, and redistribution is developed, with analytical stationary distributions derived and validated via simulations, showing that nonuniform redistribution schemes can reduce inequality as measured by the Gini index.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend P.M.C. de Oliveira's stochastic multiplicative economic growth model by incorporating general redistribution protocols, enabling a theoretical framework to study how taxation and redistribution policies affect wealth inequality.

Method: A Fokker-Planck description is derived for wealth dynamics under multiplicative noise, taxation, and state-dependent redistribution protocols. Analytical stationary distributions are obtained and validated with agent-based simulations. Inequality is quantified using the Gini index.

Result: Analytical expressions for stationary wealth distributions are derived, revealing how multiplicative noise, taxation, and redistribution jointly determine inequality. Nonuniform redistribution schemes (e.g., conditional cash transfer protocols) significantly reduce wealth disparities compared to uniform protocols.

Conclusion: The Fokker-Planck approach successfully captures the interplay of multiplicative growth and redistribution, and specific nonuniform transfer mechanisms can effectively mitigate wealth inequality.

Abstract: We develop a Fokker-Planck description of the dynamics of wealth distribution in a stochastic multiplicative economic growth model with taxation and redistribution, as introduced by P.M.C. de Oliveira. Extending the original formulation, our theoretical framework includes general redistribution protocols, encompassing a broad class of state-dependent transfer mechanisms. As a particular case, we investigate a two-state protocol designed to emulate conditional cash transfer programs. Analytical expressions for the stationary wealth distributions are derived, revealing how the interplay between multiplicative noise, taxation, and redistribution shapes the emergence of inequality. The theoretical results are corroborated by agent-based simulations. To quantify and compare the impact of the different protocols, we employ the Gini index as a measure of inequality. Our analysis highlights how specific nonuniform redistribution schemes can significantly mitigate wealth disparities.

</details>


### [127] [Extending the Mpemba effect to the underdamped realm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11797)
*Shahaf Aharony Shapira,Gene Chen,Marija Vucelja,Oren Raz*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper extends the study of the Mpemba effect (hotter systems cooling faster than colder ones) from overdamped to underdamped regimes where inertia is non-negligible. Using perturbative analysis and numerical simulations, the authors show the effect persists from overdamped to moderate damping, disappears for ultra-weak damping in smooth single-well potentials, but can re-emerge in more complex potentials like double-well systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the Mpemba effect, previously demonstrated in overdamped (high-friction) classical systems, exists in underdamped regimes where inertia plays a significant role, thereby broadening the theoretical understanding of the effect beyond the overdamped limit.

Method: Perturbative analysis of underdamped Brownian dynamics in a potential, examining finite damping corrections to the overdamped limit; analytical reasoning for the ultra-weak-damping limit; and numerical simulations using double-well potentials.

Result: 1) If the Mpemba effect exists in the overdamped limit, it persists for sufficiently large but finite damping. 2) In the ultra-weak-damping limit, the effect cannot occur for smooth confining single-well potentials with canonical initial states. 3) The effect can arise in more complex potentials (e.g., double-well) even in the underdamped regime.

Conclusion: The Mpemba effect is not restricted to overdamped systems; it can occur in underdamped dynamics, provided the damping is not too weak and/or the potential is sufficiently complex (like double-well potentials).

Abstract: The Mpemba effect is the counterintuitive phenomenon in which an initially hotter system cools faster than a colder, otherwise identical system. It has been experimentally demonstrated in various classical overdamped systems. Here, we explore the existence of the same effect in a regime where inertia cannot be neglected, namely, the underdamped regime. We consider the underdamped dynamics of a Brownian particle in a potential. We show perturbatively that, if the effect exists in the overdamped limit, it persists for sufficiently large but finite damping. In the ultra-weak-damping limit, we show that the effect cannot occur for smooth confining single-well potentials with canonical initial states, but can arise in more complex potentials. We demonstrate our results numerically using double-well potentials, the canonical setting for the Mpemba effect in the overdamped limit.

</details>

### [128] [Accessing 100 GHz Mechanical Modes in Bulk Crystals at Cryogenic Temperatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10737)
*Boxuan Tian,Jiacheng Xie,Hong X. Tang*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that bulk lithium niobate crystals coupled with 3D microwave cavities can host mechanical modes near 100 GHz, achieving strong coupling between microwave photons and mechanical phonons with cooperativity up to 16.6 at 110 GHz and mechanical quality factors up to 30,000 at W band, enabling massive high-frequency mechanical modes for quantum physics at elevated temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore high-frequency (sub-terahertz) mechanical resonators using bulk crystals for quantum electromechanics, overcoming the limitations of traditional microfabricated structures and enabling mechanical quantum motion studies at experimentally friendly Kelvin temperatures.

Method: The authors couple bulk lithium niobate crystals with plug-and-play three-dimensional microwave cavities, enabling non-contact excitation of centimeter-scale, milligram-mass vibrational modes across 7.0–110 GHz. A frequency-tunable superconducting niobium cavity at 4 K is used to demonstrate strong coupling between cavity modes and multiple mechanical modes.

Result: Achieved mechanical quality factors up to 30,000 at W band, strong coupling with cooperativity up to 16.6 at 110 GHz, and coherent energy exchange between microwave photons and mechanical phonons across 7.0–110 GHz.

Conclusion: This establishes a versatile platform for accessing massive high-frequency mechanical modes and for precision tests of mechanical quantum physics at elevated (Kelvin) temperatures.

Abstract: Sub-terahertz electromechanics offers a promising route to probe mechanical quantum motion at experimentally friendly Kelvin temperatures. Traditionally, high-frequency mechanical resonators rely on advanced microfabrication to shape complex microstructures, while bulk crystals have been largely overlooked due to their large inertia and challenging transduction at such frequencies. Here we show that bulk lithium niobate can host mechanically accessible modes near 100 GHz when coupled via plug-and-play three-dimensional microwave cavities. This approach enables efficient, non-contact excitation of centimeter-scale, milligram-mass vibrational modes across 7.0--110 GHz, with mechanical quality factors up to 30,000 at W band. Furthermore, using a frequency-tunable superconducting niobium cavity at 4 K, we demonstrate strong coupling between a microwave cavity mode and multiple mechanical modes, enabling coherent energy exchange between microwave photons and mechanical phonons with cooperativity up to 16.6 at 110 GHz. These results establish a versatile platform for accessing massive high-frequency mechanical modes and for precision tests of mechanical quantum physics at elevated temperatures.

</details>


### [129] [SpectraSensML Software: Mastering Complete Spectral Information for Luminescence Thermometry 2.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10807)
*Aleksandar Ćirić,Zoran Ristić,Tamara Gavrilović,Anđela Rajčić,Snežana Đurković,Željka Antić,Miroslav D. Dramićanin*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Luminescence Thermometry 2.0 (LT 2.0), a paradigm shift from conventional single-feature-based thermometry to full-spectrum machine learning regression. Using the SpectraSensML platform on Yb3+-doped phosphor emission (125-700 K), nineteen regression algorithms are benchmarked, with a sensor-fusion estimator achieving 0.36 K RMSE—a seven-fold improvement over the best luminescence intensity ratio method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional luminescence thermometry methods exploit only isolated spectral features (e.g., intensity ratios, bandwidth, line shift, lifetime), discarding the majority of temperature-relevant information encoded in the full emission spectrum, thus remaining fundamentally limited.

Method: The authors propose LT 2.0 using the SpectraSensML platform, where machine learning regression algorithms operate on the entire emission spectrum. Nineteen regression algorithms from four families (tree ensembles, physics-aware regression, kernel/instance methods, neural networks) are systematically benchmarked on Yb3+-doped phosphor emission across 125-700 K.

Result: A sensor-fusion estimator combining the first three principal components achieves 0.36 K RMSE, a seven-fold improvement over the best LIR variant. Multi-component regressors reduce temperature uncertainty by nearly an order of magnitude compared to single-component approaches. Decision-tree ensembles fail on unseen temperatures due to piecewise-constant prediction limitations.

Conclusion: Full-spectrum machine learning regression represents a paradigm shift in luminescence thermometry, dramatically outperforming conventional single-feature methods. The open-source SpectraSensML platform is released to enable reproducible community benchmarks.

Abstract: Luminescence thermometry has evolved through decades of research focused on optimising materials and on extracting temperature information from isolated spectral features such as luminescence intensity ratios, bandwidth, line shift and excited-state lifetime. Despite extensive material development, these conventional methods remain fundamentally limited by construction: only a small subset of pre-selected spectral features is exploited, while the bulk of the temperature-relevant information encoded in the full spectrum is systematically discarded. A paradigm shift is presented here, Luminescence Thermometry 2.0 (LT 2.0), implemented through the newly developed SpectraSensML platform, in which machine learning regression operates on the entire emission spectrum to deliver temperature readout. The approach is demonstrated on a Yb3+-doped phosphor emitting in the near-infrared biological transparency window across 125 to 700 K. Nineteen regression algorithms drawn from four families, namely tree ensembles, physics-aware regression models, kernel and instance methods, and neural networks, are systematically benchmarked. A sensor-fusion estimator that combines the first three principal components reaches an root-mean-square error of 0.36 K, a seven-fold improvement over the best luminescence intensity ratio variant. Single-component approaches are shown to be quantitatively sub-optimal: multi-component regressors that exploit the first three principal components reduce the temperature uncertainty by close to an order of magnitude. The structural reason behind the failure of decision-tree ensembles on unseen temperatures is explained: their piecewise-constant predictions cannot interpolate beyond training set-points. The open-source SpectraSensML application used to obtain the results is released alongside the manuscript to enable reproducible community benchmarks.

</details>


### [130] [Multiple Band-Gaps through the Coupling of Unit Cells from the Same Metamaterial: the Dual Cell method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10916)
*Plastiras Demetriou*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: Coupling two identical unit cells of a mechanical metamaterial into a dual-cell configuration can create a new metamaterial with enhanced wave attenuation, producing multiple directional and omnidirectional band-gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether coupling two unit cells of the same mechanical metamaterial into a dual-unit-cell configuration can produce enhanced wave attenuation capabilities compared to the original single-cell metamaterial.

Method: The study examines two coupling configurations (side-by-side and chessboard) in 2D, maintaining high plane crystallographic symmetry to simplify band structure calculations. The approach is also applied to 1D spring-mass chains to explain how band-gaps emerge.

Result: Specific configurations and unit cell choices yield multiple directional and/or omnidirectional band-gaps, some exhibiting enhanced attenuation. The results suggest that any band-gap metamaterial could have a more efficient version constructed purely from its own unit cells.

Conclusion: Dual-unit-cell coupling is a viable strategy to enhance wave attenuation in mechanical metamaterials, and the approach may generalize to any band-gap metamaterial for producing more efficient versions.

Abstract: This study investigates how the coupling of two unit cells belonging to the same mechanical metamaterial into a dual unit cell configuration, can produce a new metamaterial with enhanced wave attenuation capabilities. For two metamaterials, two different unit cell coupling configurations are examined in 2D (side by side and chessboard), with particular emphasis on maintaining a plane crystallographic group of high symmetry, in order to simplify band structure calculations given the complexity of the geometry. It is shown that for specific configurations and choices of unit cell, multiple directional and/or omnidirectional band-gaps can appear, some of which can exhibit enhanced attenuation. The way in which these band-gaps emerge is described through applying the same procedure on 1D spring mass chains. Results support the idea that any band-gap metamaterial could have a much more efficient version which can be constructed purely from its own unit cells.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.str-el'></div>

# cond-mat.str-el [[Back]](#toc)

### [131] [A d-Electron Route to Heavy-Fermion-Like Superconductivity via Geometrical Frustration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09924)
*Chaoguo Wang,Hyeonhu Bae,Jiaqi Tian,Qing-Ping Ding,Yongbin Lee,Yuji Furukawa,Shannon Gould,Brianna R. Billingsley,Si Athena Chen,Matthias Frontzek,Tai Kong,Binghai Yan,Sheng Ran,Xin Gui*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Mo4PtGa17, a noncentrosymmetric d-electron superconductor with a breathing-pyrochlore Mo lattice, exhibits heavy-fermion-like superconductivity and enhanced ferromagnetic spin fluctuations near a ferromagnetic instability, driven by geometrical frustration rather than conventional f-electron Kondo physics or Hund-metal mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore a new route to heavy-fermion-like superconductivity in d-electron materials beyond conventional f-electron Kondo lattices, Hund metals, or charge-density-wave mechanisms.

Method: Combined thermodynamic, transport, and NMR measurements on Mo4PtGa17 single crystals, supported by theoretical electronic structure calculations.

Result: Mo4PtGa17 shows heavy-fermion-like behavior, enhanced ferromagnetic spin fluctuations near a ferromagnetic instability, and nearly flat bands, van Hove singularities, and Kramers nodal lines near the Fermi energy derived from Mo-4d states.

Conclusion: Geometrical frustration in the breathing-pyrochlore lattice provides a unique mechanism for heavy-fermion-like superconductivity in d-electron systems, distinct from conventional f-electron routes.

Abstract: Heavy-fermion superconductors are mostly associated with f-electron materials with Kondo lattices, while known d-electron heavy-fermion-like systems are often linked to orbital-selective local moments, Hund-metal physics, or a charge-density-wave mechanism. Here we report Mo4PtGa17, a noncentrosymmetric itinerant d-electron superconductor with a geometrically frustrated breathing-pyrochlore Mo lattice. Thermodynamic, transport and NMR measurements reveal heavy-fermion-like behavior superconductivity and enhanced ferromagnetic spin fluctuations near a ferromagnetic instability. Theoretical calculations identify nearly flat bands, van Hove singularities and Kramers nodal lines near the Fermi energy, derived intrinsically from Mo-4d states and are robust against on-site electronic correlations. These results suggest that the geometrically frustrated lattice in Mo4PtGa17 generates an intriguing electronic structure that enhances the density of states, spin susceptibility and quasiparticle mass. Mo4PtGa17 therefore identifies a unique route to heavy-fermion-like superconductivity in d-electron materials through geometrical frustration. It also allows further investigations into superconductivity emerging from a correlated and spin-fluctuating state.

</details>


### [132] [Anyons and Inherently Complex F-symbols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10181)
*Matthew Buican,Peter Huston,Jiannis K. Pachos*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper shows that while F-symbols in many common anyon models (e.g., Abelian, Fibonacci, Ising) can be made real-valued, there exist braided fusion categories whose F-symbols are inherently complex—cannot be made real. The authors identify Rep(Z₇⋊Z₃) and Rep(Z₅⋊Z₄) as the smallest-rank examples, linking this phenomenon to the absence of charge-conjugation symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether F-symbols (fusion associativity data) in anyon models can always be made real-valued, and to understand the arithmetic properties of topological orders beyond modular data.

Method: Exhibit braided fusion categories (specifically Rep(Z₇⋊Z₃) and Rep(Z₅⋊Z₄)) whose F-symbols are inherently complex; analyze their rank and symmetry properties, particularly the absence of charge-conjugation symmetry.

Result: F-symbols in some braided fusion categories cannot be made real-valued; the smallest-rank examples found are Rep(Z₇⋊Z₃) and Rep(Z₅⋊Z₄), and their corresponding Drinfeld centers also exhibit inherently complex F-symbols.

Conclusion: Not all anyon models admit real F-symbols; inherently complex F-symbols are linked to the lack of charge-conjugation symmetry, consistent with a companion work's converse statement. These findings connect to recent efforts in classifying anyons beyond modular data.

Abstract: Anyons in $2+1$ dimensions are not only characterized by exotic braiding statistics but also by intricate fusion properties. Two anyons may fuse into multiple topological charge sectors, and associativity of fusing three anyons to produce a fixed charge sector is governed by $F$-symbols. While braiding invariants, such as the modular data, are typically complex valued, a complete description of general anyon models requires understanding the arithmetic properties of its fusion associativity data as well. The $F$-symbols for many of the most common $2+1$d topological orders, including all Abelian anyon models as well as Fibonacci and Ising anyons, can be made real valued. We show this phenomenon is not universal by exhibiting braided fusion categories whose $F$-symbols cannot be made real. We call such $F$-symbols \lq\lq inherently complex." The examples we study lack a charge-conjugation symmetry and our results are therefore consistent with the converse of a statement proved in a companion work linking real $F$-symbols in braided fusion categories with the existence of a suitable charge-conjugation symmetry. We analyse the smallest-rank braided fusion categories we know of with inherently complex $F$-symbols: ${\rm Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_7\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_3)$ and ${\rm Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_5\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_4)$. Consequently, the corresponding $\mathcal Z({\rm Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_7\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_3))$ and $\mathcal Z({\rm Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_5\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_4))$ anyon models also have inherently complex $F$-symbols. Our presentation connects these examples with recent results on classifying anyons beyond modular data.

</details>


### [133] [Pinwheel-shaped bound triplet pairs in the magnetization plateaus of SrCu$_2$(BO$_3$)$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10877)
*Igor Vinograd,Philippe Corboz,Steffen Krämer,Yanan Li,Frédéric Mila,Hiroshi Kageyama,Masashi Takigawa,Mladen Horvatić*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: NMR experiments confirm that the 1/8 and 2/15 magnetization plateaus in SrCu₂(BO₃)₂ are stabilized as Wigner crystals of spin-2 bound states, not as conventional triplet crystals, establishing a new paradigm for magnetization plateaus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The sequence of magnetization plateaus in SrCu₂(BO₃)₂ had remained unexplained until tensor-network simulations suggested they might be Wigner crystals of spin-2 bound states, a prediction that lacked experimental verification.

Method: 63,65Cu NMR spectra were measured up to 41.9 T. Using constraints from the 1/3 plateau, the spectra in the 1/8 and 2/15 plateaus were compared against predictions for spin-2 bound states versus triplet crystals.

Result: The NMR spectra in the 1/8 and 2/15 plateaus agree with the spin-2 bound state prediction and are incompatible with a triplet crystal interpretation.

Conclusion: Wigner crystals of bound states constitute a new fundamental paradigm for understanding magnetization plateaus in quantum magnets.

Abstract: The sequence of magnetization plateaus at 1/8, 2/15, 1/6, 1/4, 1/3, 2/5, and 1/2 of the saturation observed in SrCu$_2$(BO$_3$)$_2$ remained a puzzle until tensor-networks-based numerical simulations suggested that the low-magnetization plateaus are stabilized as Wigner crystals of spin-2 bound states, and not as one of the standard configurations (semiclassical up-down, or crystals of triplets). We report on $^{63,65}$Cu nuclear magnetic resonance spectra up to 41.9 T: Based on constraints deduced from the 1/3 plateau, we show that the spectra in the 1/8 and 2/15 plateaus indeed agree with the prediction for the spin-2 bound states, while being incompatible with a crystal of triplets. This adds the formation of Wigner crystals of bound states as an alternative fundamental paradigm in the theory of magnetization plateaus.

</details>


### [134] [Generalized Keldysh formalism for nonequilibrium correlation functions and its application to fluctuation dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11055)
*Ken Inayoshi,Hiroshi Shinaoka,Yuta Murakami*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper introduces a method using a contour-dependent virtual probe field within generalized Keldysh formalism to compute nonequilibrium two-particle correlation functions with vertex corrections, employing a matrix-free Krylov solver based on quantics tensor trains. Combined with nonequilibrium DMFT, it reveals that fluctuation dynamics in symmetry-broken states depend significantly on vertex corrections, with fluctuations and decay times growing near nonthermal critical points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing nonequilibrium two-particle correlation functions in correlated quantum materials remains a major challenge despite advances in time-resolved spectroscopies providing access to collective dynamics.

Method: Introduces a contour-dependent virtual probe field within the generalized Keldysh formalism, formulates a linear integral equation avoiding explicit construction of the four-time vertex kernel, and develops a matrix-free Krylov solver based on quantics tensor trains, combined with nonequilibrium dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT).

Result: The fluctuation dynamics of the order parameter in a nonequilibrium symmetry-broken state depends significantly on whether vertex corrections are included; fluctuation amplitude and decay time grow near the nonthermal critical point.

Conclusion: The approach provides a practical route for evaluating nonequilibrium correlation functions, which are key observables for characterizing states far from equilibrium.

Abstract: Recent advances in time-resolved spectroscopies provide increasing access to collective dynamics in correlated quantum materials. However, computing the corresponding nonequilibrium two-particle correlation functions remains a major challenge. Here, by introducing a contour-dependent virtual probe field within the generalized Keldysh formalism, we propose an approach that computes such correlation functions with the vertex corrections essential for describing collective dynamics. In particular, we introduce a linear integral equation that computes the correlation functions without explicitly constructing the four-time vertex kernel, and develop its matrix-free Krylov solver based on quantics tensor trains. Combining our method with nonequilibrium dynamical mean-field theory, we show that the fluctuation dynamics of the order parameter in a nonequilibrium symmetry-broken state depends significantly on whether vertex corrections are included, and that the fluctuation and its decay time grow near the nonthermal critical point. Our approach thus provides a practical route for evaluating nonequilibrium correlation functions, which are emerging as key observables for characterizing states far from equilibrium.

</details>


### [135] [Conformal Nature of Quantum Phase Transitions via Fuzzy Three-Sphere Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11371)
*Xue Meng,Liangdong Hu,Wei Zhu*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper generalizes the fuzzy-sphere approach to (3+1)-D quantum critical points, using Landau level projection on the fuzzy three-sphere to study Ising and Yang-Lee universality classes, and explicitly verifies the state-operator correspondence as evidence of emergent conformal symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directly accessing conformal algebra and microscopically revealing emergent conformal symmetry, especially in higher dimensions, remains a significant challenge. The authors aim to extend the fuzzy two-sphere approach to higher dimensions to expose conformality at (3+1)-D quantum critical points.

Method: The framework generalizes the fuzzy-sphere regularization approach to the fuzzy three-sphere. Quantum phase transitions in the Ising and Yang-Lee universality classes are investigated in a (3+1)-D quantum model (equivalent to a classical 4D system) via Landau level projection on the fuzzy three-sphere.

Result: By computing energy spectra at criticality, the authors explicitly verify the state-operator correspondence, which is a hallmark of conformal invariance.

Conclusion: Together with prior advances, this work establishes a new pathway for the microscopic study of emergent conformality in higher-dimensional phase transitions.

Abstract: Conformal field theory (CFT) offers a modern viewpoint for understanding phase transitions. However, directly accessing the conformal algebra and microscopically uncovering the emergent conformal symmetry, especially in higher dimensions, remains a significant challenge. Motivated by recent advances in revealing CFT features via the fuzzy two-sphere, here we generalize this approach to higher dimensions and aim to expose the conformality at the (3+1)-D quantum critical point. We demonstrate this framework by investigating quantum phase transitions belonging to the Ising and Yang-Lee universality classes in a (3+1)-D quantum model (equivalent to a classical four-dimensional system), realized via Landau level projection on the fuzzy three-sphere. By computing the energy spectra at criticality, we explicitly verify the state-operator correspondence, a hallmark of conformal invariance. Together with prior advances, this work establishes a new pathway for the microscopic study of emergent conformality in higher-dimensional phase transitions.

</details>


### [136] [Fractionalized metals from doped anyons: Application to tMoTe2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11484)
*T. Senthil*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper proposes that the high-resistivity metal observed adjacent to the σ_xy = 2e^2/3h FQAH state in twisted MoTe₂ is a Z₃ Orthogonal Metal — a non-Fermi liquid with charge-1/3 fermionic quasiparticles coupled to a discrete Z₃ gauge field, lacking sharp electronic quasiparticles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain the unusual high-resistivity metallic phase observed when doping the 2/3 FQAH state in twisted MoTe₂, motivated by recent experiments.

Method: Constructs two U(3) symmetric Z₃ Orthogonal Metal models for dilute gases of three species of charge-1/3 anyons with π/3 statistics, distinguished by SU(3) triplet vs. singlet fermion transformations.

Result: The Z₃ Orthogonal Metal states yield large electrical resistivities even when fractionalized quasiparticles are in a good metallic regime. Pairing of charge-1/3 fermions produces an ordinary charge-2e superconductor smoothly connected to a BCS state.

Conclusion: The normal metallic state in the lightly doped 2/3 FQAH state likely has fractionalized charge carriers, with experimentally testable signatures discussed.

Abstract: A fluid of mobile anyons may arise naturally when a Fractional Quantum Anomalous Hall (FQAH) state is doped. Motivated by recent experiments on twisted $MoTe_2$, we study metallic phases obtained by doping the $σ_{xy} = 2e^2/3h$ state. We propose that the high-resistivity metal observed adjacent to this FQAH state is a $Z_3$ Orthogonal Metal: a non-Fermi liquid with sharp charge $1/3$ fermionic quasiparticles coupled to a discrete $Z_3$ gauge field but with no sharp electronic quasiparticle. For a dilute gas of three species of charge-$1/3$ anyons with $π/3$ statistics, we construct two $U(3)$ symmetric $Z_3$ Orthogonal Metals, distinguished by whether the gapless fermions transform as $SU(3)$ triplets or singlets. We show that these states naturally yield large electrical resistivities even when the fractionalized quasiparticles are in a good metallic regime. Pairing of the charge-$1/3$ fermions produces an ordinary charge-$2e$ superconductor, smoothly connected to a BCS state but obtained through an intrinsically fractionalized normal state. We discuss experimental signatures of the idea that the normal metallic state in the lightly doped $2/3$ state may have fractionalized charge carriers.

</details>


### [137] [Stripe-Ordered Altermagnetism Emerging from Correlation-Driven Spin-Density-Wave Instability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11532)
*Zenghui Fan,Jingyao Meng,Tianxing Ma*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper reveals a novel dxy-wave stripe-ordered altermagnetic (SOAM) insulating phase driven by a correlation-induced (π,0) spin-density wave (SDW) instability in a Hubbard model relevant to iron pnictides, confirmed by unbiased quantum Monte Carlo simulations. This pushes altermagnetism beyond conventional antiferromagnets into the SDW paradigm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether altermagnetism can be realized beyond conventional collinear antiferromagnets, specifically within spin-density wave (SDW) instabilities, which remains an open fundamental question.

Method: Combined Hartree-Fock mean-field theory and unbiased determinant quantum Monte Carlo (DQMC) simulations on a minimal Hubbard model relevant to iron pnictides.

Result: Discovery of a correlation-driven dxy-wave stripe-ordered altermagnetic (SOAM) insulating phase originating from a (π,0) SDW instability. The phase exhibits nonrelativistic spin splitting under a uniaxial staggered potential via broken Td𝒯 symmetry and retained M𝒯 symmetry. Finite-size scaling from DQMC confirms its stability at finite temperatures.

Conclusion: Altermagnetism extends beyond conventional antiferromagnetic ordering into correlation-driven SDW instabilities, advancing the fundamental understanding of altermagnetism in strongly correlated electron systems.

Abstract: Altermagnetism is conventionally identified within the paradigm of collinear antiferromagnets. Its potential realization within other spin instabilities, such as a spin-density wave (SDW), remains a fundamentally compelling open question. Here, we combine Hartree-Fock mean-field and unbiased determinant quantum Monte Carlo methods to investigate a minimal Hubbard model relevant to iron pnictides. We reveal a novel $d_{xy}$-wave stripe-ordered altermagnetic (SOAM) insulating phase driven fundamentally by the correlation-induced $(π,0)$ SDW instability. Within this phase, an introduced uniaxial staggered electric potential alters the underlying symmetry: it breaks the original combined time-reversal and spatial translation symmetry ($T_{d}\mathcal{T}$) and retains a combined time-reversal and mirror invariance ($M\mathcal{T}$), thereby unlocking the pronounced nonrelativistic spin splitting. Crucially, the exact finite-size scaling from our determinant quantum Monte Carlo simulations confirms that this correlation-driven SOAM phase stably survives at accessible finite temperatures. Our study pushes the frontier of altermagnetism beyond the conventional antiferromagnetic paradigm into the realm of SDW instability, advancing the fundamental understanding of altermagnetism in strongly correlated electron systems.

</details>


### [138] [Electronic tuning of the soft-phonon transport anomaly in Ta$_2$Ni(S$_x$Se$_{1-x}$)$_5$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11679)
*Yuan-Shan Zhang,Masahiko Isobe,Hidenori Takagi,Dennis Huang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper investigates the phase transition in Ta₂NiSe₅ at 326 K, finding that S substitution tunes the band overlap and suppresses the soft-phonon transport anomaly linked to electronic instabilities. Near the Se end, electronic instabilities enhance Tc and amplify transition signatures, while near the S end, only a weak structural transition remains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify the microscopic origin and significance of the giant anisotropic scattering of phonons at Tc in Ta₂NiSe₅, particularly the connection between the soft-phonon transport anomaly and underlying electronic instabilities.

Method: Tuning the normal-state band overlap/gap via S substitution in Ta₂Ni(SₓSe₁₋ₓ)₅ and measuring the thermal transport and soft-phonon anomaly as a function of composition x.

Result: When S substitution approaches a band insulator (high x), signatures of the electronic transition and the soft-phonon transport anomaly are both suppressed. Near the Se end, electronic instabilities boost Tc to 326 K and amplify experimental signatures.

Conclusion: In the Ta₂Ni(SₓSe₁₋ₓ)₅ family, two regimes exist: a weak structural transition from lattice instability alone near the S end (Tc ~130 K), and a strong transition near the Se end where electronic instabilities (excitonic and hybridization-gap fluctuations) interact with the lattice, producing the soft-phonon transport anomaly and boosting Tc to 326 K.

Abstract: Ta$_2$NiSe$_5$ continues to be investigated for its phase transition at $T_\textrm{c}$ = 326 K, where it develops both an electronic gap and a distortion of its Ta/Ni chains. One intriguing feature at $T_\textrm{c}$ seen in thermal transport is the giant anisotropic scattering of phonons moving perpendicular to the chains, which is apparently associated with the softening of a transverse acoustic phonon, but whose microscopic origin and significance demand clarification. By tuning the normal-state band overlap/gap with S substitution, we uncover a close connection between this soft-phonon transport anomaly and underlying electronic instabilities: When Ta$_2$Ni(S$_x$Se$_{1-x}$)$_5$ approaches a band insulator at high $x$, and signatures of the electronic transition are suppressed, the soft-phonon transport anomaly concomitantly vanishes. Our results establish the following picture for the Ta$_2$Ni(S$_x$Se$_{1-x}$)$_5$ family: Near the S end, a sole lattice instability gives rise to a weak structural transition with $T_\textrm{c}$ approaching 130 K. Near the Se end, additional electronic instabilities boost $T_\textrm{c}$ up to 326 K and amplify experimental signatures of the transition. The strong interaction between electrons, holes, and the lattice is manifested as a soft-phonon transport anomaly accompanied by electronic fluctuations, which include excitonic and hybridization-gap fluctuations.

</details>
